FOREWORD

CONTRIBUTIONS TO THE SETTLEMENT HISTORY OF HUNGARIAN JEWRY
IN THE FIRST HALF OF THE 18TH CENTURY

The settlement history of the Jews of Hungary is still waiting to be written. The present publication provides source material for carrying out this work. The earlier volumes of our Oklevéltár [charter collection] do contain numerous data on the immigration of the Jews into the Hungarian homeland, their settlement here and the formation of their communities; however, it is in the material published here that we receive, for the first time, official data on their enumeration extending to the whole country and carried out county by county. With this we become acquainted with the largest and most enduring — by now the fourth — settlement of Jewry in the country encircled by the Carpathians.

Surveying the entire process of the settlement history of Jewry on Hungarian soil, starting from the earliest times, we can distinguish four periods*. In Roman Pannonia, along the Danubian limes, in the 3rd and 4th centuries, the surviving inscribed and symbolic monuments point to the presence of Syrian and Asia Minor Jews. These settlements, small in number, were unable to preserve their continuity; they disappear in the centuries of the Migration Period. Only recently discovered inscribed stone monuments proclaim their former existence.

In the Hungarian Middle Ages, from the 12th century, we can follow the traces of renewed Jewish immigration; royal protection made it possible for those who had immigrated from southern Germany to settle in the emerging towns. The expulsion decree of King Louis the Great [Nagy Lajos], stemming from religious intolerance, represents only a brief interruption of a few years in the medieval settlement history of the Jews of Hungary. The disaster of Mohács and the turmoil and internal wars that followed it led to the rapid destruction of the urban Jewish congregations that had by then existed for centuries (Buda, Esztergom, Székesfehérvár, Sopron, Pozsony, Nagyszombat.).

The third is the period of immigration into the territory of Hungary under Turkish rule and of the Principality of Transylvania. The victories of the war of liberation (1683-169D [OCR: final digit garbled]) and the fall of the independent Transylvanian principality close this era.

* Works dealing with the settlement history of the Jews of Hungary, in the order of the settlements:
Scheiber Sándor: Magyarországi zsidó feliratok [Jewish Inscriptions in Hungary]. Budapest, 1960.
Kohn Sámuel: A zsidók története Magyarországon [The History of the Jews in Hungary]. I. Budapest, 1884. Appendix XI: The population of the Jewish communities of Hungary and of Hungarian Jewry before the disaster of Mohács.
Büchler Sándor: Zsidó letelepedések Magyarországon a mohácsi vész után [Jewish Settlements in Hungary after the Disaster of Mohács]. Magyar Zsitló [OCR; read as Zsidó] Szemle X. 1893. 315-329, 370-387.
Acsády Ignác: A magyar zsidók 1735-58-ban [The Hungarian Jews in 1735-58]. Izr. Magyar Irodalmi Társulat Évkönyve [Yearbook of the Israelite Hungarian Literary Society]. 1897. 173-188.
Marton. Ernő [OCR punctuation]: A magyar zsidóság családfája [The Family Tree of Hungarian Jewry]. Kolozsvár, 1941.
Grisnuald. Fülöp [OCR; name garbled]: A zsidó települések múltja magyar földön [The Past of Jewish Settlements on Hungarian Soil]. Offprint from the 1942 Yearbook of Ararát.

Source scan
The fourth immigration process began when, after the lengthy Turkish wars, the century-long national struggles and then the devastating epidemics, the country passed into the possession of the Habsburgs largely depopulated. A veritable new conquest [honfoglalás] became necessary to repopulate the country and develop it economically. In this a task awaited the relatively small Jewish population as well. The mercantilist ideas of the age were by then already spreading, and the examples of certain Western states had their effect, when the aristocratic great landowners gave Jews the opportunity to settle and to take part in economic life. The aristocratic leaders of the country did not follow the reconstruction plan worked out under the influence of Archbishop Kollonich (Einrichtungs Werkh des Königreiche Hungaren, H(89) [OCR: year garbled]), in which he recommends that the Jews living in fairly considerable numbers in Hungary be gradually removed from the country and that where they are not yet present — thus especially in the newly acquired territories — they should not be admitted at all.

As is well known, the great settlement campaigns after the wars of liberation ran in two directions. On the one hand, a movement began from the more densely populated border regions of the country, where the raging wars had not reduced the serf population to such a great extent, toward the interior of the country. Here the great landowners engaged in settlement offered favourable living conditions to the immigrants. On the other hand, settlers were invited from neighbouring countries to the abandoned territories.

We can observe the same duality in the course of the settlement history of the Jews of Hungary in this period. Here too we find that their movement into the counties of the interior begins from the western, north-western and then north-eastern border regions, which were by then more densely inhabited by Jews; on the other hand, at the same time we know of smaller and larger seigneurial settlement schemes, primarily from neighbouring Moravia and Bohemia, as well as of the settling of individual families from Polish territory into the villages of the north-eastern border region.

It could be the subject of a separate study to show how it became possible that the first official censuses, already in the 1720s, record a relatively large Jewish population in the border counties of Transdanubia and the North-western Uplands.

1. The censuses of the western border counties of Transdanubia [heading OCR garbled: 'A ])unántú'zlIyugati haiárezéli vál'megyéinelc GlJ8zf:rásai'; tentative reading]

The oldest Jewish censuses of the Transdanubian border counties, dating from the 1720s, list 732 families in the communities of Vas, Sopron and Moson: in Vas county 179 heads of family (1725), in Sopron county 388 heads of family (1725), and in Moson county in 1727 Hi5 [OCR: number garbled] heads of family.

Characteristic from the point of view of population density is that in Vas county, where Jews lived in 5 communities, there are 152 families in the market town of Rohonc, 15 in Szalónak, 7 in Németújvár, :1 [OCR: number garbled] in Muraszombat, and 2 in Szentgotthárd.

In Sopron county: 90 in Lakompak, 95 in Kismarton, 83 in Nagymarton, 47 in Németkeresztúr, Bg [OCR: number garbled] in Kabold, 30 in Lajtaújfalu (Neufeld).

In Moson: 53 in the community of Boldogasszony, 42 in Köpcsény, 43 in Oroszvár. The large number of Jews in these communities is all the more surprising if we compare it with the official census carried out in 1715 and 1720, which recorded the tax-paying population of the localities without the Jews. Thus in Rohonc, alongside the 152 Jewish families, the tax-paying population of the market town was 210.

In the six populous Jewish communities of Sopron county, alongside the 384 Jewish families, there were 426 tax-paying non-Jewish households.
In Moson county, in the three communities with 138 Jewish families, there are 148 non-Jewish tax-paying families.
From the standpoint of settlement history, the question naturally arises: from where and when did this relatively large Jewish population move into the country? The assumption cannot be accepted that a “sizeable” Jewry already lived here in Turkish times, as a remnant of the descendants of those driven out of the medieval towns. We insert here the oldest Kismarton Jewish census known so far, from 1564, although this Jewish congregation can boast the greatest continuity of Jewish population. Its census, made to determine tax arrears, found only eight settled families with :H [OCR: number illegible] persons, to which were added six foreign, solitary, wandering Jewish persons. Of the eight permanent residents, two were born in Kismarton, three in Sopron, two in Kőszeg, and one was of Moravian origin. (M. Zs. O. II. 32 sz.] We find a Jewish population of similar composition in the second half of the 16th century in Nagymarton, Kabold and Lakompak. These are scant remnants of the medieval Jewry of Sopron, Kőszeg and Kismarton. With reference to these, the legislature (1578) could truly write: “Judaei ... , qui in Hungaria paucissimi sunt, quo citius cmigrent; ad solvendum dicam ct alia onera subeunda, in duplo cogantur” [Latin: “The Jews ..., who are very few in Hungary, shall be compelled to pay the tax and bear other burdens double, so that they may emigrate the sooner”]. The law thus even urges the emigration of these few by means of double taxation. Yet these insignificant congregations formed the seeds of the more populous Jewish communities that we encounter here in the first half of the 18th century. In investigating their origins, we may think primarily of immigration from Lower Austria, where, besides the Jewish quarter that developed in the city of Vienna, a whole series of rural Jewish settlements arose in the course of the 17th century*. These were admittedly not yet very populous; even the largest numbered no more than 25–45 families. Similar to these were the Sopron county communities of this period, which are mentioned in the documents (Kismarton 1623, Nagymarton 1(39) [OCR; probably 1639]). But we already find organized congregations headed by Jewish judges (Kismarton 1622, Nagymarton 1639). Moreover, these communities had been attached to Austria since 1491, on the basis of the treaty concluded between Ulászló II and Emperor Frederick III, and returned to Hungary by law only in ]647 [OCR; probably 1647]. These Jewish communities, however, grew greatly when Leopold I, out of religious bias, expelled all Jews from Vienna and Lower Austria in 1670–71. Numerous data support the fact that those banished from Austria partly found refuge in Hungary, although the imperial decree sought to keep them away from there too (D. Kau/mann [OCR; = Kaufmann]: Die letzte Vertreibung der Juden aus Wien und Niederösterreich. Budapest, ]889 [OCR; = 1889]. 190.). The general assembly of the town of Sopron complains in lü75 [OCR; probably 1675]: “Dass judcn-gesindel... in der nachbahrschafft sich hauffig zu vernichren anfangt..” [German: “that Jewish rabble ... is beginning to multiply abundantly in the neighbourhood”; “vernichren” is presumably an OCR error for “vermehren”]. Then in lü80 [OCR; probably 1680] the assembly states in its declaration: “Es geschicht von den juden ein grosser überlauff. Es sind ihrer (j gemeine ... ” [German: “There is a great influx of Jews. There are (j [OCR; numeral unclear] communities of them ...”]. In Kémet Kereszturon [OCR; probably Német Keresztúr], lying close to the town of Sopron, four Jews lived before 1672; in September of that year — as was reported to the Chamber — a further 24 families arrived there, and Mr Kövér Gabriel kept them. (M. Zs. O. Il. 136. sz.)

* L. Moses: Die Juden in Niederösterreich Mit besonderer Berücksichtigung des XVII. Jahrhunderts. Wien, 1935.

Source scan
The immigration of Jews naturally always depended on the authorities or landlords receiving them, who permitted their residence there and their permanent settlement, and who could provide the newcomers with the necessary protection and security. This must always be taken into account when describing the settlement history of Jewry.
The Jewry expelled at the end of the Middle Ages from the towns of the western border strip was given temporary refuge by the lords of the neighbouring castle estates (Baron Weiszpriach, lord of the castles of Kismarton and Nagymarton; likewise Ecchius von Salm, castellan of Pozsony; Jurisich Miklós, commander of the castle of Kőszeg, who, however, in 1540 yielded to the request of the town burghers and expelled the Jews from there, although they had honourably held their ground in the defence of Kőszeg castle in 1532.)
The Jews, accustomed to the conditions of medieval urban life, were forced in their new places of residence to adapt to the changed circumstances of life. Many of them, who showed no inclination to do so, sought a suitable livelihood elsewhere, far abroad. It is characteristic of those who remained in the villages that in the early period they mostly took up dealing in livestock and animal products. Greater security and wider economic opportunities came to the Jewish inhabitants of these communities when the places became centres of the estates of great landowning feudal lords (the Esterházys, Batthyánys, Zichys, Pálffys). EJI [OCR; probably “These”] lords, who held the highest dignities of the country, were able through their influence to ensure that Jewry, as a class without rights (“judaei .,. -Iurium Regni incapaces” [Latin: “Jews ... incapable of the rights of the Realm”] 1647. Decr. II. Art. 91. §. 8.), nevertheless obtained some protection. We have in mind King Leopold I’s letter of protection for the Jews of Pozsony, Moson and Vas counties (the listing of the Sopron county Jews was omitted for some reason). Upon the complaint of the Jews of the palatinal (Esterházy) and Batthyány estates, the royal letter reprimands the inhabitants of these counties and of the towns lying in their territory (Pozsony, Sopron, Kőszeg and Ruszt) for violently wronging the Jews, and takes them under protection on the grounds that: “Nos eosdom (judaeos) veluti nec dum e Regno nostro Hungariae praescriptos, sed tributaries Dominorum suorum subditos in Nostrarn Regiam, cum personis et rebus eorum universis recipimus.” [Latin: “We receive these (Jews), as not yet proscribed from Our Kingdom of Hungary but as tributary subjects of their lords, into Our Royal [protection], together with all their persons and goods.”] (6 September 1684. M. Zs. O. II. 153. sz.) This decree sounds surprising coming from the ruler who, not quite a decade and a half earlier, had not only expelled all the Jews, some four thousand persons, from Vienna and Lower Austria, but had also sought to remove them from the formerly pledged border territories, which had in the meantime returned to Hungary. This latter expulsion, however, was in force for only a few weeks (24 April 167l. [OCR; = 1671] – 20 August 1671). The great landowners of this region surely secured the annulment of the banning decree concerning the Hungarian border territories. Iaguk [OCR; probably “Maguk” = “themselves”] the magnates themselves set out the rights and duties of the inhabitants of the more populous Jewish communities in separate letters of privilege. The oldest of these letters of privilege whose text has come down to us is Count Batthyány Ádám’s letter of protection of 1687 (Schutzpunkte) for B6 [OCR; number unclear, possibly 36] Jewish families named individually, whom he settled as subjects on his Rohonc estate; two years later the estate steward of the beekói [OCR; place name uncertain] domain came to an agreement with II [OCR; possibly 11] Jewish families of Vágújhely. Palatine Esterházy Pál concluded a detailed contract in 1690 with 50 Jews of Nikolsburg, permitting them to build twenty houses on the manor farm belonging to his castle. This Kismarton contract, set out in seventeen points, served as the model for the other Jewish communities of the Esterházy estate (Köpcsény, 1692. Nagymarton, W94 [OCR; probably 1694]. Boldogasszony, 1714. Kabold, 1718. Lajtaujfalu, 1720. Németkeresztúr, 1130 [OCR; probably 1730].) Here should also be mentioned the letter of protection of Judge Royal Pálffy Mik-

8

PDF viewer page 11

Source scan
lós for the 50 Jewish families received at Pozsony Váralja (1714) and for the 40 Jewish families who settled in 1722 on his stemfai [as in source; cf. Stomfa below] estate in Pozsony county.
The Jews of these communities paid their lords a fixed annual sum in gratitude for their protection and defence (Schutzgeld), but beyond this they also strove in every possible way to make themselves useful, performing the most varied services. They sought their lords’ favour by every means, for the security of their existence depended entirely on them; this dependent social position made them servile. On commission they carried out the most varied purchases for their lords and ladies, which were needed in the Baroque era to maintain their luxurious courts. They marketed the surplus produce of the landlords, who by then had largely switched to demesne (manorial) farming. They undertook the lease of seigneurial rights, the regalia; they leased the butcher’s shop, the mill, brandy distilling and beer brewing, as well as the retail sale of drinks. Even if these did not always yield a large income, the constant connection with the lords of the estate meant a great deal to the Jewish settlers, because it ensured some protection for the newcomer, who often lived alone. They also leased the collection of seigneurial tolls and ferry dues, which was no easy task and made them unpopular among the population. Thus this often led to clashes at the designated toll stations. They became instruments of seigneurial exploitation, and although the law from 1630 onward [OCR “Ót11”] repeatedly forbade Jews from leasing the thirtieths and tolls (“.Judaei tam a tricesimarum, quam teloniorum arendatione amoveantnr” [Latin: “Jews shall be removed from leasing both the thirtieths and the tolls”], ]630 [OCR; = 1630]. Dec. Ill. Art. ]5. [OCR; = 15]), the landlords continued to keep them in their service, because ,1 [OCR artefact] by their own strength the Jews could by no means have remained at the toll stations. A royal mandate was sent in 169] [OCR; last digit unclear] to the western border counties — Pozsony, Nyitra, Vas, Sopron and Trencsén counties — concerning the removal of Jewish toll collectors. (M. Zs. O. II. UH. sz. [OCR; number illegible]) This decree, too, attests how often the western immigrants obtained such employment. The prohibitive provision was in vain; the magnates continued to keep their Jewish toll collectors in their service.
The freedom struggle of Rákóczi Ferenc was a great ordeal for the by then numerous Jewish inhabitants of the western border region, since in their places of residence they were all in the service of royalist magnates. The kuruc attacks were directed against their communities as well; generalizing, the kuruc proclaimed that the Jews all sided with the Germans. The attack on the communities of Rohonc and Kismarton alarmed the entire Jewry of the border region; they abandoned their places of residence and, as a contemporary Hebrew source writes: “All the communities of Hungary are in exile” (B. Wachstein: Urkunden und Akten zur Geschichte der Juden in Eisenstadt und den Siebengemeinden. Wien, 192ö [OCR; year unclear]. U. [OCR; page number unclear]). They found temporary refuge in Fraknóvár, Bécsújhely, Bruck on the Lajta and Vienna, while the Jews living in Pozsony and the surrounding communities availed themselves of the protection of Pálffy Miklós at Pozsony Váralja. The census of the Jews who found refuge there has survived from the year 1708. (Published from the Pálffy archive in M. Zs. O. II. :1n. sz. [OCR; number illegible]) Before the national Jewish censuses published in this volume of our Oklevéltár, this Pozsony list contains the most detailed numerical data on the places where Hungarian Jews were settled at that time. It names the :iH [OCR; number illegible] Jewish families already living at Pozsony Váralja at the time. It lists 35 refugees from Stomfa, which was likewise a Pálffy estate. From Köpcsény in Moson county, near Pozsony, which was the property of Palatine Esterházy, the whole community of 44 persons was received, and from Oroszvár, likewise in Moson county, an estate of Count Zichy, twenty-three reached Váralja, while five came from Rajka and four from Köpösd in Nyitra county.

9

PDF viewer page 12

Source scan
But in this list we also find refugees from the following places: Tapolcsány, Győr, Óvár, Szentgyörgy, Cseklész, Szerdahely, Holics, Buda, Lajtaújfalu, Kismarton, Nagymarton, Dévény, Bazin, Modor, Szered, Baja, and also persons originating from Moravia, as well as from Bohemia and Poland.
When the struggles of the Rákóczi freedom war came to an end and the devastating epidemics that followed it ceased, the Jewish communities were repopulated, the refugees returned, and indeed, in the ensuing period of peace, a larger process of immigration began from Moravia and Bohemia. Data on Jewish settlement conditions in this period are provided by the Jewish censuses carried out in the twenties of the century, which the Hungarian Royal Lieutenancy Council, newly organized at that time, ordered county by county on royal command. The royal decree lS [OCR; probably “és” = “and”] the Lieutenancy Council’s instruction inquired into the numbers, occupations and tax conditions of Hungarian Jewry because it wished to prevent their renewed mass immigration and indeed wished to reduce the number of those who had been resident for some time. The counties submitted the data in varying ways. In each county the censuses were carried out by the district magistrates (szolgabírák) and jurors, who after completing their work presented their reports to the county general assembly, which sent the material collected for the county to the Lieutenancy Council. The Jews, who lived on the estates of the lords of the noble counties and stood in their service, brought them profit — this explains why the nobility did not wish or demand their removal, and indeed made the arrival of new ones possible.
The principal numerical data of the western border counties of Transdanubia:
According to the 1725 census of Vas county, not counting the free royal town of Kőszeg, Jews lived in five communities, 179 families in all. Its most populous Jewish congregation was Rohonc, where twenty-seven houses were Jewish property and :32 [OCR; probably 32] belonged to the lord; together ]52 [OCR; probably 152] families lived in these houses; in Szalonak ]5 [OCR; probably 15], in Német Újvár 7, in Mura Szombat :l [OCR; digit unclear] and in Szent Gotthárd 2.
According to the 1n5 [OCR; year unclear, possibly 1735] census, 130 families lived in l7 [OCR; probably 17] communities. The number of families was 80 in Rohonc, 13 in Szalonak, 9 in Német Újvár, 7 in Körmend, 3 in Szombathely, 3 in Nagy Csömöte, 2 in Mura Szombat, likewise 2 in Sárvár, and in eight communities one family each lived.
According to the ]746 [OCR; probably 1746] census, 227 families lived in 53 communities: Rohonc 106, Német Újvár 24, Szalonak l7 [OCR; probably 17], Körmend 9. In thirty-six communities only one family each, in seven 2, in three 3, in another three 4.
Census of the Jews of Sopron county from the year 1725:
398 families were recorded in nine communities. While only one family each settled in Kapuvár and in the community of Páli, and two in Kisfalud, the county had the six populous Jewish communities already mentioned above.
The conscription contained in the 1735 table lists 429 families in seven communities. The number of souls in these was 194!J [OCR; last digit unclear]. Only one family lives in Füles, while the data of the six populous congregations at that time are as follows: Kismarton Váralja 112 families, Lakompak 103, Nagymarton 84, Német Keresztúr 54, Kabold 45, Lajta Újfalu 30.
The census from the forties has not survived; we find only, in the summary table of the extraordinary tax, that this tax was levied in 174G [OCR; last digit unclear, possibly 1746] on 1313 persons. This would correspond to 292 families if we reckon 4.5 persons per family. This large decrease can be explained by the fact that poor families were not counted, despite the royal decree; the census-takers proceeded in this way in other counties of the country as well.

The 1725 census of Moson county has survived only incompletely; the communities of the district beyond the Lajta are missing. The Jewish population of only six communities was recorded. The three populous communities appear as follows: Oroszvár with 43, Köpcsény with :38 [OCR; probably 38], Rajka with 15 families. In the community of Csun and in the market town of Óvár one Jewish family each, in the market town of Moson 2; altogether 100 families live in the county.
Two censuses survive from the year 1727; the county submitted one to the Lieutenancy Council on the eighth of February and the other on the second of September, because it was not satisfied with the first. The data of the first submission are as follows: it lists Boldogasszony 40, Köpcsény 29, Oroszvár 2G [OCR; probably 26], Rajlea [OCR; = Rajka] 12, Gátha G [OCR; digit unclear, possibly 6] Jewish heads of family. In Csun and Óvár one family each; Moson and Nynlas [OCR; place name uncertain] are each the residence of two families. According to this census, altogether 1] 9 [OCR; probably 119] families live in the nine communities.
The conscription submitted in September of the same year recorded 165 families in the nine communities: Boldogasszony 5:3 [OCR; probably 53], Oroszvár 4:~ [OCR; second digit unclear], Köpcsény 42, Rajka 14, Gátha 9, Csun two, Óvár and Moson one each.
In 1n5 [OCR; year unclear, possibly 1735] the census-takers found 194 families in fl [OCR; number unclear] communities of the county: Köpcsény appears with GI [OCR; number unclear], Oroszvár with 54, Boldogasszony with :39 [OCR; probably 39], Rajka with 2G [OCR; probably 26] families, while in five communities fewer than 10 families live: Bezenye, Lébény, Moson one family each, Csun :3 [OCR; probably 3], Gátha 8.
The census from 1745 contains only the data of the five populous communities; these five communities are: Boldogasszony 40, Oroszvár :39 [OCR; probably 39], Köpcsény :3G [OCR; second digit unclear], Rajka 16, Gátha 12, altogether 143 families. The extraordinary tax was levied in this county in 174G-biLl1589 [OCR garbled; possibly “1746-ban 589” = “in 1746 on 589”, but year and figure are uncertain] persons. If we again reckon 4.5 persons per family, then 129 could have lived there.
The varying figures for the Jewish population of the communities can be traced back to the fact that the censuses were carried out in different ways in the individual years, even though precisely in these three counties, where the Jews obtained settlement on the estates of the Batthyánys and Esterházys, the Jewish population was counted from time to time on the estates.
Batthyány Ádámné [Mrs Ádám Batthyány], proprietress of Rohonc, gives the following instruction in 1720: “Alle halbe jahr die Juden beschrieben, die gaaben eingetrieben und die nicht eingeschrieben, in Arest geführt, in Eysen und Pand geschlagen, der Juden Richter abel' fül' ein jeden dergl: p. 32 fl. bestrafft werden.” [German: “Every half year the Jews are to be recorded and the dues collected, and those not recorded are to be taken into arrest and put in irons and bonds, while the Jews’ judge is to be fined p. 32 fl. for each such person.”] (M. Zs. O. Ill. 169. sz.) Accordingly, she made the census the duty of the Jewish judge. If not every half year, then annually the leaders of the Jewish community were obliged to submit a census of their members by name. The census made on 28 May 1727 has survived, which shows the names of the heads of family of the Jewish community in German and Hebrew. The list gives 175 names in all. (Rechnitzer Juden Lista oder Verzciohnusz, de dato 28 May 1727. ::VI. Zs. O. Ill. 1087. sz.) At the end of the list we can read that the list submitted by the judge is identical with last year’s, except that those who had died in the meantime were omitted and the newly married were already included in the enumeration. Those, however, who have nothing are submitted to the Trivatal [OCR; word uncertain, apparently an estate office] in a separate list.
The officials of the estates prepared tabular statements on the Esterházys’ Jewish subjects living in the communities of Sopron and Moson counties. (The compilation from the year 1729 is published in M. Zs. O. V/2. 1094. sz. a.) In 1738 the chief accountant of the Esterházy estate instructs the Jewish community of Kismarton and the other seven Jewish communities under the protection of the Esterházy family that their judges and jurors should appear before him, presenting a complete table of the Jewish population of each community. The censuses of the Jews of Nagymarton and Kabold from 1738 and 1739 respectively have indeed survived. (M. Zs. O. V/2. 1110, 1111. és 1112. sz.] The numerical discrepancy between the data of the conscription carried out by the county’s men and the lists submitted to the landlords is striking.

II

PDF viewer page 14

Source scan
Thus, for example, according to the 1735 county census Nagymarton had 83 Jewish families, while 126 names appear in the seigneurial list: 44 have their own house or part of a house, and 82 live there as tenants.
Moson county, already in a statute enacted in 1712, provides for the census of Jewry for taxation purposes. (M. Zs. O. V/2. ] 123. sz. [OCR; probably 1123])

2. The Jewry of the Border Counties of the Northwestern Upland

In this period newcomers arrive in greatest numbers from two areas: Moravia, Bohemia and Poland.
Acsády Ignác (Magyarország népessége a pragmatica sanctio korában. Bp., 1896.) showed, on the basis of the data of the 1735–38 census, that more than one third of the Jewry of Hungary at that time declared itself to be of Moravian origin (38,:35% [OCR; probably 38,35%])*. The censuses from the twenties of the 18th century published in this volume confirm this finding. At the same time, however, we must state that this large-scale immigration of Moravian Jews cannot be attributed to King Charles III’s decree of 1726, valid for Bohemia and Moravia, by which he restricted the number of Jews in these two provinces of his empire, stipulating that in each family only one marrying son should receive the right of residence (incolatus). It was then considered whether to extend the closed number to our country as well, so as to restrict Jewish families; however, taking into account the demographic and economic condition of the country, which had then recently been liberated and was still sparsely populated, the chancellery did not recommend extending this decree to Hungary — adding that its introduction would “veluti piures malas sequelas post se trahere” [Latin: “as it were, bring several bad consequences in its train”]. The lords of the country at that time — as can be established — willingly received onto their estates Jews who possessed some property, because they could profit from them economically.
Immigration from Moravia, just like that from Austria and — as we shall see — that from Polish land, demonstrably (as the documents show) began at the start of the 17th century and continued steadily in the following century too, whereas no new immigrants could come from Austrian soil from the last quarter of the 17th century, after they had been expelled from there in lü70-71 [OCR; probably 1670–71]. By contrast, new groups of immigrants continuously arrived from Moravian soil, since the Jewry there showed considerable numerical growth in this period.

* An erroneous datum in Acsády’s book (op. cit. p. 282) must be corrected. Among the data on the origins of the Jews recorded in Vas county, we find in Acsády that of the county’s 131 recorded heads of family, 14 were from Hungary, 2 from Moravia, 100 from other provinces of Austria and 15 from an unknown place. The figure of one hundred arises because the Jewish heads of family found in Rohonc, Körmend, Németújvár and other communities of the county declared as their hereditary lord the widow of Count Batthyány Ádám, Strattmann Eleonóra, whose place of residence at the time of the conscription is given as Wien, although the Jewish inhabitants of these Vas county communities had been Batthyány subjects for generations; this is proven by the letters of protection received from the Batthyánys in 1673 and 1687. These 100 Jewish heads of family must be counted as being of Hungarian origin, and thus the national proportion of Jewry of Hungarian origin is modified.
The combined number of Jewish heads of family of Hungarian origin at this time (1735) is 985 instead of 885, and their national proportion is 38,!!1% [OCR; final digits unclear] instead of 35,31%.
The number of Jewish heads of family originating from other provinces of Austria (excluding Bohemia and Moravia) is only 33 instead of 133, and their national proportion only 1,29% instead of 5,31%.

12

PDF viewer page 15

Source scan
Of the historical past of Moravian Jewry we know the following: until the middle of the 15th century, the towns of this Slav-inhabited territory, which were then taking shape, served as the main stations on the eastward route of the Jewry expelled from the imperial cities of Germany, and in them the Jews fulfilled a useful economic function. From the second half of the 15th century, however, the Jews were expelled from the royal towns of the province — with the consent of the ruler (King László V) and under the influence of the anti-Jewish agitation of Kapisztrán János [John of Capistrano]. These towns (Brünn, Olmütz, Znaim, Iglau etc.) obtained from the ruler the right not to be obliged to tolerate Jews within their walls (“de non tolerando judaeos”). Here too it happened, as in many places in the German Empire, that the Jewry pushed out of the towns was partly received by the landlords of neighbouring communities. In this way more and more rural Jewish congregations formed, whose number in time exceeded fifty. The number of Jewish inhabitants of these communities grew when, in the middle of the 17th century, from 16M~ [OCR; year unclear] onward, following the Cossack attacks, the massacres began on Ukrainian, Polish and Lithuanian soil that brought about the break-up of these densely Jewish-inhabited areas. Eastern Jewry — on its route of flight westward — sought protection and the possibility of settlement in the nearest Moravian communities. The number of inhabitants of the Moravian Jewish communities was likewise increased by the masses expelled from Vienna and Lower Austria, and indeed — as is known — Jewish captives carried off from Buda (1686) and Belgrade (Hi89 [OCR; year unclear]), recaptured from the Turks, were transported to Moravia, because the high ransom demanded for them was hoped for from the Jews there.
From a whole series of documents we learn that, from the beginning of the 17th century, Jews had a significant share in the border trade between Hungary and Moravia. The Hungarian landlords early on took into their service Moravian Jews experienced in commercial matters and gave them the opportunity to settle on their estates. As early as 1599 the Czobor estate had a Jewish toll collector. In the census of the taxable portae of Nyitra county from 1593 to lG03 [OCR; probably 1603], Jewish inhabitants of Sassin (Sasvár), Holics and Galgóc already appear several times — where in 1641 a “Sido uozá” is mentioned. (M. Zs. O. VII. 585. sz.) One can observe how the places of Jewish settlement advanced: in the early period they reached from the Hungarian–Moravian border as far as the communities lying along the line of the Vág, and from there further to the line of the Nyitra. Two characteristic features of settlement from Moravia must be pointed out: they settle in a whole series of communities in the territory of Nyitra and Trencsén counties, but, apart from a few more populous settlements, the lord can mostly receive and place only one or two families on his estate. The other characteristic feature of these settlements is that the newly settled maintain their connection with the Jewish communities of their place of origin and with those communities’ landlords. These connections persisted for decades, across generations. Both sides held to them, as their interests also required. The emigrants were bound to their place of origin by family ties and piety; the members of the ancestral community long expected those who had left to return to their midst. It was also a requirement that they contract their marriages within that community. They demanded a material contribution from those settling abroad towards the maintenance of the institutions of the mother community.
The Moravian landlords regarded their Jewish subjects as their hereditary property; these were obliged to report even a temporary absence to the lordship and in such cases to pay a certain fee. The seigneurial authority also had to be informed of their return. From those settling in Hungary they demanded for years the payment of certain

13

PDF viewer page 16

Source scan
urbarial obligations. The Moravian landlords regarded this as their rightful claim, arguing that their Jewish subjects, like their non-Jewish subjects, as “Erb und Leibeigen Unterthanen und Servi Glebae” [German/Latin: “hereditary and bonded subjects and serfs bound to the soil”], being bound to the land, were forbidden to move out of the country; and if they nevertheless did so, they continued to owe their hereditary lords an annual tax. Indeed — as we see from the 1735–1739 census, in which the table drawn up by the Lieutenancy Council also contains the question “Dominus illius haereditarius in vel extra Hungariarn est” [Latin: “Is his hereditary lord in or outside Hungary”], and furthermore the column “Annue solvit haereditario domino” [Latin: “Pays annually to the hereditary lord”]. Characteristically, in the specimen sent by the government office to the counties, the answer given to the first question was: “in Moravia comes N.” [“in Moravia, Count N.”]. Summing up the data of the 1735–39 census, those who had settled in Hungary paid their Moravian hereditary lords 4882 forints 33 denars annually, more than one fifth of what they gave their then Hungarian lords in return for urbarial service. From the conscriptions we also learn that the new immigrants quite often still left their families behind in Moravia. Moreover, the immigrants procured their wares there, especially cloth and other textile goods. In the 1736 census of Heves county we find, regarding Moyses Ábrahám, living in the market town of Gyöngyös, who declares “Comes Chaunicz in Moravia” as his hereditary lord, the following about his occupation: “Habet fornicem stabilem panno, tela, fimbriis et reliquis mercibus ex Moravia adductis.” [Latin: “He has a permanent vaulted shop with cloth, linen, fringes and other wares brought from Moravia.”] Jewish merchants from Moravia also constantly travelled to distant Hungarian regions. This is attested by a datum we read in the 1727 census of Nógrád county. One son of the Jewish family staying in the community of Szirák is “in servi ti is pro nunc apud certum Judaeum Brodensem (Ungarisch Brod) Jacobuni Aller nominatum. qui alias cum mercimoniis has partes saepe frequentare solet” [Latin: “currently in service with a certain Jew of Brod (Ungarisch Brod) named Jacob Aller, who otherwise often frequents these parts with merchandise”]. On their journeys across the country the Moravian Jewish merchants came to know the Hungarian regions, and where they found a place suitable for pursuing their occupation, they sought a connection with it, came to an agreement with the lords of the community and settled there.
The Moravian landlords took their Jewish subjects, who represented considerable income for them, under their protection as their own and regarded them as falling under their jurisdiction. This is what the Dietrichsteins did with the Jews who had emigrated from Nikolsburg, the Lichtensteins with those from Göding and Prossnitz, and the Kauniczes with those from Ungarischbrod. Kaunicz Domonkos András in particular acted on several occasions in the interest of his Jews. It is worth mentioning how he took under his patronage two of his Magyarbrod subjects whom the Bishop of Eger had innocently thrown into prison. These Jews had been trading in the town of Eger for years and, with the permission of the authorities, operated a distillery in the town, which had then recently been liberated from Turkish rule. Because they did not comply with the order to leave the community within three hours, they ended up in an underground prison. Count Kaunicz obtained royal intercession for their release. He likewise intervened on behalf of his Jewish subjects with the lords of the towns of Nagyszombat and Trencsén.
The Moravian landlords kept a separate record, besides their Christian subjects, of the Jews who had departed from their estates. Evidence of this is the surviving list: “Haubt Tabella derer Vermög eingelangter individual-Consignatione eines dem Marggrafthum Máhren, von Anno 1740 in das Königreich Hungarn entwichenen .Iuden, nebst bemerkung ihrer theils wissentlichen, theils abel' mutmaslichen aufenthalts Örthern” [German: “Main table of the Jews who, according to the individual consignations received, fled from the Margraviate of Moravia into the Kingdom of Hungary from the year 1740, with a note of their partly known, partly presumed places of residence”]. From this list we learn how many came to Hungary in the said period, from which communities they emigrated and where they settled. Even if this enumeration, which contains 339 names, is not complete — several with fami-

14

PDF viewer page 17

Source scan
[…]dostul jöttek [OCR fragment at page start; the beginning of the word is on the previous page, probably “(with their) families came”] —, nevertheless it gives a broad picture of the immigration directed from Moravia into Hungary. We see that most of them came from the populous Moravian Jewish communities lying close to the Hungarian border, especially from Ungarischbrod, this true mother-city of Hungarian Jewry (78 persons). Also noteworthy is where the immigrants headed in the greatest numbers: most went to Buda, more correctly Óbuda (18 heads of family). Among these Óbuda immigrants we find some who soon attained a leading role in their new place of residence and became the founders of large families (Seckl Hollitscher). The Hungarian Lieutenancy Council sent the said list to those counties and towns to which the immigrants had made their way. The wish of the Moravian lords was that their subjects be returned to them..

From the standpoint of settlement history, particular interest attaches to all those conscriptions in which the enumerating szolgabírák [district magistrates] and jurors considered it necessary to record also how long the individual Jewish families had been living there locally. Thus in 1727, of the no [OCR reads “no”; a number, probably 110] heads of family enumerated in Pozsonyváralja, only 34 declared themselves born in Pozsony; all the rest immigrated later and married there. New places of settlement came into being in the calmer period following the Peace of Szatmár. Along the Vág, in Szered, which was an Esterházy estate, 23 families were granted settlement. The oldest declares that he came there twenty years ago. At this time the congregation of Dunaszerdahely develops in the Csallóköz, where 19 families lived in 1728. The oldest Jewish inhabitant of the community had arrived nineteen years before. Likewise in the Csallóköz, in Tejfalu near Somorja, the 1746 conscription found 15 families, of which the oldest had been residing there for 27 years. The settlers often had not yet taken root in their place of residence. Thus in Szered the 1735 conscription found only 8 families instead of the 23 enumerated in 1728. Such changes can be found in other communities as well. Of the three border counties lying on the left bank of the Danube (Pozsony, Nyitra and Trencsén), in the years of the published conscriptions Nyitra and Pozsony were the areas of our country most densely inhabited by Jews. The conscriptions of the three counties have survived from three decades of the 18th century. The conscriptions of Pozsony and Nyitra counties, however, are not complete. Thus in the 1728 Pozsony county conscription such populous Jewish settlements as Stomfa and Cseszte are missing. And in the 1727 Nyitra one the Jewry of the three districts lying close to the mining region is not recorded; the conscription took place only in the Vágújhely and Szakolca districts. By contrast, in Trencsén county the enumeration was carried out twice in the year 1727. The most accurate and reliable are the conscriptions carried out in the thirties, in these counties too, as throughout the country. Of the enumerations made in the forties, the Pozsony conscription of 1747 and the Nyitra one, as well as the Trencsén county conscription of 1746, have survived. These aimed to show the number of souls of the Jewish population for the purpose of levying the extraordinary tax. Since the counties held the poll tax levied without the consent of the Diet to be unlawful, and believed that the poor among the Jewry could not pay it, they often refrained from enumerating the destitute and laid no weight on the accuracy of the census data.
The principal data on the Jewry of the three counties are the following:

According to the 1728 conscription of Pozsony county, Jews live in 40 communities (including the free royal town of Szentgyörgy). The number of heads of family is 244; in twenty-seven communities only one family each lives. In 35 communities there are everywhere fewer than ten. More populous congregations: Pozsony Váralja III [OCR “III”, probably 111] families, Szered 21, Szerdahely 16, Szentgyörgy and Galánta 15 families each.

In Trencsén county, according to the conscription carried out on 18 February 1727, Jews live in 28 communities of the county; the number of heads of family is 69. In sixteen communities only one family each lives, in Illava 12 families, in the other communities everywhere fewer than ten families.

In the period between 29 September 1727 and 1 March 1728 the Jewry of the county's four districts was enumerated again, when 84 families were counted in 28 communities. In ten communities only one family each was found, in Illava fifteen, in Beczkó twelve, in the rest fewer than ten.

In 1735, not counting the free royal town of Trencsén, 145 families were enumerated in 44 communities. In twenty communities only one family each lived, in Beczkó 18, in Illava 15, in the rest fewer than ten.

According to the incomplete 1745 conscription, 113 families would have lived in 34 communities, in IS [OCR; probably 18] only one family each, in Beczkó ten, in Illava 9 families; in the rest fewer than ten were registered. In the year 1746 a more accurate conscription was carried out, when 172 families with .G92 [OCR; probably 692] souls were registered in 55 communities. In sixteen communities only one family lived, in Beczkó 15, in the community of Baán 13, in Illava likewise l:~ [OCR; probably 13], in Puchó 12 and in Nemes Podhragy ll [OCR; probably 11], in the rest fewer than ten. According to the national summary, the two-forint extraordinary poll tax was likewise demanded in Trencsén county from 692 persons.

In contrast with the regions of the western border, in Árva, Turócz and Liptó counties, which extend along the upper course of the Vág, the Jewish population is still sparse in this period.

In Árva county, only in one of the market towns, Nagyfalu, did two related Jewish families live in 1725; they retail the lord's wine and carry on peddling. In 1735 five Jewish families in all were found settled in three communities (Alsókubin 3, in Nagyfalu one and in the community of Zászkal one). No conscription from the forties has survived in this county. We learn only that in 1747 eleven families

Source scan
pay the extraordinary tax amounting to 51 forints. The communities in which the families lived are not named in the document.

Two conscriptions of Turócz county from the year 1727 have survived. The first is dated 13 February 1727 and lists 13 families in three communities of the county (Szent Márton 9, Szucsán 3 and Mossocz 1). The later conscription from the same year (29 September 1727) finds 10 families in Szent Márton and 2 in Szucsán. In the thirties, however, the enumeration of the Jews no longer took place in this county, because a decree expelled them from the vicinity of the mining region; indeed the county writes in its submission addressed to the Lieutenancy Council in 1745 that the single Jew, the poor chirurgus [surgeon] living in Thurán, had also been removed.

In Liptó county in 1727, 13 families lived only in Szent Miklós, and two in nearby Palugya; the noble families Okolicsányi, Pongrácz and Plathy made their settlement possible. The immigrants all came from Moravia. Judging by the names, they were made up of several related families; they live partly on manorial, partly on serf plots. The county officials carrying out the conscription took this circumstance into account, because those living on curial plots paid only the landlord, whereas those settled on portal plots also owed contribution to the county. The 1187 [OCR; the year is evidently misread] conscription finds all 15 families of the county in Liptószentmiklós. No conscription of the county's Jewry from the forties has survived. In 1746 the extraordinary poll tax levied on the Jews was paid in this county by 51 individuals. Liptó county repeatedly figures as a transit area for Jewish immigrants pressing eastward, who were compelled to avoid the mining regions.

3. The removal of the Jews from the mining region [OCR heading garbled: “A zeidákmalo a bányavidékról való eltávolítá8a”]

The settlements spread from the border counties to the more inland territories as well: to Turócz and Bars, neighbouring Nyitra and Trencsén counties, and to the more distant Hont county. Royal decrees at the end of the 17th century (23 January 1693, 29 July 1695) prohibited the residence of Jews within an area of seven miles from the mining towns, and Ferenc Rákóczi II too, in his instruction addressed to the national economic council, orders that the Turks and Jews be kept away from the mining towns. They feared for the revenue of the precious-metal mines on account of the trading Jews. These prohibitive decrees fell into oblivion, and so it could happen that landlords admitted Jews onto their estates lying in the vicinity of the mining towns as well. The newly organized government authorities, however, renewed these prohibitive provisions in 1724–25, threatening those who violated them with the severest punishment (sub poena amissionis vitae) [under pain of loss of life].

How seriously this matter was taken is attested by the documents relating to it. Nyitra county repeatedly called upon Count Péter Berényi to remove the Jews living on his Tapolcsány estate. The count turned to the ruler with a petition that the Jews be left to him, pointing out that at the time he had bought this property from the royal chamber together with the income due to him from the Jews there, and that their removal would be to his great detriment. Count János Pálffy, the Ban of Croatia, likewise asks that he be allowed to keep his Jews living in Bajmócz. The Hungarian Court Chancellery referred the request of the two magnates to the Court Chamber, which was the supreme authority of the mining towns. The king (Károly III [Charles III])

2 Oklevéltár VII. 17

PDF viewer page 20

Source scan
on 19 August 1727 refuses to grant the request of the two magnates; in the interest of securing the royal revenue he requires the removal of the Jews from the communities of Bajmócz and Tapolcsány.

In the conscriptions of 1725 and 1727 we encounter in several places Jews expelled from the mining region who are seeking a new place of residence. Most instructive in this respect is the 1725 conscription of Xógrád [OCR; Nógrád] county, where those expelled from the mining region (Korpona, Bars county; Közép Palajta, Hont county) had at that time been living for half a year or a year. Most of them, nine families, found a new home in Szécsény, where Count János Ádám Forgács received them. Komárom county, lying south of the mining region, in 1727 considered it necessary to supplement the Jewish conscription already prepared in 1725, owing to the number of expellees who had arrived in the community of Osúz [OCR; elsewhere Csúz] lying in the northern part of the county. In the covering letter with which the county submits the Jewish conscription to the Lieutenancy Council, it justifies the failure to meet the deadline thus: “de novo ad possessionem Csuz comita tu riostro adjacentern nonnullos ex comiratu Nit.triensi expulsos adveniti” [OCR Latin garbled; sense: that some persons expelled from Nyitra county have newly arrived at the estate of Csuz adjacent to our county]. For several expellees we read in the conscription: “recentcr ex oppido Drmény” [OCR; “recently from the market town of (Ü)rmény”]. In the conscriptions of the communities of Trencsén (1727) and Nyitra (1728) we find listed separately the names of the expellees who came from the localities of the mining-region territory Pöstyén, :\yitra [OCR; Nyitra], Radosnya, Tapolcsány, Ürmény, Gellúnfalva [OCR], Zsámbokrót [OCR]: “eonscriptio .Iudacorurn reeenter adventorum” [OCR; “conscription of the recently arrived Jews”]. Those newly arrived from the Bajmócz estate are mentioned in Szepes county (1728) and in Pozsony county (1728). The Bajmócz Jewish family that arrived in Szepes county went first to Hozelecz [OCR “Hozeleezre”] and from there to Hunfalva. About the members of the Bajmócz Jewish family that arrived in the Pozsony county community of Haszprunka we read: “Ex dominio Bajmócz expulsi, hue se reciperunt pro interim” [OCR; “expelled from the domain of Bajmócz, they have betaken themselves here for the time being”]. The expellees indeed often did not remain permanently at their first place of refuge, but strove onward. Of two refugees the 1727 conscription reports that they had come five weeks before from Ürmény to Csúz in Komárom county, but that from here they want to go on to Trencsén county, to the community of Ozorecz. Precisely about this community of Csúz we know that in 1727 25 Jewish families lived there under Ferenc Szluha, personalis, and three further lords; by contrast the 1736 conscriptio mentions only three families. In Szécsény, where II [OCR; probably 11] families were counted in 1727, their number had decreased to six by 174G [OCR; probably 1746].

On the other hand, precisely in Nógrád county, Balassa Gyarmat developed into a more populous community, where — according to the 1725 conscription — the first Jew, named :\Iarcus [OCR; Marcus] Leuko, had come some year and a half earlier. Here the widow of Gábor Balassa took the newcomer into her house as a zsellér [cottager], for which he pays six forints yearly to the lordship. He bears no public burden, since he settled on a curial plot. He has a three-year-old son; he trades in cheap cloth and ribbons. The whole value of his stock of goods is sixty forints. He pays a taksa [tax] of five forints yearly to the Jews of Szécsény. When in 1746 we again receive information about the conditions of the Jewry of Nógrád county — the 1735–39 conscription of this county has not survived —, 19 Jewish families already live in Balassa Gyarmat, numbering 78 souls. The congregation already has a praeceptor; of the county's 12 Jewish communities it is the most populous.

From the conscriptions of the twenties we can form a picture of the fate of the Jewry expelled from the territory of the mining towns. The strict enforcement of the prohibitive decrees contributed to a greater dispersal of the Jewry of the north-western Upland and to the formation of new Jewish congregations.

4. The Jewry of the border counties of the North-Eastern Upland [OCR heading garbled: “Az Észalckeleti Feliold. határszél; vár;negyéinek”]

The third border region where, in the twenties of the 18th century, the county men carrying out the conscription already found a larger Jewish population was the region of the north-eastern Carpathians: the communities of Sáros, Zemplén, Ung, Bereg and Máramaros counties. For the five counties listed here it can be established on the basis of the 1725–28 conscriptions in how many communities they lived and what the number of heads of family living there was:
