20:~ [OCR; 203] families, counting 4.5 persons per family, correspond to nearly a thousand souls. We know well that the conscriptions, which were prepared according to different systems, do not give exact data. Thus, for example, in Zemplén county in 172(j [OCR; probably 1726] only the árendások [leaseholders] were listed; nevertheless the figures show that in this region they were to be found in far fewer numbers than in the Transdanubian and Cisdanubian border counties. The finding of the Court Chamber in 17:~8 [OCR; probably 1738] still reads thus: “id publicum est, quod in inferiori, sic dieta Hungaria, seu trans-et cis Danubianis partibus. et numero, et opibus incornparabiliter piu res reperiantur Hebraei” [OCR Latin; sense: “it is publicly known that in so-called Lower Hungary, that is, in the parts beyond and on this side of the Danube, Jews are found incomparably more numerous both in number and in wealth”]. (M. Zs. O. III. no. 465.)

Conspicuous is the relatively small number of Jews in the north-eastern region, although this territory neighboured Poland, where since the end of the 15th century European Jewry, squeezed out of the central territories of Europe, had concentrated and accumulated in numbers of several hundred thousand. We must also take into account that from this populous Jewish centre a large-scale emigration westward began at the time of the Cossack attack (1648–1 65ö [OCR; probably 1656]). The question naturally arises why the flood of refugees did not turn toward the neighbouring territories beyond the Carpathians. There were certainly historical and economic reasons for this. We know from the history of Jewish migrations that when our forebears were compelled to leave their homes, they mostly sought refuge with their Jewish brethren. Jews were then hardly to be found among the sparsely populated, poor Ruthenians of Subcarpathia. Right up to the beginning of the 17th century we know of no Jews living here. The Jews of Poland begin to appear in the first half of the 17th century at vintage time in the Hegyalja region, to buy grapes there and carry home the wine made from them. The 13 north-eastern counties of Hungary belonged economically under the Szepes Chamber. We know the report of the harmincadosok [thirtieth-duty collectors] of the Szepes Chamber, the administrators of the border customs, and the list of Jews from nearby Polish towns already conducting trade in Hungary (Rimanov, Szandec, Visnic, Tarnov, etc.). In the second half of the 17th century the first Jewish leaseholders — originating from Poland — appear on the estates of the border areas (Makovicza, Homonna, Ungvár, Munkács, Huszt). The detailed contract that Imre Thököly concluded with the Jew Efráim concerning the beer and brandy taverns of Munkács has survived (lri78 [OCR; probably 1678]. 1 January. M. Zs. O. VII. no. 742.). Ilona Zrínyi leases to this same Jew Efráim of Munkács the tavern and toll of Szentmiklós, as well as the toll of Podhering (1 January 1691, M. Zs. O. II.

19

PDF viewer page 22

Source scan
no. 148.). In ever greater numbers the Jewish árendások [leaseholders] appear in the border Krajna and Verhovina, who, in keeping with the practice acquired in their Polish homeland, lease, from among the seigneurial usufructs and regalia [OCR “regaU~k”], brandy distilling, beer brewing and the retailing of drink, as well as the manorial mills. This became the most widespread occupation of the Jewry settled here. This also explains why the landlords admitted only one Jewish family each into their village. Of the 203 families of these five counties, 151 families live distributed one to a community. By county their number was divided as follows:

Sáros county: of its 27 communities, in 26 one Jewish family each lived. In only one community, three families.

Zemplén county: of its 56 communities, in 45 only one family each. In Kégy [OCR; probably four] communities two families each (Sárospatak, Tokaj, Varannó, Homonna). In two communities three families (Sátoraljaújhely, Tállya). In one community four families (Erdőbénye). In two communities seven families (Tolcsva, Bodrogkeresztúr).

Ung county: of its 18 communities, in only one do 2 Jewish families live, and in Ungvár four. In the rest only one each.

Bereg county: of its 27 communities, in four live two families each, in Munkács three, in the rest only one each.

Máramaros county: in four of its communities two families each, and only in Szaplonca three; in the rest one each.

This whole region had not a single more populous Jewish community; the most lived in Tolcsva and Bodrogkeresztúr, and even in these only seven families each. Jewish communal life, which enables a congregation to continue traditional religious life, could not develop — something that would surely have exerted an attraction on immigrants accustomed to a pious way of life (regular communal worship, the occupation of the youth with religious teachings, the employment of the functionaries necessary for the continuation of religious life).

In material respects, too, this Subcarpathian territory held out no advantages. They were compelled to restrict themselves exclusively to one branch of occupation: the distilling and brewing leased from the landlords and the retailing of these drinks ensured only a meagre livelihood among the inhabitants of villages languishing in misery. The Zemplén county conscription (1726) records what the leaseholders pay the lordship monthly: these were sums ranging from six to thirty kreuzers. There are leaseholders about whom the conscriptio remarks that the person is miserabilis or profugus. We read of only a few large leaseholders at the centres of the estates. Thus the leaseholder of Zboró pays 500 forints rent yearly. In Ung county the leaseholder of Nagyberezna counts as better-off; besides brandy distilling he also leases the manorial mill, and can call two draught oxen, two cows and a horse his own. In Bereg county the leaseholder of Szentmiklós, together with his eight sub-leaseholders, pays five hundred and eighty-five Rhenish forints yearly. In Máramaros county, at Huszt, one leaseholder also rents three mills in addition to beer and brandy distilling, and pays 600 forints yearly rent for this, while the other Huszt leaseholder was obliged to pay 450 forints yearly to members of the same aristocratic families (of the Kemény and Teleki families). Apart from these few large leaseholders, the rest eked out their lives in very poor circumstances, as the Ung county conscription (1739) remarks about one drink-seller: “Ex nuda cremati educillatione misere victitat” [“he lives miserably from the mere retailing of brandy”]; yearly he could pay his lord no more than eight forints twenty kreuzers.

They could not carry on trade; there was no opportunity for it among the undemanding and poor village population. They are not averse to agriculture: if they receive even a quarter plot on lease, they strive to farm it. In Sáros, Ung and Bereg

20

PDF viewer page 23

Source scan
counties the 1727 conscription mentions persons engaged in agriculture. Of one Jew living in Alsóvereczke (in Bereg county) the conscription remarks: “excolit unum fundum oeconornia omni modo exercet” [OCR; “he cultivates one plot, practises husbandry in every way”]. Of an inhabitant of another locality of the same county it writes: “unum rusticanum fundum excolit” [“he cultivates one peasant plot”], for which he pays twenty forints rent to his landlord.

Where the Jews settled not on manorial, curial plots but on serf plots, they are obliged, over and above their urbarial obligations, to pay tax also to the county treasury, the cassa domestica. From the Jewish inhabitants of the counties belonging to the Szepes Chamber a toleration tax was moreover demanded ever since the activity of the Olbern commission sent out already in 16g8 [OCR; probably 1698]. This tolerantialis tax the chamber leased out for an annual lump sum, and the Jewish leaseholders saw to the collection of the tax. To this refer the remarks that can be read in Bereg county's 1727 conscriptio — incidentally this was the only county in which some district szolgabírák [magistrates] prepared the conscription in Hungarian; these were submitted to the general assembly of the county, which then forwarded them in this form to the Lieutenancy Council — at Bilke, about one named Gléba: “.\z Földes Urnak fizett fl. 100. Az sidonak adott. fl. 10.” [Hungarian; “Paid the landlord fl. 100. Gave the Jew fl. 10.”] Likewise at Bilke: “Földes Urna.k fizctt fl. 10. Az falunak nem ad. Az sidonals adott fl. 5.” [Hungarian, OCR; “Paid the landlord fl. 10. Gives nothing to the village. Gave the Jew fl. 5.”]

The conscriptions of the twenties also attest that Polish Jewry did not at that time flood the border counties, since according to the conscriptions no Jews yet lived in the innermost districts of Sáros, Zemplén and Ung counties.

In the national conscription of the thirties of the 18th century we find the following data for the 5 border counties of this region:

  County             Communities   Families   Persons
  Sáros county           55            61       :U4 [OCR; 314 would agree with the total]
  Zemplén county         74           118       541 [OCR “ff)” after the number]
  Ung county             36            42       162
  Bereg county           37            53       235
  Máramaros county       41            66       371
  Total:                243           340      1623

The increase can be put at a mere 600 souls over a decade, natural increase included.

The data of the ]735-38 [OCR; 1735–38] conscription have already been processed from a historical-statistical standpoint by Ignác Acsády in the large statistical publication “Magyarország népessége a pragmatica sanctio korában” [The Population of Hungary in the Age of the Pragmatic Sanction], issued in 1896. He presented the conscriptions relating to the Jewry separately in the 1897 Yearbook (Évkönyv) of the Israelite Hungarian Literary Society under the title “A magyar zsidók 1735-B8-ban” [OCR; The Hungarian Jews in 1735–38]. For this reason we single out from the conscriptions of these years only a few data of greater interest for settlement history. We consider it necessary to note that, of the counties of the north-eastern border region, Acsády could not process the conscriptions of Ung and Máramaros counties, because their conscriptions are missing from the collective volume in the National Archives (U. et C. Fasc. 71. N° 2.) that served him as a basis when he processed the data relating to the Jewry.

For several families the conscriptions note that they are advena and have been living there only a short time. Their hereditary lords live in Poland; true, they often can no longer even name them. The new settlers cannot quickly and definitively establish themselves in their places of residence. In Sáros county, where in 1727 at 27

21

PDF viewer page 24

Source scan
places Jewish inhabitants were mentioned, in 1737 they are to be found at 57 places. Characteristic of their internal migration is that of the 27 communities listed in 1727, in 1737 they live in only 15. They sought and found a livelihood elsewhere, wherever they were able to come to terms with the lordship. Four families settled in Zboró alone; besides that, in three places two each, everywhere else only one lived. The nobility of Sáros county comes to the defence of the Jewish árendátorok [leaseholders] out of its own economic interest. They complain that the Szepes Chamber burdens their distilling and brewing Jewish leaseholders with tax and proceeds with ruthless means in collecting it. It is to be feared — writes the county's submission — that these burdens and the offensive procedure in collecting the tax will drive the leaseholders to abandon their places of residence and to flee. In reply to this submission the royal instruction came down that they should rather strive to rid themselves of the Jews than to increase their number. (M. Zs. O. III. nos. 451. ló [OCR; unclear] 457.) In Zemplén county, where, considering this region, the Jewry lived in the greatest numbers, more populous settlements did not develop. They lived scattered over the 74 communities. Only in Telesvan [OCR; probably Tolcsva] do ]4 [OCR; probably 14] families live, in Mád 9, in Sátoraljaújhely G [OCR; probably 6], in Nagymihály 5, besides which in four communities B [OCR; probably 3] and in five 2 families. In the other places everywhere only one each. As to their origin, as their names also show, almost all are of Polish descent; only in the southern part of this long, drawn-out county, in the Hegyalja region (Erdőbénye, Tállya, Mád, Bodrogkeresztúr, Sátoraljaújhely, etc.), did a few Moravian Jews from Ungarischbrod end up, who profess Count Kaunicz as their hereditary lord. Of the six of Hungarian origin, Iojses ] Iarcus [OCR; probably Mojses Marcus], living in Sárospatak, came there from the Pálffy estate, from Stomfa; the other five, who declare themselves born in Hungary and whose places of residence are Gálszécs, Pelejte, Pazdics, cannot name their hereditary lord. These belong among those settlers whose forebears too had already been living in this homeland for two or three generations. Thus in Pazdics, which lies near Nagymihály, a Jewish leaseholder is mentioned as early as 1691 (M. Zs. O. II. no. 188.).

In Ung county (lng [OCR]), 4:{ [OCR; number uncertain] families with ](l5 [OCR; number uncertain] souls lived in 3G [OCR; probably 36] communities. They are of Polish origin; however, of the heads of family 27 cannot name the hereditary lord whose subject they once were. These — or their forebears — emigrated from there long ago; one head of family is of Lithuanian origin, and one calls himself a libertinus. The only one of the Jews living in this county who still regards himself as a subject of Count Kaunicz is at the same time the only Jew in the county who rents a permanent shop in Ungvár. In the column of the conscription in which the head of family's hereditary lord had to be entered, we read: “Subditus Illustrissimi Domini Comitis Kaunicz in Moravia manet.” [“Subject of the Most Illustrious Lord Count Kaunicz; resides in Moravia.”] He got furthest east of the large number of Moravian immigrants. Only in Ungvár and in two further communities near it do three Jewish families each live, in Perecseny two, in the remaining 32 everywhere only one each. Under such circumstances communal life could not develop; in all, only two families engaged a praeceptor for their children.

In Hereg [OCR; Bereg] county the greater part of the Jewry lived on the former Rákóczi estate, which in 1725 passed to the Schönborn family. The 5B [OCR; probably 53] families live in :37 [OCR; probably 37] communities, in 28 of which only one each. The most populous community is Bilke, where six families are mentioned, and Munkács-Szent Miklós with four. In this county, too, most are leaseholders of the manorial brandy distilleries and breweries, and retailers of their drink. The only one who has a permanent shop lives in Vereczke. There are some who trade in wine, cattle and horses. They lease the manorial mill and toll. Among those enumerated we find a tailor newly immigrated from Poland, and another who dyes linen for the people. Of one leaseholder the conscription remarks: “simulque opifex,

PDF viewer page 25

Source scan
qui facit ollas crernatorias, non tamen exercet.” [“who makes … pots, but does not practise it”; OCR “crernatorias” unclear] Some cultivate a rented plot. The census mentions a praeceptor in five of the county's families.
      The censuses of the forties still testify to the immigration of Jews into the border counties, but in some counties there are already fewer newcomers. Thus in Sáros in 17:17 [OCR; year unclear, possibly 1737] we know of Jewish families in 55 villages, and in 1744 they live in 57 villages. The number of families, however, shows no growth over these years, or perhaps not all of them were recorded because of the taxation being demanded. They still live in scattered settlements. There are three villages where two families were counted; in all the others only one. The census mentions the teacher of the county's Jewish community: “Judaeorum instructor” [instructor of the Jews].
       In Zemplén we can already see the beginnings of concentration into communities: in Tolcsva and Mád 13 families each [OCR “1:3-1:3”], in Újhely 12 [OCR “]2”], in Tokaj 8 [OCR “s”; uncertain], and in Nagymihály H [OCR; number unclear]. These are the seeds of the later populous congregations. Only in Újhely [OCR “U jhelyen l-STállyán nw”; garbled] do two Jews of Moravian origin have a permanent shop.
       In Ung county most already live in Ungvár (H [OCR; number unclear] families); in eight villages there are two families each, in two villages three each, and in all the rest only one each.
       In Bereg county too the situation is that in the great majority of villages only one family each lives. Only in Munkács are there 5, in Dávidháza 4, in Alsó Vereczke three. Where there are several sons in a family, we find an instructor. The entire Jewry of the county is of Polish origin, as the names of the families show.
       In Máramaros county (174U [OCR; year unclear]) :l4H [OCR; possibly 348] persons in iL nn [OCR; number illegible] Jewish families were counted for tax purposes; they paid the two-forint tax demanded from them, a sum of (iDG [OCR; possibly 696] forints. Máramarossziget is already the most populous congregation; ten families live there.
       The censuses of the forties served the levying and collection of an extraordinary war tax. Queen Maria Theresa required this as a contribution to the costs of the War of Succession then in progress. Of the five border counties, only Bereg is able to report as early as 1747 that it has paid in full the sum demanded from the Jews, 544 forints for 272 persons; the other counties, however, complain in their submissions to the Lieutenancy Council of the difficulties involved in collecting first the six-forint tax levied on Jewish heads of family and then the two-forint extraordinary tax required for every individual person. Zemplén county (1748) believes that the poor Jews would emigrate to Poland to escape the new tax, and that it cannot collect even :lOO [OCR; probably 300] forints from them. In its submission written in 1747 it reports that even by distraint it could not obtain more than 27H [OCR; possibly 278] forints :m [OCR; number unclear] kreuzer from them. They are such dirt-poor people that the movable goods of individual persons cannot be valued at the two forints demanded as poll tax. Nevertheless in 174H [OCR; year unclear] it pays mH; [OCR; amount unclear] forints for :l4H [OCR; possibly 348] persons.
      Ung county reports (174:1 [OCR; year unclear, possibly 1743]) that the entire yearly earnings of the families can scarcely be estimated at 10–12 forints. And in 1745 it complains that it cannot collect the county tax from them even by distraint. The tax levied in 174G [OCR; possibly 1746] was paid for 21G [OCR; possibly 216] persons. Máramaros county (1745) calls for the expulsion of the Jews, as it had already demanded in its statutes drawn up in ](iDO [OCR; possibly 1690]: “The noble county sees a great many inconveniences in the Jews living here.” The Jews, however, were always retained by the magnates and nobles on whose estates they were employed.
       Having dealt with the border regions, let us now survey the distribution of the Jewry of the interior regions of the country.

23

PDF viewer page 26

Source scan
       5. The census of the Jewry of the counties neighbouring Subcarpathia

      Here we first examine the censuses of the counties of the territories neighbouring Subcarpathia, namely the data of Abaújvár, Borsod and Ugocsa counties.
      Abaújvár County (1727). Jews still inhabit it sparsely; they are found in only 7 villages, and everywhere only one family each. Among them there was already one who had settled long before; such may have been the one living in Fonyó [“Fonyón”], who — according to the census — leased from Trautzon the beer-brewing and brandy-distilling in Sárospatak and Regécz, for which he paid the lordship a large annual lease (árenda) of 1700 forints, and also paid tax to the county treasury, just like the other four lessees (árendátor) in the county. These were somehow already regarded as long-established residents, whereas the other two families were only commorans [OCR “CSf Lk”, read as “only”]. Even where it is indicated on whose estate they live, these two families are described as vagus and instabilis. It is to these that the county refers the remark which it formulates in the covering letter of the conscriptio as follows: “Eosclem qWLgentern vagarn in gremio riostro vix aliquod stabilimentum habere ... rarus est, qui locorum non mutaret.” [roughly: “that these, a wandering people, scarcely have any permanent establishment in our midst ... rare is the one who does not change his place”; OCR garbled in several words.] Doubtless several more newly arrived Jews who had not yet found a final place of settlement were moving about the county, but they were not entered in the list.
      For this reason it is not surprising that barely a decade later the 173H [OCR; possibly 1738] census shows Jews in 28 villages: one family each in 17 villages, two each in eight, and three each in three. Most of the families declare themselves to be of Polish origin; of the 28, 23 can also name their former hereditary lord, but five no longer can. But Moravian Jews also reached the territory of this county, ten families; seven of them may have come together, since they name the same person as their hereditary lord in Moravia, Retolt (perhaps Rotal, who was lord of Holleschau); some, by their own declaration, even pay their former lord 2–4–6 forints annually. The four families that drifted here from the populous Jewish communities of the western border come from the Esterházy, Zichy, Pálffy and Czobor estates. The Jews of the county are everywhere in the service of the landlords as beer-brewers, brandy-distillers and tavern-keepers; the census lists one tailor, one peddler and one dealer in hides. A praeceptor is mentioned with five families. Two praeceptors with families also appear; this can be explained by their belonging to the Polish immigrants versed in Jewish learning, who gathered around them the sons of those living scattered in the villages. Characteristically, in the families of the two teachers we also find an assistant teacher.
      The 1745 census of Abaúj county no longer shows any greater numerical increase compared with the previous conscriptio. It lists forty lessees, naming their landlords as well. Among the heads of family, even those who are not lessees have their lord indicated as follows: “Judaeus domini Jobi, Judaeus dominae Peehianae” [“Jew of Lord Job, Jew of Lady Peehiana”; OCR “Peehianae” uncertain]. They live scattered across the county; only in two of the thirty-nine villages do two families each live. The extraordinary tax levied in 1746 was paid by 206 individuals.
      Whereas in Abaúj county the majority of the Jewry is of Polish origin, in Borsod county the situation is different: here the Moravian immigrants are in the majority. The 1727 census knows of only a few Jewish families that had found their way here. In Hangács, in the Szendrő district, three families are mentioned; of them the longest resident there is the person who had come ten years earlier from Holleschau in Moravia, where Rothal was his landlord. We learn of his route of wandering in Hungary. He first settled in Liptó county, from there went to Zemplén, until finally he arrived here in Borsod; half a year ago his wife's uncle joined him from Silesia, with his Polish servant.

24

PDF viewer page 27

Source scan
      According to this census three Jewish families live in Miskolc. The first came 18 years before the census, with his parents, from Poland. By occupation he is a brandy-distiller. The other two came from Moravia eight and three years ago respectively. Of these two, the family of one still remained in Moravia. In Ónod [OCR “6nodon”] two Jewish families live: one is from Ungarischbrod, a subject of Kaunicz, and pays his lord a hundred thalers a year; the other originates from Trebitsch in Moravia, came here from Zemplén, and is a peddler by occupation. Apart from them, only in one village, Felsőzsolca, is a brandy-distiller from Ungarischbrod mentioned, who came there in 1724.
      The 1736 census shows a significant numerical increase in this county too. Already 35 families live in 19 villages. In fourteen villages likewise one each, but we can already observe some concentration: nine live in Miskolc, four in Csaba and in Ónod. By origin most are Moravian, coming mostly from Ungarisch-Brod. Only six are of Polish origin, even if those of unknown origin are counted as such. Three families came here from Nyitra, from the Czobor estate. Of the nine families of Miskolc, eight already carry on trade: “exercet quaestum cum mcr eibus divcrsi generis” [“trades in goods of various kinds”] or “exercet quaestum cum rebus minutioribus” [“trades in smaller wares”] — can be read in their occupation column. For the ninth family this column is not filled in. In the other villages of the county, where they live scattered, they are brandy-distillers and tavern-keepers. Particularly striking is the many-sided occupation of the Moravian Jew living in the village of Sály, to which Acsády already drew attention. He has a permanent shop, the only one in the county, yet he also peddles and practises crafts as well: glazier and tailor. For the lordship he is brandy-distiller, tavern-keeper and also várncsa [OCR; word unclear, possibly “toll-collector”]. He renders many dues in kind to his hereditary lord, Count Kaunicz, and to his present master. In this county the census-takers sent out required the Jews to take the decretal oath according to Verbőczi for their declaration, as we read in the census at every family in the Szentpéter district: “deposito Decrotali Juramento” [“having taken the decretal oath”].
      The third county, lying further inland: Ugocsa. On its territory individual Jewish families had already settled at the end of the 17th century, just as in neighbouring Bereg [OCR “Hereg”] county. In 1698–99 the thirtieth-collector (harmincados) who carried out the census mentions six Jewish families in five larger villages of the county (Komját, Tisza-Újhely, Sásvár, Bökény and Tisza-Újlak. M. Zs. O. II. no. 273). From the 1699 name list (Consignatio Judaeorum in comitatu Ugocsensi residentium) we learn not only under whose lordship the six families live, whose occupation is the retailing of wine and brandy, but also that they are very poor, because in the troubled times preceding the kuruc movement the rebels plundered most of them.
     The 1728 census shows no greater growth compared with the HiD9 [OCR; probably 1699] name list, for it lists only 12 families in nine villages of the county. The names of the Jews — apart from two — do point to Polish origin, but most of them may have lived there for some time; they farm, even if they each have a still for brandy-distilling, from which they draw an annual income of 5–12 forints. They pay their lords their urbarial dues and pay the annual toleration tax to the Szepes administration.
     Barely a decade later, the census taken in 1735 shows a considerable increase: they already live in }() [OCR; number unclear] villages, and the number of families is 25. In nine villages one family each, in five two each, and in two three each. Their origin column is not filled in on the form. When in 1744 the Jewry is recorded for tax purposes, the county makes a remarkable submission about its Jewish population. The county's

25

PDF viewer page 28

Source scan
report knows of only two or three Jewish families and writes: their lot is the same as that of the poor common people. They earn their bread by peasant labour. If they are burdened excessively with taxes, they change their place of residence. Nevertheless, the extraordinary tax levied in 1746 was paid in this county by 135 persons.



                       6. The Jewry of the counties beyond the Tisza (Tiszántúl)

      Ugocsa county leads us over into the region beyond the Tisza. Among the counties here, in Szatmár as early as 1699 five Jewish lessees were recorded, with details of their property circumstances; they distilled brandy from barley and oats (M. Zs. O. II. no. 279). The list of the Jewish lessees of Szatmár county from the year 1721 has survived (M. Zs. O. III. no. 221); they are mentioned in Nagykároly and in three other villages. In Nagykároly six Jewish families were already living at that time in dwellings built by Count Sándor Károlyi. Among them is Michael Judaeus, who as toll-collector and brewer pays his lord 300 Rhenish forints a year. We also read in the list that the German Jews who had just arrived, whose names it also gives (Áhron Leph, Leezer Orr, Német Mojses and Német Salamon), will be accommodated in the year 1722 in houses built for them. Sándor Károlyi, who was a circumspect landowner, carried out large-scale settlement on his estates. Among the many Swabian settlers he also brought in Jews. Acsády reports, on the basis of the 1723 urbarium of the village of Nagykároly, that the count had already had houses of brick and mud built for one Greek and six Jewish merchants, for each of which the person concerned paid an annual taxa of 18 forints. Just at the time of the census a similar house was being built for the seventh Jewish family. The inn had been leased since 1721 by Zsidó Mihály, who also had a house next door; he likewise leased the brewery (Acsády: Magyarország népessége a pragmatica sanctio korában. Budapest, 1896. 373–74.). Acsády likewise quotes from the work of Károly Á'ble [OCR; name spelling uncertain] (Gróf Károlyi Ferencz és kora. I. 88–89.) that in 1724 Sándor Károlyi himself sent the Jews a rabbi from Pozsony. Sándor Károlyi writes as follows to Mátyás Pollereczky from Pozsony on 17 June 1724: “I trust the Jewry too has received with good grace and joy the Rabbi sent down. Now let them just multiply, I do not oppose it ...”
      The conscriptiones of Szatmár county from the twenties and thirties have not come down to us; only the one from 174f3 [OCR; year unclear] has survived. Unfortunately this too gives only a list of names of the county's Jewish heads of family, and not even by village. One hundred and five heads of family and 241 family members, that is, 846 persons in total [sic per OCR; 105 + 241 = 346], were counted in the county, and they also paid the 2-forint poll tax per head. The names of the heads of family mostly show the Polish-style “vics” ending, but we already find Hungarian names as well: Kis Mihály, Lazarus Nagy, Simeon Szabó, Alexander Pap, Lóbl Sánta, Adamus Furkas [OCR; possibly Farkas], Husli Csenger and Moyses Kálló.
      Szabolcs was in close connection with Szatmár county; in this county too Jews were already living in 1721 in three villages (Nyírbéltek, Mada, Ibrány: M. Zs. O. III. no. 221). Of this county too only the very first Jewish census, from 174fi [OCR; year unclear], has survived. Jews lived in 44 villages in the county's four districts, GO [OCR; possibly 60] families in total, numbering 221 souls. By density their distribution was as follows: in 31 villages only one each, in Ll [OCR; number unclear] two each, and in two villages three families. The Jewish population of the county had not yet taken root. This is indicated by the county's covering letter, which it attached to the census when it submitted it to the Lieutenancy Council.

26

PDF viewer page 29

Source scan
 Many of the Jews fled from the taxation: “ut. vix medietas illorum cum summa capitationali asportata remansisset.” [roughly: “so that scarcely half of them remained, the poll-tax sum having been carried off”; sense uncertain]
       Of Közép-Szolnok (Middle Szolnok) county we can present only the census of 1727. They filled in the table sent by the Lieutenancy Council, which shows six families in five villages of the county. In Király Darócz — where Jews had lived since 1721 — there were two families; in the other villages only one each was found. They do not bear Polish-style names, only biblical ones (Moyses, Elias, Salamon, David, Judas Izsák and Judas Benjamin). Of the son of one of the Király Darócz Jews the report writes: “sehoiam Judaicam frequentat” [“attends the Jewish school”]; in another family it mentions a relative who is “filios in Literis informans” [“instructing the sons in letters”]. Four live on peasant plots and farm them, while two lease manorial taverns.
       The censuses provide few data on the other counties beyond the Tisza. There is still hardly any Jewish population in this area. This applies to Bihar, Békés and Csanád, as well as Csengrád [sic; Csongrád] counties.
       In Bihar county — apart from Nagyvárad and the villages belonging to it — the 1727 census mentions no Jews. In 173G [OCR; possibly 1736] too — apart from Nagyvárad and Pece Szőlős, which belongs to it — only two families are mentioned in the county: one in Sámson, the other in Nyíracsád. Even in 1745 it is reported that they could not record the county's Jewry because of their constant wandering. They appeared in the county several times and in considerable numbers, but had not yet found permanent settlement. Nevertheless in 174H [OCR; year unclear] the extraordinary tax levied was collected from 252 persons.
       In the whole of Békés county, in whose depopulated areas large-scale settlement was then taking place, only two newly arrived Jewish families are mentioned. One is a brewer on Baron Harruckern's estate in Gyula, the other a brandy-distiller in Gyula-Vár on the Levernborg [OCR “Levern- borg”] estate. The Jew of Gyula came from Buda; his lease runs for three years; when his contract expires — Harruckern's officials reported — they will not renew it. They will not tolerate him there any longer. According to the 1n5 [OCR; year unclear, possibly 1735] census the county's only Jewish family again lives only in Gyula, and in 174G [OCR; possibly 1746] we find one more family there with five employees, one of whom, however, absconded at the time of the census. In 1748, 5 persons paid the extraordinary tax in the county.
      Csanád county was freed late from Turkish rule and is for the most part deserted territory. The national census of 1720 found in the whole county, apart from Makó, two villages, in which the number of taxpaying households was 278. Besides the three villages, six puszták belonging to Makó are mentioned, where agriculture is carried on. Among these puszták appears Földiák. There the 174:~ [OCR; year unclear] census mentions a Jewish family, a lessee of György Návay, which came there from the village of Újszász in Pest county. Návay also took in a Jewish family from Acsa, likewise in Pest county. Jewish families gladly moved to places where they found relatives and acquaintances. Thus according to the 1745 census two more families from Acsa moved to Földiák. All of them duly paid the extraordinary military tax. The census from 1740 [OCR; year as printed, possibly misread] lists in Földiák, still the only place in the county inhabited by Jews, three families and one unmarried man, because at the news of the census two families with (j [OCR; number unclear] children fled to the neighbouring county. According to the summary from the year 1748, 12 Jewish individuals paid the poll tax.
      In Csongrád county, which like the previous one was very sparsely inhabited, no Jew had yet settled at the time of the censuses published here. Csongrád, Csanád and Békés counties already lay far from the more densely settled places of the Jewry of Polish origin,

27

PDF viewer page 30

Source scan
and therefore they did not reach here at that time. Moreover, in the still very sparsely inhabited areas of the Great Plain they could hardly hope for an adequate material basis for their livelihood; it also contributed to this that they did not wish to move too far away from the Jewish-inhabited areas, where they had the opportunity to carry on their religious community life in one way or another.


                        7. The counties of the separating zone

     Here we must point out that the three counties last listed formed part of that zone in which Jews either did not live or were not permitted to live. The counties of this zone to some extent separated the western and eastern immigrant Jewish groups from each other. This separating area begins in the north in the Szepesség, where only in Hunfalva could some Jews live. It continued across the territory of Gömör county, which as late as 1752 reports to the Lieutenancy Council that no Jew has lived on its territory for many years, and which in its statute drawn up in 17U7 [OCR; year unclear] declared: “years ago, for well-founded reasons, the universitas of the county resolved that no nobleman may admit a Christ-denying Jew harmful to the common good.” Of our censuses, only that of 1746 mentions five Jewish families in the county: two in Putnok and three in three villages close to it, 24 souls in all. The zone continues with Torna county, where only one family resided in 1728, then with Heves and Külső Szolnok counties, where at the time of the 173ü [OCR; possibly 1736] census 8 families did live in Gyöngyös and one in Gyöngyös Halászi, but in the following year the two united counties expel the Jews: “solely for the purpose of protecting the inhabitants of the county”. In the privileged territories, in the Nagy Kunság (Greater Cumania) and in the towns of the Hajdúság, Jews could not live. In response to the Lieutenancy Council's call for a census of the Jews, the administrator of the Jász-Kun district writes from Jászberény in 1745 that not a single Jew is to be found in their district; only from time to time does one or another obtain permission to carry on trade in petty goods, travelling hither and thither. The captain of the Hajdú towns reports in 1744, when the census of Jews is requested of him — referring to his earlier replies: “In our towns not even one Jew has yet been found, so now too we can write nothing other than that.” Békés county, as we have seen, did not admit Jews. The one or two families mentioned in the 1735 and 1746 censuses form an exception. In Csanád too only one landlord admitted a few persons. Csongrád county reports on the occasion of the 174G [OCR; possibly 1746] census that there is not a single Jew in the county holding a residence permit. The existence of this separating zone over several decades in some way restricted the mass advance of eastern Jewry towards the western parts of our country, and in this way separated the eastern and western Jewry, which differed somewhat from each other in customs, culture and language. This found expression in the distinction, long current within Hungarian Jewry, which designated the western Jewry as “oberlandi” and the eastern as “unterlandi”.


             8. The Jewry of the southern and northern counties of Transdanubia [OCR heading partly garbled]

      We intend to return to the settlement conditions of the Jews between the Danube and the Tisza, but for the sake of chronological order let us first survey the situation that developed in Transdanubia. In the development of the settlement of the Jewry of Transdanubia we can clearly distinguish

28

PDF viewer page 31

Source scan
two forms. One is characteristic of the southern part of Transdanubia; the other is observable rather in the more northerly areas.
      The southern parts of Transdanubia — primarily Vas, Zala and Somogy counties — are characterised by the fact that the first Jewish settlers reached them by swarming out from the populous Jewish communities of the western border. Here we must particularly emphasise the role of the populous Jewish community of Rohonc in Vas county. It was truly the mother community of the Jewish inhabitants of the said counties. In Rohonc, under the protection of the Batthyány family, so populous a Jewish congregation developed that its members, because of their great number, were compelled to earn their living in villages outside their place of residence. According to the 1725 census — as already mentioned — 152 Jewish families lived in 59 houses. Already according to the canonical visitation record (canonica visitatio) of 1713, 400 Jews lived in the community (Jenő Házi: Die kanonische Visitation des Stefan Kazó. Eisenstadt, 195G [OCR; year unclear].). The “Rechnitzer Juden Lista oder Verzeichnusz” [“Rechnitz Jews' list or register”], prepared for the lordship in 1727 by the leaders of the Rohonc Jewish community, lists by name the WO [OCR; number unclear] heads of family of their community, which — taking even four-member families as a basis — would amount to G40 [OCR; possibly 640] persons. At the end of the 1725 census there is a remark that the women, unmarried sons and daughters, and the widows were not listed. It adds further: if we consider the number of the Rohonc Jews, we must say: “non aliud nisi multitudo copiosa dici potest.” [“it can be called nothing other than an abundant multitude.”] Rohonc, which at this time was the most populous community in the county, counted 210 taxpayers according to the national census published in Acsády's book, while Szombathely had only 1G5 [OCR; possibly 165]. The numerous Jewry there could by no means earn its bread, although in Rohonc too, as in other communities of the country where Jews obtained land, they practised agriculture on serf plots. Acsády writes of Rohonc: “they also cultivated the land, vineyard and meadow belonging to the house in return for census. They likewise leased the toll. Although they also farmed, the steward of the estate, Zsigmond Somogyi, did not allow them to be recorded” (op. cit. 387.). In 1735 twelve Jews in Rohonc had a permanent shop, more than in Pozsony and Kismarton with their more numerous Jewish populations. Among the inhabitants of the community there were also various craftsmen: besides butchers, tailors, tanners, braid-makers (paszományos) and glaziers. The poorer ones, the cottars, dealt in selling agricultural produce (beans, kölcs [OCR; probably millet], cabbage, honey, “sír” [OCR; word unclear]) and also bought up animal hides, furs and wool. Nevertheless many of them were forced to secure their subsistence outside the community. Since in Rohonc they enjoyed security under the protection and jurisdiction of the Batthyánys, they had little wish to move elsewhere, even if they set out for neighbouring and then more distant villages to carry on their itinerant trade there: they went round the villages carrying their wares on their backs, or, if they had been able to acquire a horse, took the goods for sale by cart. From the nearby villages, for which they set out in the morning, they returned home by evening. If they went further, they carried on their trade there on weekdays and returned to their home only for the Sabbath day of rest. There were, however, some who already travelled to more distant counties: they visited the villages of Zala, Veszprém, Somogy and Tolna. These spent only the great festivals in Rohonc with their families. The 1725 census, following the list of the Rohonc Jews, states the following: “Ex praenominatis Judaeis quidarn in comitatibue Szaladicnsi, Veszprimierisi, Simeghiensi, Tolnonsi, ac ubi ipsis comodius videtur quaestum suum exercont, ae solum tempore Festorum, ad dornicilia sua redeunt.” [“Some of the aforenamed Jews carry on their trade in the counties of Zala, Veszprém, Somogy and Tolna, and wherever it seems more convenient to them, and return to their homes only at the time of the Festivals.”] The Jewish list of 1727 mentioned above already adds to the names of 42 heads of family where they trade away from home, and

29

PDF viewer page 32

Source scan
we also learn from the list whether each stays there alone or with his family, while nonetheless continuing to count as a member of the community. These entries read as follows: Aron Simon mit Weib und Kinder zu Oanischa [“with wife and children at …”; OCR “Oanischa” uncertain], or Abraham Samuel handelt zu Pintzehelle [“trades at …”] etc. (M. Zs. O. V/2. no. 1087). Those absent were regarded as members of the community, since they contributed to the community's burdens; it is therefore understandable that the decree supplementing the community's organisational regulation, the “Judenpolizei”, approved by Eleonora Batthyány, states: “The election of the Jewish judge shall be held at the time of the Jewish New Year, because then they are all at home” (Magyar Zsidó Szemle. XXX. 191:3 [OCR; probably 1913]. 184.).
     Where — in larger places — they could count on a permanent clientele, they opened shops. Acsády mentions that as early as 1720 three Jewish merchants had shops in Szentgotthárd, but lived in Rohonc. In 1725, apart from Rohonc, there were permanent Jewish residents in the county only on the two other Batthyány estates, Szalonak and Németújvár. Starting from these communities, they begin to settle in the villages of the county. This growth is shown by the following data: in 17:15 [OCR; possibly 1735] we find Jews in 17 villages, and in 1746 already in 53 villages.
     The neighbouring county of Zala was populated by the Jews of Rohonc. The 1728 census lists Jews in seven villages (Keszthely, Szentgrót, Sümeg, Tapolca, Kanizsa, Zalaegerszeg, Alsó Lendva). Their families are for the most part still in Rohonc; they themselves also appear in the 1727 list of the Rohonc Jewish community, with a note of where they trade outside the community. Thus: Josephus Moyses handelt zu Kistel (Keszthely), Jacob Hirshel handelt zu St. Geróth (Szent Grót), Benedict Marx handelt zu Simeg, etc. The covering letter of Zala county to its 1728 census remarks that the Jews came to the county from Rohonc: “ex oppido Rohoncz comitatui Castriferrei adjacente isthuc transire eonsuevissent et majori in parte uxores et famulitium proles que suas actu quoque in ubi conservarent.” [roughly: “that they had been accustomed to come over here from the market town of Rohoncz belonging to Vas county, and for the most part still actually keep their wives, servants and children there”; OCR “in ubi” garbled.]
     The increase of the Jewry in Zala county is shown by the following compilation:
                    In the year 1728: 19 heads of family in 7 villages.
                    In the year 1735: 22 heads of family in 7 villages.
                    In the year 174:i [OCR; year unclear]: 35 heads of family in 15 villages.
                    In the year 174ű [OCR; year unclear]: 90 heads of family in 27 villages.

    We see an extraordinary growth in the mid-40s; by then the later populous Jewish communities are already taking shape:

                        Zalaegerszeg       9 families,
                        Keszthely          9 families,
                        Zalaszentgrót      9 families,
                        Sümeg              8 families,
                        Nagykanizsa        7 families,
                        Tapolca            7 families.

     As we can see, the greater part of the families concentrated in the more populous market towns of the county, where there was opportunity to carry on trade in open, permanent shops. In these places they could achieve a larger turnover on the occasion of the national fairs held several times a year. Even in the 40s there are still [nagy- … text continues on next page]

30

PDF viewer page 33

Source scan
heads of families from Kanizsa and Tapolca, whose wives and children had remained behind in Rohonc. The overwhelming majority of the county's Jewry continued to originate from there. However, in Sümeg, in the northern part of the county, we encounter family names that point to origins elsewhere, such as Herczei Lakornpag (Lakompak) [OCR: “Lakornpag”] and Volfg~1llgus Morvai (from Moravia) [OCR garbled; possibly “Volfgangus”].
      In Somogy County the permanent settlement of Jews began late and slowly. In 1725 only two families were found in the county: one in Marcali under the protection of the Széchenyis, and another in Kaposvár under the protection of the Esterházys. In 1728 only one family is mentioned in the entire county, and that one was not a permanent resident either: he traded in Szigetvár, while his family was [OCR: “{~l.”] in Rohonc. The situation was the same in 1737, with the difference that the single Jew was then found in Marcali. He stayed there accompanied by a servant, while his wife, four children and their tutor had remained in Rohonc under the protection of his hereditary lord, Count Lajos Batthyány. At his place of residence the head of the family himself was under the jurisdiction of Count Zsigmond Széchenyi, to whom he paid 12 forints annually, while he paid his hereditary lord II [OCR; possibly 11] forints. He has a shop at his place of residence and sells cloth, linen, paper and other goods.
      In the report attached to the 17::n [OCR; year unclear] census, Somogy County states that the county has no permanent Jewish resident: “ne unum quidem, qui stabiliter hic permarieret reteriri posuissemus” [Latin as in OCR: “we could not find even one who stayed here permanently”]. However, Jews bring in various goods from the neighbouring counties and peddle them. The report adds that the above-mentioned Isaacus Léb had recently come from Rohonc. The itinerant traders mentioned in this report may have been the origin of the members of the GO [OCR; possibly 60] Jewish families that, nine years later, the 174G [OCR; possibly 1746] census already mentions as hospites in 38 villages. Their distribution was as follows:

                          in 20 villages one family,
                          in 18 villages two families,
                          in 2 villages four families.

      Four families lived in Balatonkeresztúr and in the village of Büssü.
      Baranya was one of the counties that did not admit Jews, and for this reason the county does not appear in the conscriptions at all. On only one occasion, in 1727, did it report to the Lieutenancy Council that the Jew Izsák lived in Siklós [OCR: “Siklósori”]. He was, incidentally [OCR: “leülönben”], a resident of Rohonc and only had a storehouse here, from which he carried his goods around the countryside for sale. He did not cultivate land and bore no public burdens; he paid only his landlord.
      The wide dispersal of the Rohonc Jews is attested by Tolna County, whose resettlement began only in the 1720s: in 1728 Jews are mentioned there in 3 villages. The earliest of them, Ábraham Sámuel, had come ten years before from Rohonc with his two married brothers to Pincehely, a market town of the Esterházy estate. The families of all three had remained behind at their original place of residence. In Pincehely they rent a house and a shop, and they have an assistant and an apprentice with them. They also keep a ritual slaughterer (shochet). Their stock of goods is estimated at one thousand forints. They trade in textiles, spices and salt. We encounter the name of Ábraham Sámuel again in Tolna County's 1735 conscription, but by then his wife and three adult sons also lived there with him. He still had his shop and fulfilled his seigneurial (urbarial) obligations towards his hereditary lord, Count Batthyány, and towards Prince Pál Antal Esterházy, in whose manor house and under whose jurisdiction he was then living.

                                                                                  31

PDF viewer page 34

Source scan
       In the southern half of Transdanubia we have thus seen the migration of the Jews of Rohonc and Szalonak. In the northern and north-eastern part of Transdanubia, by contrast, we already encounter large numbers of immigrants from Moravian communities, alongside Jews who came from the Esterházys' villages in Sopron County and the Pálffys' villages in Pozsony County. It is also characteristic of this part of Transdanubia that in almost every county a more populous Jewish centre gradually develops, in which Jewish communal life becomes possible. This was made possible by aristocratic protection. In return for appropriate material compensation, the lords gave their consent and set down the concessions for the Jewish communities in a separate charter of privileges, while requiring of them the stipulated services in money and in kind. Among these communities are Tata in Komárom County, Pápa in Veszprém, Lovasberény in Fejér, Paks in Tolna, and finally Óbuda and Zsámbék in Pest County.
       The settlement of Jews in all these Transdanubian counties and their villages began only after the Rákóczi War of Independence had subsided. This is attested by those conscriptions in which conscientious enumerators also recorded how long each Jewish family had been living in the place. It is regrettable that not all the commissioned enumerators took this into account, and our knowledge of settlement history is therefore very incomplete.
       We have already discussed Csuz, the more populous Jewish community that arose in the part of Komárom County north of the Danube. The county's other populous congregation developed at Tata, in its Transdanubian part. According to the 1725 census, 19 Jewish families lived there. None of the heads of families had been born in the village. They arrived there in different years and from different places, so there was no simultaneous group settlement from a single place. The data of the conscription prove this. The oldest Jewish resident of the village had arrived from Stomfa thirteen years before the census. Apart from him, only two had lived there for six years and another two for four years; all the rest had immigrated during the twenties. Six came from Hungarian villages: two from Köpcsény and one each from Stomfa, Rajka, Oroszvár and Rohonc. Four were of Bohemian origin, and six came from Moravia. Of two, the conscription writes that they were from Gallia, that is, Alsace, from where part of Tata's inhabitants had incidentally also come. The first and only employee of the nascent community was the ritual slaughterer (shochet).
       Of later numerical data on the Jews of Tata, only the 1736 census is known. It shows a certain decrease in the number of families, counting only 15 families with ü5 [OCR; digit unclear] souls. By then Tata had passed into the possession of the Esterházys: József Esterházy had bought it in 1727 from the Austrian government councillor Krapf F. J.-Gáspár [OCR; name order across line break unclear]. The Esterházys promoted the settlement of Jews, whose number soon grew rapidly, and they formed a religious community. This is shown by the fact that they already had a cemetery in the forties. The oldest surviving tombstone dates from 1743; the deceased bore the name Auspitz and, according to the inscription, came from Nikolsburg. We also know of their synagogue in this period. The community's old charters of privilege from the lordship have not survived, but the known text of their privilege from Hl:38 [OCR; year unclear] goes back to the first half of the 18th century (Goldberger I.: Estcrházyuk szerződései a tatai zsidó hitközséggel [OCR; “The Esterházys' contracts with the Jewish community of Tata”]. IMIT Évkönyv. 191H [OCR; year unclear]. 228–242.).
       For Veszprém County we can publish only two conscriptions from the period under discussion, from the years 173G [OCR; 1736, per the next sentence] and 1745. In 173G-biU1a [OCR; 1736] Jews lived in 15 villages of the county, 44 families in total. Their distribution is as follows:

:32

PDF viewer page 35

Source scan
                  in five villages one family,
                  in seven villages two families,
                  in one village three families (Veszprém),
                  in two villages II [OCR; possibly 11] families (Pápa, Nagyvázsony).
      By origin, the heads of families are distributed as follows. Seventeen came from Hungarian villages on the estates of the Esterházys, Czirákys, Batthyánys and Pálffys. The localities from which they came are not named; these aristocratic families are mentioned only as their hereditary lords. Nineteen families are of Moravian origin and two of Bohemian origin; five cannot name their former lords. Surviving documents establish that Jews were living in Veszprém County as early as the end of the 17th century, before and during the Rákóczi War of Independence. “Jacob Hirschl von Pápa” is mentioned in 1698 (M. Zs. O. VI. no. 288). Izsák Hirschl appears in 1701 as the toll collector of Pápa; he was a native of Nagymarton (M. Zs. O. VI. nos. 391–92). We find these Hirschl brothers, Izsák and Jacob, named several times in the field book of Antal Esterházy, kuruc general, between 1706 and 1708 (M. Zs. O. II. 493–94.). Of the eleven families living in Pápa in 1736, five were of Hungarian origin: four had come there from Esterházy estates and one from a Pálffy estate. Four came from Moravia, and the origin of two is unknown. Pápa was an Esterházy estate, and nine of the Jewish families were Esterházy subjects; only two lived in the manor house of another noble family, the Szántóházys. Of the Jew mentioned first in the list we read: “Telonialis, vectigalis exactor” [“collector of tolls and duties”].
      In Nagyvázsony eleven families were counted in 1736 [OCR: “l73G”]. Five came from the estates of the Esterházys and Batthyánys [OCR: “Ratthyányak”], and six from Moravia; four of the latter were Dietrichstein subjects who had originally come from Nikolsburg. Two could not give the name of their Moravian lord. All of them reside on land belonging to the Zichy family. Of one head of family, who incidentally was a butcher, it is stated that he does not owe his lord any payment: “Qua aulicus Judaeus adeoque nihil solvit” [“as a court Jew, and therefore he pays nothing”]. The beginnings of a community are shown by the fact that they jointly employed a teacher. Of one head of family we read: “Instruetor puererum Hebraeorum” [OCR; “instructor of Hebrew boys”].
      According to the 1745 census, Jews were already living in 32 villages of Veszprém County. Most villages still had only one family; even in the town of Veszprém this was the case, and the occupation of that family's head was “coetor” [OCR; reading uncertain]. He probably kept a cookshop for the Jews who came into the town, as happened in other places too. Larger numbers of families lived in only three places: four in Várpalota, nine in Nagyvázsony and 19 in Pápa, where Bernardus Isaac rabbinus is listed last. In the counties extending over the Bakony Mountains, primarily Veszprém and also Fejér and Esztergom counties, potash boiling is mentioned as an occupation of the Jews (they are called ?inerariusok [OCR; first letter unclear]). In Veszprém County this provided a livelihood for the Jews of Tót Vázsony, Szent Gál and Öskü. In 1748 payment of the poll tax was demanded from 327 persons.
      In Fejér County in 1727, according to the conscription, only five families were enumerated, in three villages. One of the three villages was Lovasberény, where two families were found. Nine years later, in l7aG [OCR; 1736], their number had already risen to twenty. Of the families, 14 were of Moravian origin. They had gathered from various villages of the [OCR: “[t”] province, since they name the lords Lichtenstein, Dictrichstcin [OCR], Kaunicz and Szilke [OCR: “Szilkc”] as their hereditary lords. Two declared themselves to be of Slavonian origin

3 Oklevéltár VII.
                                                                              33

PDF viewer page 36

Source scan
. Three did not know their hereditary lord, and for one family the column for origin is blank. Lovasberény, which had been owned by General Heister and then by the Austrian Baron Fleischmann, was acquired in I730 [OCR; probably 1730] by Count József Cziráky. The 20 families, numbering 95 souls, lived under his protection and on his land (fundus).
      The published conscriptions supply authentic data for monographs on the individual Jewish communities of the more recent period, those founded in the course of the 18th century: when they arose and where their founders came from. In his history of the Lovasberény Jewish community, Sándor Neumann (A lovasberényi zsidó hitközség története [History of the Jewish community of Lovasberény]. Bpest, 1912.) writes that oral tradition places the founding of an organised community at the beginning of the 1700s. Yet according to the 1725 conscription only two families lived there at that time. Likewise, we can regard only as legend the report that the entire Jewish population of Lundenburg in Moravia “set out together with its leadership — with its rabbi — and moved to Hungary under banners”. The only basis for this legend can have been that seven of the Lovasberény families immigrated between I/27 and ~U [OCR; years unclear] from Lundenburg, which was a Lichtenstein estate. The development of the community is attested by the fact that, according to the 174G [OCR] census, the number of families had already reached thirty-one. By then the community's recognised Jewish congregation may already have held a seigneurial charter of privileges, the text of which survives only in a later privilege dating from 1798. The growth of Fejér County's Jewry is shown by the county's three published conscriptions:
                    In the year 1727: 5 families in 3 villages,
                    In the year 173f) [OCR]: 33 families in 10 villages,
                    In the year 174U [OCR]: 65 families in 18 villages.

     This last figure for the number of families was arrived at by calculation from the total of 322 souls, taking five persons per family as the basis. Of the 33 families in the 1736 census, the conscription gives origin data for twenty-nine: 2-1 [OCR; number unclear] Moravian, 4 Hungarian and one Polish.
     In Tolna County in 1728 the enumerators found five families in three villages (Pincehely, Dombóvár and Dunaföldvár). In Pincehely there were the three brothers from Rohonc already mentioned. In Dombóvár there was one family, which had already been leasing the manorial butcher's shop for seven years. In Dunaföldvár, on the abbey's estate, one family and one unmarried young man had been living for a year. The whole county was then still very sparsely populated. Since its liberation from Turkish rule, the region had been unable to recover from the devastation, and severe epidemics had reduced its population. Settlement of the uninhabited territory began only in the 1720s, when many Swabian and Franconian immigrants arrived. The Jews also moved in at this time. In 1735, 42 Jewish families numbering 181 souls were recorded in II [OCR; possibly 11] villages of the county. Their distribution in the county was as follows:

      in six villages one family,
      in one village two families (Gyönk),
      in two villages three families (Dombóvár, Pincehely),
      in one village eight families (Ozora, at that time the centre of the Esterházy estate in Tolna County),
      in one village twenty families (Paks).

 34

PDF viewer page 37

Source scan
      As we see, almost half of the county's Jewish families lived in Paks. All of them arrived in the village between 1728 and 1735. The 1720 census of taxpayers had found only 51 households there in total (Acsády). The village's noble landowners, the Nedeczky and Száraz families, worked to populate their market town, and they promoted the settlement of Jews. Of the twenty Jewish families of Paks, the 13 that could state their place of origin or the name of their hereditary lord were all of Moravian origin; seven of them even name the same lord, called Pruszkau, as their hereditary lord. It can also be observed among the immigrants that several left their Moravian homeland together: relatives, siblings, and fathers with their sons and sons-in-law came together. This can be established from the published names. Paks was already an organised community (communitas). This is shown by the list, which names the rabbi first and, last, the “pracceptor Communitatis .Iudaeorum Paksicnaiurn” [OCR; “teacher of the community of the Jews of Paks”], the teacher of the Jewish community of Paks.
      The data on Tolna County's Jewry according to the 174G [OCR] census are as follows:

             in eighteen villages, in total H5 [OCR; number unclear] families,
             in six villages, in total 1 family,
             in eight villages, in total 2 families,
             in one village, in total 3 families (Lengyel),
             in one village, in total 4 families (Hőgyész),
             in one village, in total l:i [OCR; number unclear] families (Bonyhád),
             in one village, in total 25 families (Paks).

      This census is the first to mention Jews in Bonyhád. In the national census of InO [OCR; year unclear] we read of this village: “No one has lived in it for four years” (Acsády: op. cit. 378.). This explains why the village of Bonyhád does not appear in the Jewish conscriptions of the twenties and thirties. The 1740 [OCR; as printed] census found 13 Jewish families in the village. In the second half of the 18th century Bonyhád developed into the most populous Jewish congregation of the county. In the sixties of that century they had a prayer house and a rabbi; yet even in InO [OCR; year unclear] they complained that, unlike “the other congregations of the house of Jacob”, they had no privilege from the lordship (Biichler [OCR; Büchler] Sándor: A bonyhádi hitközség múltjából [From the past of the Bonyhád community]. M Zs Sz. xn. [OCR; possibly XII] 1895. 414.).
      Since we intend to discuss the Jews of Győr castle together with those of the other Hungarian castles, we examine here only the villages of Győr County. In Győr County the Jewish conscriptions show the following data:
      The 1725 census lists 6 families in three villages. The 1735 census lists 12 families in 9 villages. By origin these 12 families divide as follows: three are from Hungary (two from Esterházy estates and one from a Pálffy estate), and the other nine from Moravian villages; six of the latter name Count Kaunicz as their hereditary lord.
      The 1743 census found 20 heads of families in nine villages. The largest number, four, lived in Felpéc; one had lived there for ten years and another for 7 years.
      The 1744 census mentions Jews in 13 villages; the number of families is not known. In Felpéc, however, it records six. We also learn that one family each had come into the county from Tata and from Nagymarton.
      The 1746 census likewise finds Jews in 1B [OCR; number unclear] villages. The number of heads of families is not given here either. In the census conducted by districts their number of souls is 120, which corresponds to roughly 24 families.

3*
                                                                                  35

PDF viewer page 38

Source scan
      It is striking that this county's Jewry showed no significant growth over the two decades, even though it lay close to the populous, Jewish-inhabited western border strip. The Jews here lived in difficult material circumstances, and because of their poverty the county itself requested remission of the extraordinary war tax. Izsák Hirschel, whom the county had commissioned to collect the 150-forint war tax levied on the Jews, wrote of the great misery of certain families. He paints a sad picture of the poverty of Marcus Donát, living in Hédervár, and Jakab )10yses [OCR; probably Moyses], living in the village of Öttevény, and of the great privations of these families with many children. In the intervals between the various censuses, individual families changed their place of residence, which shows that they hoped in this way to find a better livelihood. Only in Felpéc and Gyömörő had they already settled permanently.
      No censuses of Esztergom County's Jewry survive from the twenties. The oldest conscription we can publish dates from 1735. Jews lived in four places in the county: 12 families with 47 souls. Among these places was Esztergom Víziváros, with 2 families. No Jews lived in Esztergom itself, because the town's charter of privileges of 1711 stipulated, among other things, that heretics of whatever denomination “shall not be admitted as residents into this royal free city”. The largest number of Jews lived in Bátorkeszi, in the part of the county north of the Danube: six families on the estate of Miklós Pálffy. Four of these families were of Moravian origin, coming from Ungarisch Brod, an estate of Count Kaunicz. One pair of brothers, Jacobus and David Hollender, however, could not name their hereditary lord. Four of the families were peddlers (mercator in dorso Cum minutiis [“trader carrying small wares on his back”]). In Bajna lived a well-to-do, numerous Jewish family, which boiled potash together with two partners living in Tata. It paid its lord, Baron Mihály Sándor, 100 forints a year, but its three-year contract had expired. Two teachers also appear in the lists of the families' household members.


                 9. The Jewry of the counties between the Danube and the Tisza [OCR: “Dnna-'l'i8za”]

      The first Jewish conscription of the united counties of Pest-Pilis and Solt, dating from 1725, lists 24 families in eight villages. By number the families were distributed as follows: one family in each of five villages, three in one village (Vörösvár), four in one village (Aszód) and 12 in one village (Óbuda). This census did not satisfy the Lieutenancy Council, which found the number of Jews listed far too small. The county argued that the census could not be carried out accurately because many of the Jews were away.
     The 1727 census again records Jews in only eight villages. Apart from Óbuda, Aszód, Vác and Vörösvár, however, these were in other villages of the county, not where they had been enumerated in 1725. Their distribution according to the 1727 conscription:
                       in three villages 1 family,
                       in two villages 3 families (Vác, Sáp),
                       in one village 6 families (Aszód),
                       in one village IG [OCR; number unclear] families (Zsámbék),
                       in one village 22 families (Óbuda).

     From the standpoint of settlement history, this census is notable because for almost every family it states how long the family had been living in the place. The county's oldest Jewish inhabitants were those who had arrived in Vörösvár 20 years before 1727 and in Vác IS [OCR; number unclear] years earlier. Of the 22 heads of families in Óbuda, not one had been born in the village. Its first Jewish inhabitant, in 1712,

36

PDF viewer page 39

Source scan
arrived there 15 years earlier; two had lived there for 14 years and four for 12 years. All the others settled in Óbuda during the twenties. This conscription does not yet state where the settlers came from; it notes only of Marcus Mandel: “recenter advems-sct ex Vörösvár” [OCR; “had recently arrived from Vörösvár”]. According to the census, the Jews of Óbuda all still lived in rented dwellings and paid their landlords a considerable sum for the hospitium. They enjoyed the protection of the widow of Count Péter Zichy and her permission to reside there, for which they owed an annual joint [OCR: “közbi”] sum of 3 é138 [OCR; amount unclear] forints.
      Zsámbék is the only village all of whose Jewish inhabitants, according to the conscription, came at the same time and probably as a group, settled there by Countess Péter Zichy. At the time of the national census of taxpayers the village of Zsámbék had 4~ [OCR; second digit illegible] households; by 1720, when a new census was taken, their number had risen to I08 [OCR; probably 108] (Acsády). This was owing to the lordship's settlement initiatives. At the end of the 1727 census for the Jews of Zsámbék, after the list of families, we find the following remark: “Praeseripsi Sambekiensis Judaei omncs hoc anno ad praeliba.tum oppid um advoncrunt, in gremium Comitatus vel Communi-tatis ad pJ'aescns nihil solverunt. sed Dominio erga aceordarn Excellcrrtissimae nimirum Dominae Comitissae Petra Zichianae soh-unt annue in toto fl. 120.” [Latin as in OCR; approximately: “All the Jews of Zsámbék listed above came to the said town this year; they have so far paid nothing into the treasury of the County or of the Community, but pay the Lordship, by agreement with the Most Excellent Lady Countess Péter Zichy, 120 florins a year in total.”] Paying taxes jointly as a single sum bound the Jews of Zsámbék together early and united them into a community.
      According to the 1737 census, Jews lived in II [OCR; possibly 11] villages of the county (not counting the royal free city of Buda), distributed as follows:
            in 4 villages                                       1 family,
            in 3 villages (Domony, Sáp, Tétény)                 3 families,
            in 1 village (Aszód)                                6 families,
            in 1 village (Isaszeg)                              7 families,
            in 1 village (Zsámbók)                             22 families,
            in 1 village (Óbuda)                               43 families.

      The number of Jewish families in Óbuda almost doubled in ten years. This is partly attributable to the city of Buda obstructing every new Jewish immigration. Whereas Pest County's 1727 conscription records for individual families how long they had been living in the place, the 1737 census, as if supplementing it, gives the place of origin of the heads of families. According to the census, all the heads of families in Óbuda were newcomers, with the sole exception of Marcus Simon, who says of himself: “Vetero Budae natus” [“born in Old Buda”]. Seven had moved there from Hungarian villages (Stornfa [OCR; probably Stomfa], Holics, Nagymarton, two from villages in Pozsony County and one from a village in Bars County). Many had immigrated from Moravia, chiefly from Dietrichstein and Lichtenstein estates. Five came from Bohemia and four from Poland. The contract concluded by the Óbuda congregation, already organised as a community (“gesambte Juden-Gemeinde”), with Countess Péter Zichy, to whom the Óbuda estate belonged, dates from precisely 1737, the year of this census. Under this contract she leased to them the plot on which the synagogue stands, together with the two properties next to it, as well as a site for a cemetery. The Jewish community confirmed this contract with its own seal: “mit dern gemeinde petschaft corrobirt” [German as in OCR; “confirmed with the community seal”] (M. Zs. O. III. no. 462). The census lists five employees of the community: Schachter, Cantor, Subjudex, Educillator Judaeorum, and the Ludi Magister seu Praeceptor Judaicus. From the 1727 census of the Jews of Zsámbék

we learned that they all came to this village at the same time. The 1737 census shows that they came from Bohemian and Moravian villages, that several were members of a single family, and that six came from the estate of the same Moravian lord, Priskovsky.
     The 1746 census, which, as already mentioned, was ordered for the purpose of tax assessment, names 32 villages in Pest County where Jews lived: 136 families and 529 persons in total. Their distribution:
                        in 15 villages                 1 family,
                        in 6 villages                  2 families,
