                        six to Tétény (Theting),
                        six to Aszód (Oshart),
                        three to Zsámbék (Schambok).

       Within the county, the Jews settled mostly in the villages of the Pilis and Vác districts. Fewer settled in the Kecskemét district, and fewest of all in the more southerly Solt district. In the Danube–Tisza region they moved southwards only slowly. It may even be supposed that the families enumerated in the two villages of the Solt district near Kalocsa, Bátya and Hajós, came not from the north but across from Transdanubia. As is well known, after the end of Turkish rule the Danube–Tisza region returned to the mother country as a completely uninhabited, deserted territory. The sparsely populated region did not attract the Jews, who were mostly engaged in trade, to settle there. Moreover, the counties between the Danube and the Tisza then had no connection to the east with any territories inhabited by Jews. Immigrants could only come down from the north-west, from Nógrád, or come from the Transdanubian parts.
      In the extensive Bács County, in the southern part of the Danube–Tisza region, we likewise find that, apart from the Pétervárad entrenchment, the censuses mention a few Jewish families only in Baja. The 1727 census records two leaseholders (árendátor), and in 1728 too there were only two families there, one of which had come from Rohonc. In 1736 six families with 37 persons were enumerated in the market town. Three came from a Batthyány estate and the other three from the Moravian Lichtenstein estate. At their present place of residence all six were subjects of Lady Lebinna, who resided in Vienna. By occupation they included a “CResarius Liferaní.” [OCR; probably “Caesarius Liferant”, imperial supplier], two brandy distillers, two merchants and a ritual slaughterer (shochet). This Jewish congregation of Baja developed slowly, but in the second half of the 18th century it became the leading community of the Bácska. In 1744, too, only seven heads of families were enumerated, two of whom already owned their own houses. These were already permanent

38

PDF viewer page 41

Source scan
chose Baja as their place of residence. In 1745, again only six families were found there.
Jews were surely already living scattered in the county, for in the covering letter of the 172R [OCR: year unclear, probably 1728] conscription they complain to the Lieutenancy Council, which had not been satisfied by the previously submitted Jewish conscription, that the Jews live in villages far apart from one another, which makes their enumeration difficult.

10. The Jewry of the Fortresses and Towns

Having reviewed the conscriptions of the Jewry of the counties, we must also discuss the conscriptions made in the fortresses and towns of the country.
Of the fortresses, Győr, Komárom, Esztergom, Buda, Nagyvárad, Arad and Pétervárad are relevant here. The beginnings of the Jewry of these fortresses can be traced back partly to the time of the sixteen-year war of liberation, and then to the Rákóczi War of Independence and the Turkish wars of the first half of the 18th century. At this time the Jewish army contractors living in Vienna, Oppenheimer Sámuel, Wertheirner [OCR; probably Wertheimer] Simson, Lehmann Herz and the others, played an important role in equipping and provisioning the troops stationed in the fortresses. Much information about them can be found in the work of Jrfax Grumoald [OCR garbled; probably Max Grunwald]: Samuel Oppenheimer und sein Kreis (Wien-Leipzig, ] 913. [OCR; probably 1913]). The first Jews came to the fortresses as agents of these Vienna-based contractors, enjoying the protection of the chamber administrators and fortress captains. They obtained the right of settlement in the fortresses or in the Váralja [area below the castle; OCR: 'il. V áralja']. Once the wartime had passed, the chamber and military administration confined itself to a narrower sphere. The towns that had grown up around the fortresses, and the counties, then sought to extend their jurisdiction to the Jews living there. As a result, conflicts arose between the chamber and military authorities and the civilian population.
We see this in Győr, where the 1725 conscription finds Hirschl Izsák with his whole family under the protection of the fortress commander. He boasted that by delivering a large quantity of grain he had saved the fortress of Győr from famine when it was besieged by Rákóczi's troops. He later became keeper of the military depots of Győr, Székesfehérvár and Veszprém. In one of his petitions, dated 1725, he signs himself: "Isaac Hirschl Judaeus jaurinensis caesareus". However, the Jews living in Győr do not appear in the 1735 conscription, because the deputy commander of the fortress did not permit the county to enumerate them. The county complained to the ruler, and the complaint succeeded, for in later years, in the conscriptions of 1743, '44, '45 and '46, we again find the Jewry of Győr. Their number is always small, 5-6 families. For the most part they consist of relatives of Hirschl Izsák. From the 1743 conscription we learn of Hirschl: "tenet dornum [OCR; probably domum] titulo hypotecae Kiralyianam in interiori civitate sive praesidio Jauriensi". The lí45 [OCR; probably 1745] conscription reports that he trades in gold and silver and supplies uniforms and horses to the military. His widowed daughter, for her part: "tabernam Judaeis extraneis servans". Hirschl Izsák still appears in the conscriptions of 1746 and 1748. His financial situation may have deteriorated: he fell into the ranks of those unable to pay tax and could no longer even keep a servant.
In Komárom the 1nR [OCR: year unclear, probably 1728] conscription mentions one Jewish head of family with two married relatives. A document from 1727 (M. Zs. O. III. no. 311 [OCR: 'Ill.']) relates that the commander of the Komárom garrison refuses to permit two Jews, Simon Leb and

39

PDF viewer page 42

Source scan
his relative, Daniel Aron, to exercise among the soldiery the right of trade they had purchased from the town. According to the 1738 conscription, Simon Leb, originating from Kabold, now lives with two relatives, Aron and Wolff Daniel, in the curia of the deputy commander of the fortress. He pays the deputy commander 300 forints annually, and pays the fortress commander two hundred for protection. So that he may also enjoy the protection of the Pálffy family, he also pays [OCR: 'juttat'] the Jewry of Pozsony six forints a year. This is a contribution to the Sohuztgeld [OCR; probably Schutzgeld, protection money] that the Jews of Pozsony had to pay into their landlord's treasury.
In Esztergom, liberated from Turkish rule, there lived Jews whom the fortress captain had admitted (M. Zs. O. II. nos. 297-98). When the fortress passed into the possession of the Archbishop of Esztergom, the Jews had to leave. The town of Esztergom, citing its charter of privileges mentioned above, did not admit them. Nevertheless, according to the 173G [OCR; probably 1736] conscription, a Jew of Moravian origin was found with his son-in-law in the Víziváros of Esztergom. They have a permanent shop there and belong to the archbishop's jurisdiction.
No Jew was permitted to live in the fortress of Buda after its liberation from Turkish rule. In the Víziváros of Buda, however, Jews were able to open shops under chamber and military protection from lG89 [OCR; probably 1689] onward. They received permission for this so that they could supply the common people and the soldiery with useful goods and cheap foodstuffs. The settlement history of the Jews of Buda is treated in depth by Büchler Sándor [OCR: 'Büehler'] in his work A zsidók története Budapesten [The History of the Jews in Budapest] (Bp., HlOl. [OCR; probably 1901]). The first Jew to obtain the right of settlement was Fe it [OCR; probably Feit] Hirschl, a relative of Hirschl Izsák of Győr. Mentioned alongside him is Jakab ~ áthán [OCR; probably Náthán], who was probably of Moravian origin. These two families were soon followed by others, including those bearing the names Bachrach and Bürgl from Germany. 1690 Ma már egy szehát [OCR garbled; probably 'In 1690 they already'] maintained a room for the purpose of communal worship. In 1703 Buda received its privilege as a free royal town, which also contained the clause: "Authoritatis solius erit Magistratus ... Judaeos ct Zingaros adrnitterc vel non ... admittere ...". From then on the town began a struggle to force the Jews out. When the town was called upon in 1743 to conscribe the Jews, it wrote in its covering letter to the Lieutenancy Council: "From the first years since the town's privileges were restored, they have constantly endeavoured, pro gloria dei et publico bono, to rid themselves of the Jews". For decades the Jewry of Buda had to wage a constant struggle for its existence and survival. Büchler, who describes these events in detail, rightly states: "In the history of the Buda community there are only two things that command admiration: the boldness with which it was founded, and the tenacity with which it was able to survive for sixty years". This was due solely to the chamber and military leadership, aided by the influential Viennese court Jews.
Whenever the Buda authorities were asked to conscribe the Jews, they insisted in their submissions that the Jews should not be conscribed but expelled. Three conscriptions of the Buda Jews survive from this period: those of 1725, l735 [OCR; 1735] and finally 1745. Over these two decades the number of heads of family shows a decrease rather than an increase. In 1725 there are thirty-six heads of family there; in '35 thirty-two are recorded; in '45 thirty-one are counted. They could not acquire real estate and lived in rented dwellings. According to the 1725 conscription, four families have an open shop. The conscription always adds: "hat ohne Erlaubnusz des Raths ein offenes Gewölb". Among the heads of family listed we find the representative of Wertheirner [OCR; probably Wertheimer], the Viennese agent: "ist von Wertheirner von Wien sein bestölter". Members of the Viennese Leyderstorffer and Schlesinger families also appear in the conscription. Several supply the garrison with uniforms, sheets, straw

40

PDF viewer page 43

Source scan
sacks and candles. Bürgl Joachim, a protégé of the Court War Council, supplies it with scrap iron. One family lives from its own means, but the community also has its poor. The congregation has a rabbi, a schoolmaster and a slaughterer. An inn serves Jews arriving from elsewhere.
The 1735 conscription gives the places of origin of the 32 families. Seven state that they were born here, and five that they are of Hungarian origin, coming from the Zichy, Esterházy and Pálffy estates and from the towns of Győr and Kornárom [OCR; Komárom]. Among them are six Poles, six Moravians, six from the German Empire, one from Vienna and one from Silesia. This conscription also records whose jurisdiction each family falls under. Nine are under the town's jurisdiction, 15 enjoy chamber protection and 7 military protection. The rabbi has both chamber and military protection; however, all of them live on town plots. Several related families keep an open shop jointly. Here too the conscription does not fail to note beside every Jewish shop: "in damnum et pracjudicium [OCR; praejudicium] hujatum mercatorum civiliurrr [OCR; civilium]". The organized community has six employees. Though there are some well-to-do members, the poor are also present ("mendicus, depauporatus, nullum habet quaestum"), and there are also several peddlers and even a shepherd among them.
The third published conscription of the Buda Jews dates from 1745, the year before their expulsion from the town. After Maria Theresa came to the throne, the leaders of Buda could press with hope of success for the fulfilment of their long-standing wish. On 8 July 1746 a royal decree was proclaimed in the town calling on the 3l [OCR; probably 31] Jewish families of Buda to leave within two months. Several of them had been born in the town or had lived there for several decades and regarded it as their permanent home.
Of the heads of family named in the 1745 list, five also appear in the 1725 conscription and fourteen in the 1735 conscription. Among them are descendants of the first settlers of more than half a century earlier (Natl [OCR; reading uncertain], Hirschl, Bachrach etc.). Four have an open shop, and three heads of family have been absent for some time. The Judex Judaeorum still heads the community, but no rabbi is mentioned; the ludi magister and the subjudex live in the synagogue house. They clung to their place of residence even under the most difficult conditions. On royal command, the Hungarian Chancellery instructed the Chamber and the War Council, which had until then protected the Jews living there, not to hinder the town of Buda in removing them. The ordered expulsion was indeed completed by 8 September 1746. The Jewry of Buda scattered. Several took refuge in neighbouring Óbuda, hoping for permission to return to their old home. Others swelled the Jewish communities of Tata, Aszód and Pozsony, and a few settled on the estates of landlords in Pest County. Some also went abroad.
Thus, for the fourth time over the centuries, the Jewish congregation of Buda was destroyed.
The first conscriptions of the Jews settled at Nagyvárad and Arad, in the vicinity of two fortresses on the eastern edge of the Great Plain, were made in 1727. The seven Jewish families conscribed at Nagyvárad in 1727 lived in different places. Four lived in Várad; one of these stayed in the forest as the bishop's leaseholder (árendátor). One lived at Szőlős on a treasury estate. One lived among the nationality soldiers [nemzetiségi katonák] in the house of an army officer. The seventh had a shop below the fortress under the protection of the fortress commander. The 1736 conscription found 18 families at Nagyvárad and records of them: "Omnes sunt sub jurisdictione Carnerali [OCR; Camerali] et Protectione Commendantis Varadi-

41

PDF viewer page 44

Source scan
ensis." The origin of two heads of family is unknown; eleven came from Moravia, two from Poland, likewise two from the German Empire, and finally one from the estate of the Bishop of Nyitra. The congregation has a teacher and a cantor. Together they pay the Szepes Chamber a toleration tax of fifty forints annually, and the same amount to the commander of Várad for protection, supplemented by spices worth 30 forints. At Nagyvárad, too, we hear the county complain of how strongly the fortress commander supports the wandering (vagabundus) Jews at fairs. He has already made it possible for them to build small houses and even to elect a judge. Yet they do not contribute to the public burdens, because they stand under military protection (M. Zs. O. III. no. 496).
The situation is similar in Arad. As early as 1717 the commander-in-chief of the Maros region took two Jews in the suburb of Arad under his protection and patronage as "Hausz und Hof Juden". One of them is Elias Izsák, who, as we learn from a later conscription, came from a Batthyány estate (M. Zs. O. III. no. 91). The 1727 conscription, which covers Arad and Zaránd counties together, lists six families in the chamber town of Arad, two at Orovil Szállás, and likewise two in Zarand County. One family lives at Boros Jenő and one at Zaránd. In the 1735 conscription 19 families live in Arad and its associated settlements. Their distribution by origin: from Hungary ~ [OCR: number illegible] (from the Batthyány, Esterházy and Czobor estates), from Poland 7, from Moravia likewise 7, from Germany two. Jointly they keep a teacher, whose place of origin is not given. Differences of wealth and legal status can be seen among those conscribed. The first six heads of family in the list live in their own houses, on either treasury or town plots. These six together pay the Chamber ] 00 [OCR; probably 100] forints in toleration tax annually and the commander of the fortress of Arad 96 forints. The next eight lived in humbler circumstances. Among them are the rabbi, named Israel Schachter, Isac Mussicus, and a poor man dependent on charity. The last five heads of family listed live in various military settlements belonging to the fortress of Arad: one in the Rácváros, one at a place called Babunczky Gaj, one at Glogovác and two at Mondorlak. Three of the families living among the soldiery have their own houses, and they pay the captain of the nationality soldiery. The 1745 conscription is incomplete, as it names only six heads of family obliged to pay the extraordinary tax, whereas the 174(j [OCR; probably 1746] conscription records 19 heads of family with 72 souls. The judge of the Jewish community in 174u [OCR; probably 1746] is the same Elias Izsák who had already been the fortress commander's house and court Jew in 1717.
Jews seeking to settle in Hungary reached on their wanderings Pétervárad, then the southernmost fortress of the country. They were not permitted to settle in the fortress itself, any more than the Serbs, Croats and Armenians were. Instead they established themselves in the Pétervárad entrenchments opposite the fortress, on the left bank of the Danube, "in fossato Petrovaradiensi". A military map from 17lu [OCR: year unclear] marks these settlements with the names "Rait.zen-Stacdtl" [OCR; probably Raitzen-Staedtl] and "Croaten-Dörfl".
The first Pétervárad conscription we publish dates from 1727. It merely lists the names of the six Jews living there and gives no other data about them. The Lieutenancy Council objected to this, so in ] 72H [OCR; probably 1728] the county's district magistrate (szolgabíró) and assessor prepared a new table, this time with 21 columns, recording the data of twelve heads of family. The first head of family listed is Marcus Filip, who, we read, had come there 35 years earlier from Stemfa in Pozsony County. Years later three more came from there: two from Levard, a village near Stemfa, and one from Buda. Thus seven of the 12 heads of family were of Hungarian origin. Three

42

PDF viewer page 45

Source scan
came from Moravia, two of them from Nikolsburg and one from Ungarisch Brod, and one from Poland. Finally, for one, the village of Ramzaicz [OCR: '(n'; reading uncertain] is given as place of origin. Three heads of family already have their own houses; the rest are cottars (zsellér). Their occupations are not indicated.
The 1736 conscription lists 15 heads of family with 56 souls. Their distribution by origin: five from Hungary, who had been subjects of the Pálffy family; six Moravians, 3 Poles and one Bohemian. All are under the jurisdiction of the Court Chamber. In this list too, as in the conscriptions of 1727 and 1728, the first name given is that of Marx (Marcus) Philip, the oldest-established and wealthiest Jew. Among the occupations of the heads of family we find three carters, three tailors and four dealers in second-hand goods. The Jewish colony has a teacher and a slaughterer.
The 1744 conscription shows the congregation growing. The number of heads of family is twenty-seven [OCR: 'Huszonhé.']. This growth can be connected with the fact that after the unsuccessful Turkish war of 173ü-:39 [OCR; probably 1737-39], the Jews who had settled in Belgrade under Austrian rule left the city. Seventeen of the heads of family own houses. The organized Jewish community is headed by Joseph Kohn, judex. Its employees are the rabbi, the teacher and the slaughterer. The 1745 conscription lists the 19 heads of family obliged to pay the six-forint extraordinary war tax. Among them is Ábraham "Buch Hintr" [OCR; probably 'Buchbinder'], a bookbinder who may also have been a bookseller, and a "Felcserer" [barber-surgeon]. In 1748 the king raised the rapidly growing community to the rank of free royal town under the name Újvidék, Ncoplanta [OCR; Neoplanta]. The Jews thus passed from chamber authority to the town's jurisdiction, and they soon felt the disadvantages of this change (Radó Imre and Mayor Jozse] [OCR; probably Mayer József]: A novisadi zsidók története [History of the Jews of Novisad]. Novisad, 1930.). In Újvidék, Hungarian, Moravian, Bohemian and Polish immigrants met, that is, Jews from all the regions that peopled the then still thinly inhabited parts of the country. The Pétervárad conscriptions mention no Sephardic Jews of Balkan origin, apart from one employee of Belgrade origin (1728). Only at Sivácz, in Bács County, do we find Szamuel Niszim, born in Belgrade, who lives there with his family in his own house and deals in Turkish goods in his shop.
The relationship of the Jews to the towns must be treated separately. A review of the towns' reports to the Lieutenancy Council on the conscription of Jews shows that the two centuries since the disaster of Mohács had brought no change in the attitude of the urban burghers toward the Jews. The towns of the western part of the country, which had not come under Turkish rule, continued constantly to invoke against the Jews the royal privilege they had obtained in the years after Mohács, under which they were not obliged to admit Jews: "ius de non tolerando Judaeos" (Pozsony, Sopron, Nagyszombat). After the wars of liberation, the more populous places in the newly acquired territories joined the ranks of the free royal towns in the course of the 18th century. These towns exercised the right granted by their charters to decide for themselves whether or not to admit Jews. Every time the Lieutenancy Council ordered a nationwide conscription of Jews, it also called on the towns to conscribe the Jews living among them. The towns then hastened to report that, under their privileges and statutes, Jews could not settle permanently, but could at most stay there during the national fairs. Since their negative replies are mostly identical in wording, we did not consider it necessary to publish them in our edition. As a sample, only the first, 1725

43

PDF viewer page 46

Source scan
conscription is represented: we present the text of the negative replies submitted on that occasion. For the other conscriptions we give only a list of the towns whose negative submissions have survived.
Nevertheless, one, or sometimes two or three, families did gain entry to certain towns. In Bártfa in 1738-46 the town's leaseholder (arendator) is a Polish Jew who pays more than 1000 Rhenish florins in rent annually. In the town of Modor in 1725-36 a merchant of Moravian origin lives with his son-in-law. In 1727 the only Jew of Szatmárnémeti ran the brewery and paid an annual lease of 400 forints. In InG [OCR; probably 1746] three related Jewish families were found here; they lived in Németi. In 17:37 [OCR; probably 1737] at Szentgyörgy in Pozsony County there was a Jewish shopkeeper from Pozsony who paid the town 200 forints a year. Szentgyörgy and Baziri [OCR; probably Bazin] did not tolerate Jews, yet Jews did find admission in the curiae of the nobles living there. In Nyitra, the county with the largest Jewish population in the country, the 1725 conscription already names Jewish inhabitants in Szakolca, its only free royal town. They came from the neighbouring Moravian villages of Göding and Strassnitz, and people from other Moravian villages also came in there [OCR: 'Od;Legyéb']. They paid the town for permission to trade. According to the 1736 conscription, Szakolca had only seven permanent Jewish residents, all of Moravian origin. Three of them had lived there since 1725; four sold their goods in rented shop premises. They jointly keep a teacher. According to the 1747 conscription they paid the two-forint poll tax for 63 persons.
The Jewry of the town of Trencsén enjoyed a special position. As early as 1702 the Court Chamber instructed the town not to hinder its Jewish inhabitants in practising their religious ceremonies. In its rescript the Chamber referred to the old privilege the Jews had received from the ruler: "Incolae .Iudaei jam a longaevo tempore ex Privilegiis sibi il Sacratiesima Caesarea Rcgiaquc Majestate clementer concessis, solitas suas Ceremonias pracea tionum scilicct nuptiarum, circumcisionis et similium in ... Civitate Regia Trcnohinicnsi exerceant" [OCR Latin reproduced as read; 'pracea tionum' probably precationum] (M. Zs. O. II. no. 310). It thus permitted their ceremonies, but required them to pay the same taxes and portions as the town's other burghers. The religious ceremonies mentioned require the presence of at least 10 adult men. Thus, probably, Jews were to be found in such numbers in the town and its surroundings at the beginning of the 18th century. During the Kuruc wars, however, their numbers fell, and only with the return of peace did they begin to settle again in Trencsén. The 1725 conscription lists 12 heads of family, of whom 10 are incorporated (incorporatus) and two are not (non incorporatus). This is the first time we encounter this legal distinction among the Jewry. In 17:3ö [OCR; probably 1736] the names of 15 incorporated and four non-incorporated heads of family are given. The order of the names also indicates a ranking, the latter being placed at the end of the list. They own no houses and rent their dwellings. Eleven of the 19 heads of family have a permanent shop. This is strikingly many, since in the most populous Jewish communities, Pozsony has only three Jews with shops and Kismarton only ten. Only Rohonc, with twelve, and Vágújhely, with fifteen, have more Jews with open shops. These merchants supplied goods not only to their own place of residence but also to the large number of Jews peddling in the countryside. They brought the goods from Moravia. All the heads of family came from there, and all but three from Ungarisch Brod. This permanent connection with Ungarisch Brod led them to pay a considerable sum annually to the Jewish community there. In this way they satisfied their hereditary lord, Count Kaunicz, and contributed to the salary of the local rabbi, whose authority also extended to Trencsén. The Jewish

44

PDF viewer page 47

Source scan
community of Ungarisch Brod regarded the Trencsén congregation, to some extent, as its branch community. It also required them always to elect their leaders in Ungarisch Brod and to gather there for the autumn High Holidays (A. Frank! [OCR; probably Frankl] Grün: Geschichte der Juden in Ungarisch Brod. Wien, 1905. p. 20).
According to the 1744 conscription, the number of taxpaying heads of family in Trencsén is twenty. Different sums are demanded of them, because many of them are poor and cannot pay. When a more precise survey was made in 174ü [OCR: year unclear, probably 1746], 32 families were counted. This is a relatively high proportion, since in 1720 the town of Trencsén had only 9 noble and 221 burgher households in all.

*

Reviewing the nationwide Jewish conscriptions of the second quarter of the 18th century, we can accept Acsády's judgement that they were very valuable works from the statistical, contemporary-historical and cultural points of view alike (op. cit. p. 488). Complete statistical accuracy could not be achieved under the conditions of the time, and many were omitted from the survey, as also happened with the serf conscriptions of 1715 and 1720. Counting the Jews was made difficult because in many places they lived widely scattered, in villages far apart, often with only a single family in a village. The conscription was not carried out simultaneously throughout the country, so it was possible to evade the conscribers by moving to other counties. The landlords and their officials disliked these operations and therefore gave inadequate support to carrying out the conscriptions.
On the basis of the conscriptions of the 1730s, Acsády put the Jewish population at 11,ü21 [OCR; probably 11,621, consistent with the total of 12,907 below]. This total can be supplemented with county data that have come to light since, even if they are not from the same years.

County               Year              Souls
Máramaros County     1735              371 souls,
Ung County           1189 [OCR; year unclear]   lG5 [OCR; 165, per the total] souls,
Gömör County         174G [OCR; prob. 1746]     24 souls,
Nógrád County        1746              159 souls,
Szabolcs County      1747              221 souls,
Szatmár County       1746              346 souls.
                                       --------
                     Total:            1286 souls.

This raises the figure given above to 12907. According to the summary of the 174G [OCR; probably 1746] conscription, the two-forint extraordinary tax was payable by the country's Jewry, numbering 14,847 souls. We may safely put the number of Jews then living here at one-third higher, since that many may have been omitted from the conscriptions. We can therefore estimate the Jewry of Hungary at the end of the 1740s at twenty thousand, at a time when the total population of this territory had risen to two million. The Jewry thus made up one per cent of the population.
The conscriptions give detailed information on the Jews' places of origin, times of immigration and places of settlement.
From the last years of the 17th century, the immigration of Jews from the Austrian provinces was already subject to certain conditions. Even before the conclusion of the Peace of Karlóca

45

PDF viewer page 48

Source scan
a royal decree provided that emigrating Jews had to pay a so-called "abfahrt Geldt" in proportion to their property (M. Zs. O. II. no. 271, 1 December 1698).
Prince Lichtenstein, lord of Lundenburg in Moravia, releases a Jewish family that had been his hereditary subjects and transfers it to Czobor Márton, owner of the Sasvár estate. The prince issues a letter of release ("Losz-Brief"), signed in his own hand and sealed (M. Zs. O. III. no. 164, 1720). The settlement of immigrant Jews likewise required permission. In 1701 King Leopold I granted a Jew from Prague, then living in Pozsony, permission to settle anywhere in Hungary, subject to the prior consent of the landlords, and to trade there. Every settlement required the landlord's consent, and an agreement had in any case to be reached with him on the annual payment due. Where a Jewish congregation existed in a village, its permission was also needed for settlement. The letters of protection that landlords granted to Jewish communities always stipulated that new members could be admitted only with the lord's consent. In the 17315 [OCR: year unclear, probably 1735] conscription of the Jewry of Nagymarton we repeatedly read: "alhero komben mit Consens der Gemain und des ambts" (M. Zs. O. V/2. no. 1111 [OCR: '1111. RZ.']). Naturally there were newcomers who could not or would not settle permanently. The conscriptions refer to them as vagus, vagabundus, oberrans and inatabilis [OCR; probably instabilis] Jews.
The conscriptions offer little basis for genealogical research, since only a few people bore a fixed family name. Most were recorded under traditional biblical names and their corrupted forms. Some took their names from their place of origin or occupation, and there are also nicknames referring to physical appearance. These conscriptions also give insight into the family, social, economic and cultural conditions of the Jews of this period, but a full treatment of these matters requires detailed research. The lists also yield some data on prominent individuals of the period.
The published conscriptions give a picture of the relationship between Jewish subjects and their noble and aristocratic lords. They provide no data, however, on how the Jews actually lived among the mass of serfs in the villages, although, with few exceptions, almost all of them settled among the serfs. Only in a few western localities, where Jews lived in larger numbers, were spatially separate Jewish congregations established (Pozsony Váralja, Kismarton Váralja, the Jewish quarter of Német Keresztúr, etc.). Most of them lived in scattered settlements, and very many formed the only Jewish family in their village, serving as the landlord's tavern-keepers. Even where several of them lived together, as shopkeepers and as peddlers travelling from village to village, they were in constant contact with the villagers. In several places, where the serf plot allotted to them came with its appurtenances, they, like the serfs, also cultivated the ploughland and vineyards: "alimoniam rustici labore quaererent" (Ugocsa County, 1744). In Bereg County in 1727: "unum rusticanum fundum exeolit" [OCR; probably excolit].
In the German- and Slavic-speaking border regions, the Jews living there spoke these languages as well. Even in the more inland, Hungarian regions of the country, we find them in larger numbers among the newly settled Swabian and Franconian population (Tata, Lovasberény, Zsámbék, Óbuda, Paks, Nagykároly). Only slowly and in smaller numbers did they reach purely Hungarian villages, where they naturally had to learn the Hungarian speech needed for everyday contact, so that they could make themselves

46

PDF viewer page 49

Source scan
make themselves understood by the population. In the mouths of the first generation Hungarian speech still sounded foreign, but in the speech of their children, who grew up in a Hungarian community and environment, this was no longer noticeable. In Szatmár, Szabolcs and Zala counties the enumerators carrying out the conscription already recorded their Hungarian names. Characteristic of this change of names is that two brothers who appear in Rohonc under the name "Volf" go by the name "Farkas" in Muraszombat (1735).
      According to the census, the better-off, larger Jewish families employed menservants and maidservants, who came from among the children of poor Jewish families. In several places we find Christian coachmen employed by Jewish families, thus in Zala county (Keszthely, Zalaszentgrót, Nagykanizsa, Zalaegerszeg, 1728). In Muraszombat the Jewish toll-collectors have a Christian agent. In Rohonc, Vas county, they lease out their lands to Christians [OCR: "áreridába", read as "árendába" = in lease]. The censuses record Jewish musicians, lute players and cimbalom players. As an example of fraternization we cite the following entry: for the single five-member Jewish family living in Mikusócz [OCR: "Mikusoczon"], a village in Trencsén county, we find the following designation of occupation: Parens fidicon Cum duobus filiis, in popinis solummodo rusticos et heluones exhilaret, praeterea a.hena crematoria plieat [OCR readings "fidicon", "a.hena", "plieat" uncertain]. The father plays music with his two sons, they entertain the villagers and the revellers in the taverns, and in addition the head of the family practises the trade of kettle-mender (1735).
      In the foreign environment the Jewish families clung steadfastly to their traditional customs. In several places where a Jewish family with many children lived alone, they engaged a praeceptor [OCR: "praecoptort"] for their children, to keep the ancient teaching alive.
 Jewish schools were established in the more populous Jewish communities. Thus, for example, in Holics, Nyitra county, the census of 1728 mentions the "Ludimagister judaicus" [OCR: "judaious"], the schoolmaster, and five assistants (coadjutores). From the villages of Trencsén county they send their children to study in Pozsony and Vágújhely (1746). In the more populous Jewish communities we find a whole range of employees.
       Surveying the censuses of the three decades, we find mention only three times of Jewish individuals leaving their community. In Pozsony county, at Felső Diós, we find concerning the daughter of a family: "filia una quam habuit facta christiana" [one daughter whom he had became a Christian]. In 1746 [OCR: "174G"], in Bereg county, in the village of Voloc, we read of a boy: "filius Iczko catechumenus factus est" [OCR: "catcchuarnenus"; his son Iczko became a catechumen]. In Árva county in 1747 it is mentioned of a Jew's companion: "sodalis vero eiusdem Christianus factus esset" [his companion, however, had become a Christian]. Otherwise the Jewish families everywhere held fast to their traditional way of life.
       In this volume we publish the conscriptions carried out up to 1748, since with the toleration tax of the 1740s [OCR: "] 740-es"; the year/decade reading is uncertain] a new era begins in the life of Hungarian Jewry and a new system of censuses spreads. The market-town and village Jewry living under seigneurial authority was subordinated to the fullest extent to its feudal lords. Even the noble counties concerned themselves little with them. The burghers, for their part, excluded them from their towns. However, through the fact that from 1750 onward Jewry jointly and regularly, year after year, paid the toleration tax levied on it, whose amount increased from time to time, it came in some way into a stronger relationship with state power. The government authorities of the absolute state — in accordance with the spreading mercantilist outlook — recognized in them subjects who could be put to good use for the state. In this way Jewry could more than once count on the support of state power against its feudal lords and against the narrow-minded, exclusive urban burghers.
 The mixed commission formed from members of the Lieutenancy Council and the Hungarian Chamber entered into negotiations — for the first time in 1749 — with the delegates of Jewry.

                                                                                    47

PDF viewer page 50

Source scan
It agreed with them on the annual amount of the toleration tax and made it the task of Jewry to carry out the assessment and collection of this tax in the individual counties. This commission compelled the Jewish congregations to organize themselves, which meant progress compared with their past. Thus a new system of censuses also came into being.
     The material of the next volume of our Oklevéltár will consist of the more important documents relating to the toleration tax and of the registers showing the assessment and collection of this tax.

                                        INTRODUCTION


               THE NATIONWIDE CENSUSES OF JEWS IN HUNGARY
                     IN THE FIRST HALF OF THE 18TH CENTURY

      Among the most valuable sources for the history of Hungarian Jewry in the first half of the 18th century are the documents, surviving in various forms, that contain the results of the nationwide Jewish censuses held at that time; these documents themselves we are accustomed to call censuses. The special source value of these censuses lies in the fact that, at certain points in time when the Jews were enumerated, they illuminate the economic and social, and likewise the legal, situation of the entire Jewry living in the territory of Hungary of that time — in the narrower sense. From them we learn, among other things, where Jews lived, how many there were, what they occupied themselves with, and who paid what, to whom, and on what title. Without knowledge of these data it would not be possible to write the history of Jewry in a proper manner.
      We have no knowledge of any Jewish census of nationwide character in Hungary, i.e. one extending to the entire territory of the country, before 1725. We do know, however, of a few Jewish censuses prepared for individual regions of so-called Upper Hungary, which belonged to a separate treasury administration, that is, for one part of the country. These censuses are known from 1698 and 1699; they were prepared, on the order of the treasury authority of Upper Hungary, the Szepes Chamber, by the thirtieth-duty (harmincad) offices subordinate to the Chamber, and accordingly they contain the data on the enumerated Jewry arranged by thirtieth-duty districts. From 1698 we know of the Jewish censuses of five Upper Hungarian thirtieth-duty districts, namely those of Bártfa, Eperjes, Lubló, Szatmár and Sztropkó. From 1699 we know of the Jewish censuses of six Upper Hungarian thirtieth-duty districts, namely those of Bártfa, Homonna, Kurima, Szatmár, Szőllős and Sztropkó. From the two years, therefore, the Jewish censuses of a total of eight Upper Hungarian thirtieth-duty districts are known; for three of them they have survived from both years.¹
      At the turn of the 17th and 18th centuries, 27 thirtieth-duty offices belonged under the administration of the Szepes Chamber.² It may be supposed that the Jews were enumerated in all 27 thirtieth-duty


      ¹ The text of the mentioned 1698 and 1699 Jewish censuses was published in volume II of the Magyar-Zsidó Oklevéltár, Bp. 1937. The compiler of the volume did not take into account the connection between the censuses and the letters of the thirtieth-duty officials who submitted them. The letters of the individual thirtieth-duty officials and the censuses prepared by them are found at the following places in the volume: from 1698, Bártfa pp. 283-284, 289, Eperjes pp. 292-295, Lubló pp. 289-290, 295-296, Szatmár p. 296, Sztropkó pp. 290-291; from 1699, Bártfa pp. 300-301, Homonna pp. 306-308, Kurima pp. 308-309, Szatmár pp. 310-312, Szőllős p. 302, Sztropkó pp. 303-306.
      ² For their list see Ember Győző: A szepesi kamarai igazgatás tisztviselői 1703-ban [The officials of the Szepes Chamber administration in 1703]. Levéltári Közlemények, vol. 24, 1946, p. 274.

4 Oklevéltár VII.
                                                                                                 49

PDF viewer page 52

Source scan
districts, but apart from the eight mentioned we do not know the censuses of any further districts.
      There is also some basis for supposing that at this time the Jews were enumerated not only in Upper Hungary but also in other parts of Hungary in the narrower sense. For the ruler — on the initiative of the Court Chamber in Vienna — at the end of November / beginning of December 1698 sent out a three-member commission charged with enumerating the Jews of Hungary, assessing their situation, and levying an annual tax on them accordingly. The head of the commission was a councillor of the Viennese Court Chamber; its further members were a councillor of the Hungarian Chamber in Pozsony and a secretary of the Viennese Court Chamber. The commission's competence extended to the entire territory of Hungary.³
      The history of the commission's activity has not yet been clarified. We do not know whether the planned census of the Jews was completed. It is uncertain whether the 1698 and 1699 Upper Hungarian Jewish censuses mentioned above were prepared for the commission. The 1698 censuses were prepared in July and August, when the commission was not yet operating. This argues that they were not prepared for the commission. Regarding the 1699 censuses it is rather more plausible that they are connected with the commission's activity, although the covering letters of the thirtieth-duty officials, with which they submitted the censuses to the Szepes Chamber, make no mention of the commission.
      Since we cannot state with certainty even of the surviving 1698 and 1699 Upper Hungarian Jewish censuses that they were prepared for the commission, the probability is very small that the commission prepared the planned censuses in the rest of the country. The circumstance that no such censuses have come to light so far argues rather that they were never made.
      It is likewise uncertain what became of the planned taxation of the Jews. We know of one datum indicating that the commission collected 24,000 florins of tax in 1699 [OCR: "](j(JB"; year read in light of note 4]⁴, but we know nothing more specific about this. We also have data indicating that in the territory under the authority of the Szepes Chamber, i.e. in Upper Hungary, the Jews in the first half of the 18th century paid an annual tax to the treasury with more or less regularity; in the rest of the country, however, this tax of a toleration character did not take root before 1743 or 1749, respectively [OCR: "l743 illetve lí4D"; second year uncertain].⁵
      It is probable that the few censuses known from this region from 1721 [OCR: "1í21"] are connected with the treasury taxation of the Upper Hungarian Jews. These censuses were prepared by the officials of the treasury estates of Diósgyőr, Szentmiklós, Munkács and Szatmár


       ³ The ruler notified the Hungarian Chamber in Pozsony of the dispatch of the commission in his decree dated 24 Nov. 1698. Magyar-Zsidó Oklevéltár, vol. II, Bp. 1937, pp. 296-298 [OCR: "2!J6-2DI;"; page range uncertain]. The royal open mandate ordering support for the commission is dated 1 Dec. 1698 [OCR: "1691;"]. Ibid., pp. 298-300. For one measure of the commission, dated 11 Feb. 1699, by which it appointed a head for the Jews of Pozsony, see ibid., pp. 309-310. — In the three documents cited, the name of the commission's president appears in three different forms — Olbern, Olberen, Ul berich [OCR spacing; possibly "Ulberich"] János Ferenc. The Hungarian chamber councillor was Bornemissza János.
      ⁴ According to the minutes of 26 Apr. 1749 of the court commission dealing with the taxation of the Jews of Hungary, iidem comissarii anno 1699. in commissione sua procedentes 24 mille florenorum exegerunt [the same commissioners, proceeding in their commission in the year 1699, collected 24 thousand florins]. Országos Levéltár, Magyar kanc. lvt., Origo ref. 1749. num. 81.
      ⁵ Ibid.


50

PDF viewer page 53

Source scan
treasury estates.⁶ We cannot classify these censuses as being of nationwide character even if we assume that they were prepared throughout the whole of Upper Hungary.
                                          *
     The first Jewish census of nationwide character in Hungary was ordered by the ruler in 1725, and it was carried out between 1725 and 1728. In the first half of the 18th century the nationwide census of the Jews of Hungary took place three more times, between 1735-1739, 1743-1745 and 1746-1748 [OCR: "174(j"].
     In all four cases the territory of the censuses was Hungary in the narrower sense, to which Transylvania, Slavonia, Croatia, Dalmatia, the Banat, the Military Frontier and the Szepes towns did not belong. The so-called Partium (Közép-Szolnok, Kraszna and Zarand counties and the Kővár district) appears in the censuses for a time because, although it belonged to Transylvania, in certain matters it was under Hungarian administration.
     Hungary in the narrower sense had the following 43 counties:
Abaújvár, Arad, Árva, Baranya, Bars, Bács-Bodrog⁷, Bereg, Békés, Bihar, Borsod, Csanád, Csongrád, Esztergom, Fejér, Gömör, Győr, Heves with Külső Szolnok, Hont, Komárom, Liptó, Mármaros, Moson, Nógrád, Nyitra, Pest-Pilis-Solt, Pozsony, Sáros, Somogy, Sopron, Szabolcs, Szatmár, Szepes, Tolna, Torna, Trencsén, Turóc, Ugocsa, Ung, Vasvár, Veszprém, Zala, Zemplén and Zólyom.
     The counties (comitatus) had a significant role in the Jewish censuses. It was their task to enumerate the Jews living in their administrative territory.
      The free royal and mining towns (civitates liberae regiae ac montanae) had a similar role; geographically they belonged to the territory of the counties, but administratively they were independent. Hungary in the narrower sense had 37 such towns: Bakabánya, Bazin, Bártfa, Besztercebánya, Bélabánya, Breznóbánya, Buda, Debrecen, Eperjes, Esztergom, Győr, Kassa, Késmárk, Kismarton, Komárom, Korpona, Körmöcbánya [OCR: "Körrnöobánya"], Kőszeg, Libetbánya, Lőcse, Modor, Nagybánya, Nagyszombat, Pest, Pozsony, Ruszt, Selmecbánya, Sopron, Szakolca, Szatmárnémeti, Szeben, Szeged, Szentgyörgy, Székesfehérvár, Trencsén, Újbánya, Zólyom.
     Finally, the role of the so-called districts (districtus) was the same; these were separate from the counties both geographically and administratively. Hungary in the narrower sense had 2 such districts: that of the Jász-Kun and that of the Hajdú market towns. (The Kővár district belonged to the Partium.)
     The counties, towns and districts alike were administratively under the supreme authority of the royal Lieutenancy Council (consilium regium locumtenentiale). They received decrees from it and sent their submissions to it. It did occur, however, that they petitioned the ruler directly in some matter.
     The Lieutenancy Council, organized in 1724, was a national government authority. The administration of the country fell within its competence, except for chamber or treasury matters and military matters. For the former, the national government authority was the Hungarian Royal Chamber (camera regia Hungarica); the latter were handled until 1740

    ⁶ These censuses were published in volume III of the Magyar-Zsidó Oklevéltár, Bp. 1937, pp. 380-381, 385-386, 387-389, 395-397.
    ⁷ The two still appeared separately.


4*
                                                                                  51

PDF viewer page 54

Source scan
by the military commanders, and from 1740 onward by the national supreme military commands (supremae armorum praefecturae).
      The affairs of Jewry, including the matter of the Jewish censuses, fell at the national level within the competence of the Lieutenancy Council. The chamber and military national government organs had only a secondary role in the matter of the Jewish censuses, in contrast to the situation before 1724, when — as we have seen — the censuses were carried out by chamber authorities.
       In connection with the role of the Lieutenancy Council, mention must also be made of the national or provincial commissariat (commissariatus provincialis), which took part in the Jewish census of 1746-1748. This commissariat operated as an authority subordinate to the Lieutenancy Council; its competence covered military matters affecting the civilian population and, in connection with these, the collection and accounting of the state tax. The director (director) of the commissariat was one of the councillors of the Lieutenancy Council. Subordinate to him, and through him to the Lieutenancy Council, were the national or provincial district commissioners or chief commissioners (commissarii, supremi commissarii). In the 1740s Hungary in the narrower sense had 7 national or provincial commissariat districts (districtus): those of Pozsony, Besztercebánya, Sopron, Buda, Kassa, Debrecen and Eszék. They operated in parallel and in harmony with the war commissariats; through them the Lieutenancy Council was able to call on the support of the military and, when necessary, to enforce its decrees by armed force against the counties, towns and districts.
       Legally the Lieutenancy Council was directly subordinate to the ruler; it addressed its submissions to him, and the royal dispositions arriving at the Lieutenancy Council spoke in his name. In reality, however, these royal dispositions were prepared by the Hungarian Royal Court Chancellery (cancellaria regia Hungarica aulica); the Lieutenancy Council's submissions came in to it, and it laid them before the ruler accompanied by its own proposals. In practice the Chancellery was the superior authority of the Lieutenancy Council.
       The royal dispositions concerning Jewish affairs, including the matter of the censuses, in the period between 1725 and 1748 were all prepared at the Hungarian Royal Court Chancellery.
       We would be mistaken, however, if we believed that the series of administrative organs that dealt with Hungarian affairs — and thus with the affairs of Jewry and with the matter of the Jewish censuses — ended with the Chancellery, and that after the Chancellery came the ruler himself. Legally this was indeed the situation. Here too, however, reality differed from the legal situation. The Hungarian king was at the same time the ruler of the Habsburg empire. Besides the Hungarian government authorities there were also imperial government organs. These he consulted — contrary to Hungarian laws — in Hungarian matters as well. In Hungarian matters the ruler's decisions were more than once born in these imperial government organs, foreign from the Hungarian point of view. This finding applies also to Jewish affairs and to the matter of the Jewish censuses.
       In what follows, when we speak of royal decrees, we must be aware that the roots of these decrees may be sought, besides the Lieutenancy Council and the Chancellery, in these imperial government organs as well — naturally without leaving the rulers themselves out of consideration. The role of the Lieutenancy Council and the Chancellery is easy to clarify; their records are available in the National

52

PDF viewer page 55

Source scan
Archives. The role of the imperial government organs could also be clarified on the basis of their records; these records, kept in the Viennese archives, could not, however, be used for the writing of this study. Thus the author of this study has had to be content with speaking of royal initiative even in cases where the initiative may have come not from the ruler personally but from one of the ruler's imperial government organs. It is a task for further research to examine more closely the motives of such royal initiatives, extending also to the role of the imperial government organs.

                                               *
      Each of the four Jewish censuses held between 1725 and 1748 took place on royal initiative. What part in this initiative the rulers themselves had, and what part the imperial government organs, only further research extending also to the material of the Viennese archives will be able to clarify — as we have said.
      Further research may also shed light on who was the initiator of the royal decree that the Hungarian Royal Court Chancellery addressed in the name of Charles VI (III) to the Hungarian Royal Lieutenancy Council on 28 June 1725. From that part of the decree's introduction which refers to measures taken concerning Jewry in other provinces of the empire, we may infer that the initiator was one of the imperial government organs. It can be regarded as almost certain that the initiative did not come from the Hungarian government authorities.
      The decree starts from the premise that Jewry, if its number exceeds a certain measure, is to the detriment of the population [OCR: "bkosság kárára YI1I1"]. On the basis of this consideration, certain provinces of the empire, the so-called hereditary provinces, decided to reduce the number of Jews living in their territory. For Hungary these measures meant the danger that the Jews expelled from the hereditary provinces would come here. The ruler therefore calls upon the Lieutenancy Council to prevent the immigration of Jews and, further, to reduce the number of those already present, or to submit its proposal concerning the achievement of these aims.
      In connection with the above, the ruler also orders the Lieutenancy Council to have the entire Jewry living in the country enumerated. As to what the census should be like, the royal decree says little: it should show where Jews live and what public burdens they bear.⁸
       The Lieutenancy Council forwarded the royal command to the counties, towns and districts in its circular decree dated 9 July 1725 [OCR: "júl. H-én"; day read from note 9]. It instructed them, on the one hand, to prevent by appropriate measures foreign Jews from moving into their territory, and on the other hand, to enumerate the Jews living in their territory.

Jews. The Lieutenancy Council's circular said no more about the manner of the census than the royal rescript.⁹
      In response to the Lieutenancy Council's circular, some counties and towns enumerated the Jews living in their territory, others did not. The Lieutenancy Council did not press the laggards. The pressing came, in the ruler's name, from the Hungarian Chancellery to the Lieutenancy Council, nearly a year and a half after the earlier royal rescript, dated 13 Dec. 1726. It ordered that the Lieutenancy Council submit without delay the report and proposal requested concerning Jewry.¹⁰
      The Lieutenancy Council replied to this royal decree relatively quickly, on 8 Jan. 1727, which allows us to conclude that it had already been dealing with the question before the royal urging.
      In the first part of the submission it made a detailed proposal as to how, on the one hand, the immigration of foreign Jews into Hungary could be prevented and, on the other, the number of Jews living in the country could be reduced. However interesting and instructive this part of the submission may be, I do not deal with it on this occasion, since it is not closely connected with the matter of the census.¹¹
      The second part of the submission dealt with the question of the census. It reported that only 7 counties and 4 towns had prepared and sent in the census.¹² The Lieutenancy Council attached an extract of these censuses to its submission. At the same time it reported that for the other counties and towns it had set the end of February of that year as the deadline by which they were obliged to send in the census.¹³
      The ruler, or rather the Hungarian Chancellery, replied to the Lieutenancy Council's submission on 29 April 1727. The reply touched only in passing on the proposals contained in the submission, saying that a decision on them could be made only after the census had been completed. It ordered, however, that the censuses be prepared everywhere within one month, i.e. by the end of May, and that the Lieutenancy Council submit them.¹⁴
      We would know poorly the custom of the counties and towns, and likewise of the districts, in executing the decrees of the Lieutenancy Council if we believed that the

returns had been completed everywhere by the end of either February or May 1727, and had been sent in from everywhere to the Lieutenancy Council. The Lieutenancy Council entrusted one of its councillors, János Antolchich, with reviewing the reports received in the matter of the Jewish census and informing the Council about them.
      János Antolchich did very thorough work. He showed which of the counties, towns and districts had reported that they had no Jews, which had not answered the Lieutenancy Council's call, and which had sent returns that could not be considered adequate. He also explained in detail why these latter returns were inadequate. He submitted his statement to the Lieutenancy Council on 30 May 1727.15
      On the basis of János Antolchich's report the Lieutenancy Council drew up the statement which it submitted to the monarch on 19 July 1727.16 This statement grouped the counties, towns and districts into four categories.
      In the first category it placed those that had reported that they had no Jewish inhabitants. This category consisted primarily of towns, namely the following: Bakabánya, Bazin, Bártfa, Besztercebánya, Bélabánya, Debrecen, Eperjes, Esztergom, Kassa, Késmárk, Kismarton, Korpona, Körmöcbánya, Libetbánya, Nagybánya, Nagyszombat, Pest, Ruszt, Selmecbánya, Sopron, Szeben, Szeged and Zólyom.17 – Of the counties, Bars and Csongrád, as well as Kraszna from the Partium. – Likewise from the Partium, Kővár district.
      In the second category the Lieutenancy Council placed those counties and towns that had prepared and sent adequate returns. A return qualified as adequate if it showed the place of residence and number of the Jews enumerated, and also whether they paid public tax and seigneurial dues. This category comprised the counties of Árva, Bihar, Hont, Sopron, Vasvár and Zemplén, and the towns of Buda, Kőszeg, Modor, Szakolca, Szatmárnémeti and Trencsén.
      The returns prepared by the counties and towns placed in the third category did not meet the said requirements. This category comprised the counties of Baranya, Bács, Békés, Borsod, Fejér, Győr, Heves, Liptó, Moson, Nógrád, Pest, Somogy, Trencsén, Turóc and Ung. There was no town in this category.
      The fourth category comprised those counties and towns that did not even answer the Lieutenancy Council's repeated calls. These were: the counties of Abaújvár, Arad, Bereg, Bodrog, Csanád, Esztergom, Gömör, Komárom, Máramaros, Nyitra, Pozsony, Sáros, Szabolcs, Szatmár, Szepes, Tolna, Torna, Ugocsa, Veszprém, Zala and Zólyom, and the towns of Breznóbánya, Lőcse, Pozsony, Szentgyörgy, Székesfehérvár and Újbánya in Hungary proper,18 as well as Közép Szolnok county in the Partium.19

      Thus, two years after it had been ordered, the result of the census was far from satisfactory. 22 counties and 6 towns had not even deigned to reply to the Lieutenancy Council. 15 counties had prepared inadequate returns. Only 6 counties and 6 towns had sent in returns that met the requirements.
      The Lieutenancy Council sent up all the returns received up to then to the court in Vienna, to the monarch, or rather to the Hungarian Chancellery.
      At the same time, i.e. on 19 July 1727 [OCR: 'ID'; read as 19], it again issued an order to those counties and towns which the said statement had placed in the third and fourth categories, namely those which had either sent in no return at all or had prepared an inadequate one. In this order it called upon these counties and towns to enumerate the Jews living in their territory without delay. It now prescribed much more precisely what the return should be like and what it should contain. It should list every Jewish male by name, not only the heads of household but also their sons, relatives, dependants and servants. It should list the name of the locality where those enumerated lived. It should state whether they had land, and if so, what kind, and whether they cultivated it themselves or had it cultivated by others. It should state what they paid, and under what title, to the state, the county or town, and to their landlord on account of their land or their trade.20
      The monarch, or rather the Hungarian Chancellery, replied on 16 October 1727 [OCR: 'l ö'; read as 16] to the Lieutenancy Council's submission dated 19 July of that year. In the meantime, however, it had also urged the census in its order of 25 September 1727. In this rescript it pointed out that restrictive measures against the Jews were being prepared in Bohemia, so it was to be feared that they would come from there to Hungary. To prevent this, their enumeration was urgent.21
      The royal order of 16 October 1727 expressed astonishment that the return had not been completed even by such a county as Pozsony, in whose territory the seat of the Lieutenancy Council was located. It again urged the submission of the returns.22
      On 1? November 1727 [OCR: 'l d'; day uncertain] the Lieutenancy Council sent a further report to the monarch in the matter of the Jewish census. It submitted the returns that had come in in the meantime. Returns came in from the following counties: Abaújvár, Árva, Baranya, Bereg, Heves, Komárom, Liptó, Moson, Nógrád, Pest, Sáros and Turóc in Hungary proper, and Közép Szolnok in the Partium. Liptó, Nógrád, Pest and Turóc counties sent in their second return, Abaújvár, Bereg, Komárom, Sáros and Közép Szolnok their first, and Moson its third. – Gömör and Zólyom, as well as – contrary to its earlier report – Hont counties reported that no Jews lived in their territory.23
      At the end of 1727 and in the first months of 1728 further returns reached the Lieutenancy Council. It submitted these on 10 March 1728. They were the returns of Arad, Bács, Máramaros, Pozsony, Szepes, Tolna, Torna, Trencsén, Ugocsa, Ung and Zaránd counties, the last from the Partium, the other 10 from Hungary proper.       We do not know of any further submissions by the Lieutenancy Council in the matter of the Jewish census ordered in 1725. Apart from the returns mentioned in these submissions, a few more came in. Of the counties, Nyitra and Zala, and of the towns, Szentgyörgy also sent returns to the Lieutenancy Council.
      But even so, despite the provisions of the monarch and the Lieutenancy Council repeated so many times, there remained counties, towns and districts that sent neither a return nor a report stating that they had no Jewish inhabitants. Among the counties these were Csanád, Esztergom, Szabolcs, Szatmár and Veszprém; among the towns Breznóbánya, Lőcse, Pozsony, Székesfehérvár and Újbánya; and among the districts the Jász-Kun district and the Hajdú towns.
      If we look at where Jews were enumerated in the 1730s [OCR: 'InO-as'; read as 1730s] and the 1740s, then on this basis we may assume almost with certainty only of the 5 towns and 2 districts in question that they had no Jewish inhabitants.25 Of the 5 counties in question we can venture this assumption only for Csanád. Csanád county sent a negative report to the Lieutenancy Council in 1736 [OCR: 'InG'; read as 1736], but in the 1740s it already submitted returns. In 1748 [OCR: '174S'] Csanád county had 12, Esztergom 1?6 [OCR: '] :i6'; digits uncertain], Szabolcs 22? [OCR: '22]'; last digit uncertain], Szatmár 346, and Veszprém 327 Jewish inhabitants.26 Thus in the 1720s, of the five counties in question, it is probable only in the case of Csanád that it did not enumerate the Jews living in its territory not out of negligence but because there were none. The other four counties in all probability did not carry out the orders of the monarch and the Lieutenancy Council.
      We have no knowledge of their having received any punishment for failing to carry out the census.
      The Hungarian Chancellery had the returns it received copied, placed the copies among its own records, and sent the originals27 back to the Lieutenancy Council; these entered the Lieutenancy Council's records.

      The returns are now among the records of the Hungarian Chancellery and of the Lieutenancy Council, in the custody of the Hungarian National Archives.28
      Altogether the Jewish returns of 36 [OCR: '3H'; cf. p. 60 '36'] counties and 7 towns from the period 1725–1728 survive here. Two of them (that of Győr county and that of the town of Buda) survive only in Chancellery copies; the rest survive in the "original" copies received by the Lieutenancy Council and for the most part also in Chancellery copies.
      The history of the census has revealed the explanation of why some counties prepared the return twice, and Moson even three times.
      The history of the census also provides an explanation of why the returns prepared in the various counties and towns are not uniform. We have seen that the Lieutenancy Council's circular of 9 July 1725 defined only approximately what the return should contain, and only on 29 July 1727 did it prescribe the requirements more precisely. But even so it could not ensure that the enumerators commissioned by the counties and towns proceeded uniformly everywhere.
      The completed returns thus illuminate the situation of Hungarian Jewry in the period 1725–1728 in varying detail.29
      The analysis of the content and form of the returns and the establishment of the lessons arising from them is not the task of this study.

                                                 *
      There is no trace of any measure having been put into effect against Hungarian Jewry on the basis of the national census carried out in the second half of the 1720s. The planned measures to prevent the immigration of Jews and to limit their number did not materialize.
      The number of Jews thus grew unhindered, and this growth was evidently not merely the consequence of natural increase, but also resulted from immigration from beyond the borders. The Viennese Court Chamber drew the monarch's attention to this growth in 1785 [sic in OCR; context suggests 1735]. It referred to the excessively increased number of the Jews of Máramaros on the basis of a return which was presumably prepared for Chamber purposes.
      By his order dated 9 September 1735 the monarch sent this return to the Lieutenancy Council. The royal order considered it beyond doubt that the number of Jews had risen in a similar manner not only in Máramaros but in other parts of the country as well, as a result of restrictive measures having been introduced in the neighbouring hereditary provinces. It laid down that in Hungary, too, only as many Jews might be tolerated as did not harm the public and did not cause the ruin of the country's other inhabitants. It called upon the Lieutenancy Council to have the Jews enumerated in the other counties and towns of the country as well, and on the basis of the result of the census to make a proposal for reducing the number of Jews, or rather for preventing their further growth, as it had done in its submission dated 8? [OCR: 'R'] January 1727.

Helyt. lvt. Fasc. mens. Litt., under various daily dates. Those in the latter place will be transferred to the former.

Let it meanwhile order that the landlords should not admit wandering Jews to their estates.30
      This royal rescript ordered the second national census of Hungarian Jewry in the first half of the 18th century. The aim of the census ordered in 1735 was the same as that of the one held ten years earlier: establishing the number of Jews with a view to reducing it, or rather preventing its further growth. Thus this, too, was primarily a census with a public-order purpose.
      This census, however – like the one ordered in 1725 – also had an economic purpose. The monarch wished to learn not only where and how many Jews lived in the country, but also under what material circumstances, and not least under whose seigneurial protection they lived and what they paid in return for this protection. On the other hand, he did not wish for information on whether they shared in public burdens, or whether they paid state, county or town taxes.
      Although the royal order did not say what the return should contain, the columns of the Máramaros county Chamber return enclosed as a model indicated what the Viennese court wanted to know.31
      The Lieutenancy Council took this Chamber return as its basis when it drew up the questions of the census. It did, however, somewhat expand these points. It likewise set out the questions in a tabular header, though not in German but in Latin. The questions, i.e. the columns of the tabular return, were the following:
      name of the locality,
      names of the Jewish heads of household,
      number of wives,
      number of older children,
      number of younger children,
      number of teachers,
      number of male servants,
      number of female servants,
      number of persons belonging to the household,
      number of horses,
      number of oxen,
      number of cows,
      number of calves,
      name and residence of the hereditary landlord,
      name of the present landlord,
      annual sum paid in cash to the hereditary landlord,
      type of produce paid to the hereditary landlord,

      In accordance with the royal rescript, the Lieutenancy Council ordered the national census of the Jews on 23 September 1735. This time too it entrusted the counties, towns and districts with carrying out the census. It informed them of the contents of the royal rescript, sent them the census table, the idea tabellae, and set a deadline of three months. No Lieutenancy Council order went to Máramaros county, since its Chamber return already existed. Nor did the mining towns receive a call from the Lieutenancy Council, nor Nagyszombat, evidently because it was common knowledge that they had no Jewish inhabitants.33
      The census ordered in 1735 was carried out comparatively more smoothly than that of ten years before.34 Naturally not every county, town and district kept the three-month deadline – rather those that sent negative reports. The bulk of the returns reached the Lieutenancy Council in 1735 and 1736; on 18 December 1736 it sent reminders to a further 15 counties and 2 towns.35 In response to the reminder further returns or negative reports came in in 1737 and 1738, and Ung county sent its return only in 1739 [OCR: '173H'].
      In the end the returns of 31 counties and 8 towns were completed, as against the 36 and 7 returns, respectively, of the 1720s. Of the counties, 7 sent neither a return nor a negative report: Gömör, Nógrád, Szabolcs, Szatmár and Torna in Hungary proper, and Közép Szolnok and Kraszna in the Partium.
      The Partium – with the exception of Zaránd county – no longer fell under the competence of the Lieutenancy Council as regards the census at that time; this is the explanation why Közép Szolnok and Kraszna counties, and likewise Kővár district, sent neither a return nor a negative report.
      In the said 5 counties of Hungary proper Jewish inhabitants were enumerated in the 1740s; it may therefore be assumed that in the 1730s they did not prepare returns out of negligence, and not because they had no Jewish inhabitants. Only Gömör county may have been an exception to this, which in the 1720s made a negative report and in the 1740s had only 18 Jewish inhabitants.

      Negative reports came from 8 counties: Baranya, Bars [OCR: 'BaIB'], Csanád, Csongrád, Hont, Turóc and Zólyom in Hungary proper, and Zaránd in the Partium. Of these, Baranya, Hont, Turóc and Zaránd still had Jewish inhabitants in the 1720s.
      The number of towns in which Jews also lived rose in the 1730s from 7 to 8; Bártfa became the eighth. The towns all sent reports, except Nagyszombat and 7 mining towns, from which the Lieutenancy Council did not even request them, because it was common knowledge that no Jews lived in them.
      The Jász-Kun district and the Hajdú towns submitted negative reports.
      We mentioned that in 1735 the monarch ordered the census of the Jews on the initiative of the Chamber. This is the explanation why on 10 December 1738 the Lieutenancy Council forwarded the returns received thus far to the Hungarian Chamber.36
      The Hungarian Chamber had the returns copied. Copies were made of the returns of 29 counties and 8 towns.37 It then sent the original returns back to the Lieutenancy Council on 12 June 1739,38 and they entered the Lieutenancy Council's archive.
      Volume III of the Magyar-Zsidó Oklevéltár published the Jewish returns of the towns of Pozsony, Buda, Kismarton and Kőszeg, and of the market town of Óbuda, from the 1730s.39 According to the editor, the returns were in the archive of the Hungarian Chancellery.40 From this one might conclude that the Hungarian Chancellery also had copies made of the returns. There is, however, no other trace of this; at present – to our knowledge – the Jewish returns prepared in the 1730s are not in the archive of the Hungarian Chancellery.
      The Jewish census ordered in 1735 was the most uniformly prepared of the national Jewish censuses of the first half of the 18th century. It is among the most valuable historical sources illuminating the economic and social situation of the Jews.41
      On the basis of the returns received, the Lieutenancy Council had various, partly fragmentary, statements prepared. The more significant of these are the two which show the Jews under domestic and under foreign hereditary landlords, respectively.42 A summary statement was also prepared of the Jews living in the towns.43
      We have no knowledge of the Lieutenancy Council having made a proposal to the monarch, on the basis of the result of the Jewish census carried out in the 1730s,

for any restriction of the Jews. It did, on 26 [OCR: '2o'; cf. note 44: 26] January 1737, issue a circular in which it instructed the counties, towns and districts to proceed against the Jews in the same way as against vagrants, i.e. to check their identity and, if they had no passport, to arrest them and deport them to their place of domicile. It also ordered that the Jews living in their territory should be given a regular passport if they went anywhere.44 These provisions, however, can hardly be connected with the census; they contain no reference to it either. What was at issue here was the extension of the regulations concerning vagrants to the Jewish population of the country. In his rescript dated 27 February 1737 approving the provision,45 the monarch, too, regarded it only as a temporary measure for the time until the other measures planned for restricting the Jews were introduced, for the sake of which he had ordered both the 1725 and the 1735 census. These further measures, however, did not materialize.46
      The two national Jewish censuses of the 1740s took place for an entirely different reason.
                                                   *
      A brief royal rescript dated 12 February 1743 reached the Lieutenancy Council. The rescript referred to the grave situation of the treasury, which had to be remedied by every possible means. It further referred to the fact that many Jews lived in Hungary. On all these grounds it called upon the Lieutenancy Council to make a proposal for the extraordinary taxation of Hungarian Jewry.47
      On the Hungarian Chancellery draft of the royal rescript the note can be read that the king gave the instruction for the provision.48 It is probable, however, that the idea of the extraordinary taxation of Hungarian Jewry, to cover to some extent the costs of the War of the Austrian Succession, arose at the Viennese Court Chamber.
      This royal provision introduced the third national census of Hungarian Jewry in the first half of the 18th century. The aim of this census was the extraordinary taxation of the Jews, in contrast to the previous censuses of 1725 and 1735, which were prepared with a public-order purpose, for the restriction of the Jews.
      The Lieutenancy Council entrusted one of its councillors, Kristóf Festetics, with making a proposal in the matter. Festetics's opinion was that the extraordinary taxation of the Jews planned by the monarch was not possible. He argued that, under the agreement reached between the monarch and the Estates in the matter of the war tax, everything that belongs to the tax base [is] at the disposal of the Estates
