stands. The Jews too belong to the tax base, and therefore only the estates have the right to tax them. By this right the counties levy the war tax on the Jews as well, and on the occasion of the noble insurrection they even levied an extraordinary tax on them. The resolution passed at the diet, which practice has also confirmed, could be changed only at a new diet. There is little hope of that, since at the most recent diet, too, numerous complaints were voiced because the Szepes Chamber taxes the Jews living in the area under its jurisdiction, in the 13 Upper Hungarian counties [OCR: 'a13']. Nor is it worth stirring up the question of taxation for the sake of an extraordinary tax on the Jews, since according to the latest conscription only 2430 Jewish families live in the country.49
     The Lieutenancy Council submitted the proposal requested by the ruler on 6 March 1743. In its submission it set out the concerns that Festetics Kristóf had raised, except that it did not mention that the extraordinary taxation of the Jews would bring the treasury no worthwhile profit. Reckoning, however, that the concerns raised would not turn the ruler from the ruler's intention, it also made the requested proposal on the manner of taxation.
     It took as its starting point that Article 10 of 1595 had imposed a tax of 50 denars per month on the Jews. On this basis it proposed that every Jew living on separate bread, that is, every Jewish family, should pay a one-time tax of 6 [OCR: 'f)'; the figure 6 is given on p. 66] forints. This extraordinary tax, however, should not affect the right of the counties and towns, nor that of the landlords, to tax the Jews or to collect dues from them. And since some Jews are richer and some poorer, the Jews living in each county or town should themselves apportion among themselves the sum falling on them, collect it themselves, and take it to the county and town treasuries. The counties and towns should then forward it to the war cash offices of the treasury. So that the number of Jewish families on which the extraordinary tax was to be paid could be known, the counties and towns should conscribe the Jews living in their territory and report the result of the conscription to the Lieutenancy Council, which would then report on it to the ruler.50
     On 22 March 1743 the ruler replied to the Lieutenancy Council's submission that, for the sake of the desired aim, it should communicate its arguments to the counties and towns and then report on the result.51
     The Lieutenancy Council interpreted the somewhat oracular [OCR: 'kicsit rlodonai'; probably 'kissé dodonai' = 'somewhat Delphic/ambiguous'] royal rescript to mean that the king insisted on the extraordinary taxation of the Jews and approved its proposal on the rate and manner of taxation. On 2 April 1743 — in the case of the towns

in its circular dated 1 June — it therefore put forward only the arguments that spoke in favour of taxation and kept silent about its concerns. Its chief argument was that the Jews could make many kinds of profit through the war. It meant to give no less weight to the argument that Article 10 of 1595 had already imposed a tax on the Jews, and that the amount of the present tax was the same as that of 1595. It fixed the amount of the one-time extraordinary tax at 6 forints per family, and regarding its assessment and collection it ordered what it had proposed to the ruler. It ordered the counties and towns to conscribe the Jews and to send it the conscriptions, so that it could submit them to the ruler.52
        Following this circular of the Lieutenancy Council, dated 2 April and 1 June 1743 respectively, the third national Jewish conscription began. It may be said at the outset that this conscription produced the fewest results. The main reason was that the purpose of the conscription was the taxation of the Jews. Taxation was the most sensitive question of all, one on which ruler and estates were always opposed to each other. The concerns raised by Lieutenancy Councillor Festetics Kristóf in his report all proved well founded. Most of the counties of Hungary were unwilling to acknowledge the ruler's right to tax the Jews separately. These counties — and there was even a town among them — either did not deign to reply to the Lieutenancy Council's circular at all, or replied but lined up arguments in their reply to support their rejection.
        It would lead too far, beyond the scope of this study, if I were to set out one by one which county refused the extraordinary taxation of the Jews, when, and on what grounds. In essence, these arguments were the same as the concerns of Festetics Kristóf, who had himself intended his concerns as arguments against the ruler's intention.
        When the ruler inquired, in a rescript dated 21 May 1743, what had happened in the matter of the taxation of the Jews,53 the Lieutenancy Council, in its submission dated 24 May 1743, sent the reply it had given to Zala [OCR: 'ZF1la'] and Borsod counties, which had protested against the taxation. It reported that it was giving the same reply to the other protesting counties as well. It would also have given this reply to Pozsony county, which had likewise protested, had that county not indicated that it had sent its protest directly to the king as well.54
        The essence of the Lieutenancy Council's reply to the protesting counties was that royal taxation of the Jews did not prevent the counties from also taxing them separately. It further stated that everyone takes an extraordinary share of extraordinary wartime hardships, and that this applies to the Jews as well.

      In a rescript dated 31 May 1743 the ruler asked the Lieutenancy Council what the outcome of the reply given to Zala and Borsod counties had been. At the same time the ruler forwarded the protest that Pozsony county had addressed directly to the ruler, asking for a proposal on how to answer it.55
      The Lieutenancy Council did not reply to this royal rescript. The reason was that on 31 May 1743, before receiving the ruler's decree of the same date, it had submitted copies of the protests against the taxation of the Jews that it had received from 10 counties, among them Zala, Borsod and Pozsony.56
      The ruler, in turn, did not reply to this Lieutenancy Council submission. It seems that the ruler was waiting for the Lieutenancy Council's reply, while the Council was waiting for the ruler's instructions.
      During this period of mutual waiting nothing of substance happened. The Lieutenancy Council contented itself with giving the protesting counties a reply similar to the one it had given Zala and Borsod. These replies were not effective enough to make the counties change their position.
      Nearly a year passed in this way. Then the ruler's decree dated 22 May 1744 moved the matter of taxation, and with it the conscription, somewhat further forward. The royal rescript declared that the arguments raised by the counties against the taxation of the Jews were not considered valid. It called on the Lieutenancy Council to do everything in its power to bring the taxation to success as quickly as possible.57
     The Lieutenancy Council appointed a four-member committee to clarify the state of the matter and move it forward. The committee proposed that the Lieutenancy Council address a new circular to the counties and towns and set a deadline for paying the tax.58
      The Lieutenancy Council acted on the committee's proposal. On 6 June 1744 it reported to the ruler that it had addressed a circular to the counties, towns and districts, setting St. Michael's Day, that is, 29 September, as the deadline for the tax.59
     We would be mistaken, however, to think that the Lieutenancy Council's new circular was more successful than the earlier one. Sopron county, for example, in its letter dated 25 August 1744, again refused to tax the Jews. In its reply dated 25 September 1744 [OCR: 'lí44'], the Lieutenancy Council explained that the ruler had the right to tax the Jews and again called on the county to carry out the decree

It also sent the text of its reply to the county to the ruler, in its submission dated 25 September 1744. Maria Theresa dealt with the matter in person as well. Maria Theresa's autograph note sheds light on why the government did not act more forcefully against the counties. There was a war, and the fate of the empire depended not least on the attitude of the Hungarian estates. This explains the helplessness, or rather the compromising attitude, of the government authorities. The Lieutenancy Council's submission in the Sopron matter was simply noted and filed; this was the decision of the Hungarian Chancellery at its council session held on 5 October 1744 [OCR: 'okt. ő-én'; day probably 5].60
     Many more examples like that of Sopron could be listed. Vasvár county intended to use the Jewish tax it had collected for the purposes of the noble insurrection. As it wrote in its letter dated 15 September 1744, the tax of the Jews had always been used to cover county needs, and only the diet could change this practice. The Lieutenancy Council wrote to Vasvár county as well that taxing the Jews was the ruler's sovereign right, and called on it to pay the collected Jewish tax into the war cash office. In this matter too it addressed a submission to the ruler, which met the same fate as its predecessor in the Sopron matter mentioned above.61
     Receiving no further instructions from the ruler, the Lieutenancy Council contented itself with waiting for the county reports. Meanwhile it tried to convince the opposing counties, by written arguments, that their position was wrong, and repeatedly ordered them to carry out the decree that had been issued.
     The Michaelmas deadline had long passed when the ruler, in a rescript dated 4 December 1744, called on the Lieutenancy Council to report again on the result of the taxation and also to submit the Jewish conscriptions it had received.62
     The Lieutenancy Council replied to the ruler's request on 18 December 1744 [OCR: 'lS-án'; probably 18]. It submitted a tabulation of the state of the Jewish tax. The tabulation showed that, of the country's 44 counties,63 only 7 had conscribed the Jews, 7 reported that they had no Jewish inhabitants, 14 had not replied to the Lieutenancy Council's decrees, and 16 [OCR: 'Hi'; 16 is consistent with the total of 44] had refused to carry them out. — The situation was more favourable with the towns. Of the 8 towns in which Jews lived, only Buda refused to carry out the decree. Kőszeg, Szatmárnémeti and Szentgyörgy did not reply. Bártfa, Modor, Szakolca and Trencsén conscribed the Jews. — The Hajdú towns and the Jász-Kun district had no Jewish inhabitants.64

       On 18 February 1745 [OCR: 'febr. 1S-án'; note 65 gives febr. 18] the ruler ordered the counties and towns to proceed more vigorously in taxing the Jews.65
       On 13 March 1745 the Lieutenancy Council reported to the ruler that a further 5 counties and 1 town (Buda) had complied with the decrees on the conscription and taxation of the Jews.66
       After this, no significant developments occurred in the matter of the Jewish conscription and taxation ordered in 1743. Conscriptions arrived from 2 more counties and 2 towns (Győr and Kőszeg). The remaining counties and towns either protested in writing or expressed their protest by not replying to the Lieutenancy Council at all.
      The Lieutenancy Council, for its part, contented itself with forwarding copies of the letters received from the counties to the ruler, where they ended up in the archive of the Hungarian Chancellery.
       The ruler's last disposition concerning the 6-forint-per-family tax and the related conscription was dated 14 June 1745. In it the Lieutenancy Council was called on to report regularly when, from where, and how much Jewish tax came in.67 By then, however, more than three years after the taxation and conscription had been ordered, no further results could be expected.
       Summing up the result of the 1743 Jewish conscription, we can establish that of the 4 national Jewish conscriptions held in the first half of the 18th century, this one was the least successful. Of the country's 44 counties,68 only 14 complied with the Lieutenancy Council's decree: Abaújvár, Arad, Bács-Bodrog, Bereg, Csanád, Győr, Moson, Pozsony, Sáros, Szepes, Trencsén, Vasvár, Veszprém and Zala. For two of these, Pozsony and Vasvár, the conscription does not survive among the Lieutenancy Council records. 8 counties reported that they had no Jewish inhabitants: Baranya, Békés, Csongrád, Heves, Hont, Turóc, Zarand and Zólyom. No Jews lived in Bars county either. Finally, 21 counties in which Jews did live refused the conscription and the taxation.
       The situation was more favourable with the towns. After Győr was raised to the rank of free royal town, there were by then 9 towns in which Jews lived. Of these, 7 conscribed and taxed their Jewish inhabitants: Bártfa, Buda, Győr, Kőszeg, Modor, Szakolca, Trencsén. Two towns, Szatmárnémeti and Szentgyörgy, did not comply with the Lieutenancy Council's decree.
      No Jews lived in the Jász-Kun district or in the Hajdú towns.
       If we look for the reason for the failure of the 1743 Jewish conscription, we find it in the balance of power between the ruler and the Hungarian estates. During the years of the War of the Austrian Succession the ruler depended on the support of the Hungarian estates. For this reason the ruler did not act against them with the vigour that could have secured greater success. The ruler did reject the estates' argument that the ruler had no right to impose an extraordinary tax on the Jews, and held fast to the decree. However, the ruler did not do everything possible to have the decree actually carried out, and tolerated opposition to the royal will.

5*                                                                                            67

PDF viewer page 70

Source scan
      As to the question of which side was right in the dispute between the ruler and the estates over the taxation of the Jews, it can hardly be decided. The estates were right that the Jews living in the country belonged to the tax base. Their taxation — whether in the form of war tax or domestic tax — was therefore the right of the counties, towns and districts that held the tax base, and not of the ruler. The ruler, on the other hand, was right that the Jews had become part of the tax base only because they were allowed to live in the country, and permission for this was granted by the ruler, according to the general practice of the age. According to that general practice, the ruler could demand a separate tax from them, a toleration tax, in return for permission to live in the country and for tolerating the Jews there.
      How the question was settled ultimately depended on the balance of power between the ruler and the estates. The ruler could conscribe the Jews and tax them for being tolerated in the country only if the counties, towns and districts responsible for carrying out the conscription and taxation could be persuaded or compelled to do so — or else by carrying out the conscription and taxation without them.
      The history of the 1743 national Jewish conscription also sheds light on its value as a source. This conscription was completed for far fewer counties and towns than either the 1725 or the 1735 conscription. It is also much poorer than those, containing far less data on the economic and social situation of the Jews. The conscription was made in order to levy the 6-forint [OCR: '(j forintos'] per-family tax. The enumerators therefore often contented themselves with recording the name and place of residence of the head of the family. At other times, however, they prepared a more detailed conscription containing more data. Despite its lack of uniformity and the varying detail of its data, the 1743 conscription is a usable source for the history of Hungarian Jewry. It is regrettable that it was completed, and has survived, for only a relatively small area.

                                           *
      Presumably the relative failure of the 1743 attempt at taxation also played a part in the ruler's decision to try once more to subject Hungarian Jewry to an extraordinary tax. The War of the Austrian Succession was still going on and the treasury was exhausted; these circumstances served as the justification for the new levy.
      Maria Theresa ordered the new extraordinary Jewish tax in a rescript addressed to the Lieutenancy Council on 23 May 1746 [OCR: '174G']. The rescript ordered that every Jewish person who wished to be tolerated in the country by the ruler must pay 2 forints to the treasury. This extraordinary tax did not exempt the Jews from other taxes. Every head of family was to pay the tax for the persons belonging to the family, including assistants and male and female servants. The deadline for payment was 3 or 4 weeks after the promulgation of the royal decree. Anyone who failed to pay would pay five times the amount as a penalty. Every customary means, including military execution, could be used to collect the tax. Half of the fivefold penalty was to go to the informer, as well as to the officials taking part in the execution. Every Jew who had not paid this tax was to be regarded as lacking royal protection, as one proscribed. The royal officials could seize such proscribed — that is, rightless — persons together with the goods and other belongings in their possession, and were indeed obliged to arrest them.

68

PDF viewer page 71

Source scan
      For the purpose of levying the tax, and so that the excessively growing number of Jews could be known precisely, every Jewish person had to be conscribed.
      Every county and town was obliged to entrust one of its officials with taxing the Jews, and this official was to receive one percent of the tax collected for the work. This tax collector was to pay the collected tax into the nearest war cash office, together with a list of the persons who had paid.
      The conscriptions were to be sent to the Lieutenancy Council, which would submit them to the ruler.
      The Lieutenancy Council was obliged to promulgate the royal decree and was responsible for its execution regardless of objections.69
      Learning from the experience of the previous conscription and taxation, the ruler at the same time addressed a decree to the lord lieutenants (főispánok) and their administrators. The ruler communicated to them the rescript addressed to the Lieutenancy Council and ordered them to have the royal decree promulgated in their presence. They were further ordered to do everything in their power to carry out the decree, to proceed with full rigour, and to report on the result.70
      The Lieutenancy Council sent out the circular ordering the taxation and conscription on 1 June 1746 [OCR: 'jún. ] -én'], in accordance with the royal rescript.71
      These measures introduced the fourth national conscription of Hungarian Jewry in the first half of the 18th century.
      The purpose of this conscription was the same as that of the preceding one ordered in 1743: this too was a tax conscription. The basis of the tax, however, was now the person rather than the family. This meant that the conscription on which the tax was based had to be more detailed and cover every person.
      The levying and collection of the new tax differed somewhat from those of the previous one. Under the previous tax, the Jews had been left to decide how to divide among themselves the tax falling on them. Now every head of family had to pay according to the number of persons belonging to the family. This method of taxation weighed heavily on the poorer families, which in many cases could not pay the amount falling on them at all.
      In the new conscription and taxation the ruler threatened much greater severity than had been applied in the previous one. Success again depended on how far the ruler could enforce the royal will, and on how the balance of power between ruler and estates developed.
      The new conscription and taxation did not begin under more favourable auspices than the previous one. Most of the counties again took a negative position. On 28 July 1746 [OCR: '] 74G'] the Lieutenancy Council submitted copies of the letters of Pozsony and Komárom counties to the ruler, to show what the situation was.72
      The ruler was unwilling to accept the counties' arguments against the extraordinary toleration tax, which were essentially the same as those that


     69 Original fair copy of the royal decree: O. L. Helyt. lvt. Jud. Lad. B. Fase. 7. No 2.1746. No 1. — Original draft: O. L. M. ka.ne. lvt. Cane. exp. 1746. Mai. No 68.
     70 Original draft of the royal decree: O. L. M. kanc, lvt. Cane. exp. 1746. Mai. No 67.
     71 Original draft of the Lieutenancy Council circular: O. L. Helyt. lvr. Jud. Idealia. No 1. 1746. 1 June.

PDF viewer page 72

Source scan
had been invoked in previous years as well. The ruler instructed the Lieutenancy Council to enforce the decree of 23 May and to overcome the difficulties raised by the counties.73
      In accordance with this royal decree dated 5 August 1746, the Lieutenancy Council rejected the protests of Pozsony, Komárom and several other counties against the taxation of the Jews.74
      On 19 August 1746 the Lieutenancy Council submitted to the ruler the first summary tabulation of the county, town and district replies received up to that point.75 The 3–4-week deadline originally set had long passed, yet few replies had arrived, and a considerable part of these were negative.
      Besides the counties' protests on grounds of principle, a new difficulty arose: what should be done with the poor Jews who could not pay the 2 forints per person? Referring to a letter from Zemplén county, the Lieutenancy Council set out this difficulty before the ruler in its submission of 6 September 1746.76
      The ruler did not reply to this submission. Instead, in a rescript dated 12 September 1746, the ruler expressed dissatisfaction with what had been done so far in the matter of the conscription and taxation. The ruler set a new one-month deadline for the conscription and the payment of the tax, and ordered military execution against all who failed to pay within this deadline.
      Military execution had already been mentioned in the royal decree of 23 May, and some counties had in fact used this means.77 A new element, however, was the ruler's decision to involve the country or provincial commissariat organization in carrying out the taxation and conscription. The rescript ordered that the Jews must prove before the provincial commissary of the relevant district that they had paid the tax. Otherwise the provincial commissary would carry out military execution against them.78
      Involving the provincial commissariat organization in carrying out the conscription and taxation proved a decisive step. This organization depended far more on the Lieutenancy Council and the ruler than the counties, towns and districts did. It was therefore much better suited to enforcing the royal will, relying on the strength of the troops stationed in the country.
      The Lieutenancy Council informed the provincial commissaries of the ruler's decision on 16 September 1746 [OCR: '174G. szept. Hi-án'; day uncertain, cf. note 79].79 At the same time it informed the counties, towns and districts of the same.80
      One consequence of the provincial commissariat's involvement in carrying out the taxation and conscription was that the counties, towns and districts

70

PDF viewer page 73

Source scan
the Jewish conscriptions had to be sent not only to the Lieutenancy Council [helytartótanács] but also to the provincial commissioners. It happened that the conscriptions were sent only to the competent provincial commissioner.
      It would be a mistake to think that from then on taxation and conscription took place without any obstacle or complication. Such cases continued to occur. It happened that the Jews were conscribed not by the competent county, town or district, but by a chamber [kamarai] or military authority.81 It was a frequent occurrence that the tax was not paid into the competent war treasury [hadipénztár].82 Sopron county wanted to redeem the tax of the Jews living on its territory in a single sum of 300 gold ducats, to which the ruler did not consent.83 There were difficulties concerning the payment of the one percent due to the tax administrators.84 I consider that a description of cases similar to these may be omitted within the limits of my study.
      Such difficulties, however, essentially only delayed, but did not prevent, the Jews from being continuously conscribed and taxed everywhere they lived. The director of the provincial commissariat, Count Balassa Pál, informed the Lieutenancy Council, of which he was himself one of the councillors, of the progress of conscription and taxation. The Lieutenancy Council in turn forwarded these reports to the ruler, on the basis of which the Hungarian Court Chancellery from time to time also sent statements to the Court Chamber in Vienna.85
      From the report of the Lieutenancy Council dated 30 June 1747 [OCR; footnote 85 gives 20 June 1747 for the submission], submitting the letter and statement written by Count Balássa Pál on 19 June 1747, the ruler was, however, able to establish that some counties had not yet executed the decrees, while others had remained in arrears. He therefore ordered that the Lieutenancy Council should now no longer address summonses to the counties, but proceed with the utmost rigour. The provincial commissioners were to collect the outstanding Jewish tax with military assistance.86

      The Lieutenancy Council communicated the contents of the royal rescript to Count Balassa, who prepared a precise plan for the military enforcement and attached it to his report dated 11 July 1747.87
      We have no further information on the manner in which the enforcements were carried out. In the ruler's rescript to the Lieutenancy Council of 1 September 1747 we can read that even then there were still non-payers and persons in arrears.88
      Complete collection of the tax, without any arrears, was not achieved even in the end. What the provincial commissariat was able to accomplish, with the cooperation of the military, was that the Jews were conscribed everywhere they lived, the tax was assessed on them, and the greater part of it was actually collected.
      Excellent information on the final result of the taxation [OCR "adóstatás"] and the conscription is provided by the statement which was prepared on 25 July 1748 in the office of the provincial commissariat, which Count Balassa Pál, director of the provincial security [OCR "biztonsági"; elsewhere "biztossági" = commissariat] submitted to the Lieutenancy Council on 27 July 1748, and which the latter sent on to the ruler on the very same day.89
      It emerges from this statement that in the second half of the 1740s, of the 43 counties90 of Hungary in the narrower sense - without the Partium, from which by then Zaránd county too no longer fell under the jurisdiction of the Lieutenancy Council - 36,91 and of its 37 towns 9,92 had Jewish inhabitants, while in neither of its 2 districts93 did Jews live.
      The number of Jewish persons living in the 36 counties and 9 towns was 14847. On them a tax of 2 forints per head was assessed, in total [OCR "2D ü94"; probably 29694] forints. Of this, 25900 forints 45 kreuzers, i.e. more than 87% [OCR "87';;,"], was actually paid in cash. The treasury could be satisfied with this result, especially if we compare it with the failure of the 1743 taxation.
      The statement prepared at the provincial commissariat shows the distribution of the number of Jews by provincial commissarial districts, and within these by counties and towns. Below I publish this statement in extract, omitting from it the figures relating to the payment of the tax.

      Comparing these figures with the data of the earlier 3 national conscriptions, and drawing the conclusions arising from them, is no longer the task of this study.
      If we compare the 1746-47 Jewish conscriptions that reached the Lieutenancy Council and have survived among its records with the provincial commissariat statement described above, we must note with regret that the conscription is not available to us for every county and town where, according to the evidence of the statement, Jews lived and where they were in all certainty also conscribed. We find the explanation for this in the fact that, ever since the provincial commissariat became involved in carrying out the taxation and the conscription, the conscriptions were sent primarily there, and not to the Lieutenancy Council. The Lieutenancy Council presumably did not receive the conscriptions from several places. It is probable that it did not even press to receive them, since it was regularly informed of the course of the taxation and the conscription from the reports of the director of the provincial commissariat. The statements sent to the ruler were prepared by the directorate of the provincial commissariat.
     Among the records of the Lieutenancy Council, of the 36 counties where Jews lived in 1746-47 [OCR "174G-47"], the Jewish conscriptions of 25 are present. The conscriptions of 11 [OCR "II"] counties are missing: those of Abaújvár, Bács-Bodrog, Bihar, Esztergom, Komárom, Liptó, Moson, Sáros, Sopron, Ugocsa [OCR "Ugoesa"] and Veszprém counties. - With regard to the towns the situation is considerably more favourable; only that of Buda is missing, from where the Jews were expelled in [OCR "174ü"; possibly 1746].
     We have no knowledge that the conscriptions of the 1740s were copied either for the Hungarian Chancellery (as in the 1720s) or for the Hungarian Chamber (as in the 1730s), and that they would be extant among the records of these government authorities.

76

PDF viewer page 79

Source scan
     To our knowledge, not a single conscription from the 1740s has been published so far.
     As regards the historical source value of the 1746 conscription, it is considerably greater than that of the 1743 one [OCR "1743-as§"], but does not reach that of the 1735 one. Its chief weakness is that it was not prepared uniformly. In general the Jewish persons were listed by name, not only the heads of families but also the family members; there was, however, also a county which gave only a summary by locality. In one or another of the conscriptions we also find the ages of the persons and the occupation of the head of the family, as well as references to their financial situation.
     Despite its lack of uniformity, the 1746 conscription is a source of the first order for the history of Hungarian Jewry.

                                       *
     My aim in this outline of the history of the four national Jewish conscriptions carried out in the first half of the 18th century was to shed light on their origin, and thereby to facilitate the work of those who process the conscriptions. Establishing the abundant knowledge that can be obtained from the conscriptions [OCR "összcfrrisokból"] I did not, as I have already mentioned, regard as my task.
     In 1749 the toleration tax [türelmi taxa], which in 1743 and 1746 had still been levied on the Jews of Hungary only in the form of an extraordinary tax, became regular. In connection with the levying of this toleration tax, the Jews were conscribed relatively frequently in the second half of the 18th century and the first half of the 19th century. Clarifying the history of these conscriptions, as well as their publication and the evaluation of their results, is among the primary tasks still awaiting solution in the history of Hungarian Jewry.

                                                           Ember Győző

4. 15 August 1725. Komárom. Census of the Jews of Komárom County.

CENSUS OF THE HEBREWS LIVING IN THE COUNTY OF KOMÁROM
CONDUCTED IN THE YEAR 1725

In the year 1725, on the 7th day of the month of August, by virtue of the gracious Mandate of the Exalted Royal Lieutenancy Council, a census of the Jews was made in the District of Győr [Processu Jauriensi] by us, the undersigned Judges, in the following manner and order.

IN THE MARKET TOWN OF TATA

Salamon Taies [OCR; elsewhere Taics] of Nikolsburg, lessee of the Lord's iron mill [Molae Fertariae, sic], pays the Domain 200 fl. annually. Simon, a Jew, scribe of the said Salamon Taies of Nikolsburg.
Ábraham Salamon, about 34 years old. Trader, born in the Estate of Köpsény in Moson County, has resided here for three years. Pays the Domain 40 fl. Has two sons, two daughters. Has a servant named Abraham, 17 years old.
Simon Farkas. About 5] [OCR; probably 51] years old. Trader, born in the Market Town of Stompha in Pozsony County, has resided here for ]3 [OCR; probably 13] years. Has one son, two daughters. Pays the Domain 40 fl. Also leases a distillery from the Domain for 200 fl. Has two servants, the first named David, about 23 years old, the second Joannes, 15 years old, both from Moravia.
Isaac Markus. About 18 years old. A petty trader, wanders here and there through the villages, married, from Moravia, has resided here for four years.
Jacobus Heisel. About 36 years old. Married, a tailor from Bohemia, has resided here for ] 8 [OCR; probably 18] weeks. Has one son, one daughter.
Isaac Salamon. About 3G [OCR; probably 36] years old. Married, from Moson County, in the Market Town of Rayka. A petty trader, wanders here and there to the villages. Pays the Domain 8 fl. Has 3 sons, 1 daughter, has resided here for G [OCR; probably 6] years.
Ábraham Saehter, about 70 years old, married. A Jewish butcher, from the Market Town of Rahovies (Rohone) in Vas County, has resided here for 6 years.
Jakobus Joachim, about 25 years old, married, a tailor from Bohemia, has resided here for half a year.
Joachim Fridek, from Moravia, married, scribe of Salamon Taics in the iron mill, about 35 years old. Pays the Domain a census of 15 fl.
Salamon Farkas: manual labourer from Bohemia, about 36 years old. Has resided here for 1 year. Pays the Domain a census of 3 fl.
Ábraham Marcus. Manual labourer, married, about 35 years old, from Moravia, has resided here for 1 year. Has one son.
Samuel Moyses, manual labourer, married, about 30 years old, from the Market Town of Köpcsény in Moson County, has resided here for 1 year. Has one son.
Marcus Leb, married, about 2G [OCR; probably 26] years old, from Moravia, has resided here for 2 years, a petty trader, wanders here and there through the villages, has one daughter.
Moyses Leopold, from Moravia, married, about 26 years old, from Moravia, has resided here for 4 years. A petty trader wandering here and there through the villages, has 4 sons, one daughter.
Samuel David, from Gallia, married, about 33 years old, has resided here for 1 year. Overseer of Salamon Taics at the iron mill. Has one son, 4 daughters.

 10. 1725 October 20, Ják. Census of the Jews of Vasvár County. [heading partly garbled in OCR]

     We the undersigned acknowledge by these presents: that whereas the exalted Royal Lieutenancy Council [Consilium Regium Locumtenentiale], by means of its gracious mandate, deigned to intimate to this illustrious County that each and every Jew living in this County be registered and their condition be investigated in detail, and that genuine information thereon be given to the exalted Royal Council; by virtue of which gracious mandate the illustrious County deigned to commit this investigation of the Jews to us. We therefore, wishing most humbly to comply with the gracious dispatch and commission of the said illustrious County, as the requirement of our office demands, carried out the census of the Jews as follows, namely:
     First. In the market town of Rohoncz the Jews having dwellings and possessing sessions [sessiones], although they do not cultivate the arable lands and meadows belonging to them, nevertheless lease them out to Christians; yet they contribute and pay the portional quota [Portionale Quantum] into their common fund, like the other townsmen; they are the following.

      Fourthly. Of the aforenamed Jews, some carry on their trade in the Counties of Szala [Szaladiensi], Veszprim [Veszprimiensi], Simegh [Simeghiensi], Tolna [Tolnensi], and wherever it seems more convenient to them, and return to their dwellings only at the time of the festivals. And both these and the others residing at home trade in goods of every kind, and carry hides, wool and honey out to the neighbouring foreign provinces and sell them there. The lodgers [Inquilini], however, trade in beans, piffa [meaning unclear; OCR reading uncertain], cabbages, indeed even lard or Arimae [OCR reading uncertain] (which ought to be forbidden to them), and in sheep and goats.


     The Jews in the market town of Szalonak, living not in their own but in leased houses, and paying nothing into the Christians' common fund but to the lordship [Dominium], are the following:

    In the market town of Németh Ujvár, likewise not having their own dwellings, and paying only to the lordship [Dominium]:
           Salamon Moises                   Savedi                  Majerl
           Majerl                           Moises                  Baruch
                                            Sochter

      And these Jews living in the aforesaid two market towns, Szalonak and Németh Ujvár, trade on a smaller scale [minoris sortis], like those of Rohoncz.
       Lastly, the wives, unmarried sons and daughters, offspring, and widows of all the above-specified Jews can be called nothing other than a copious multitude.
      Concerning which census and investigation of the Jews, carried out in the foregoing manner, we most humbly present to the illustrious County these our letters of report, fortified with our signatures and seals. Given at Jáák on the 20th day of the month of October, in the year 1725.
      Ladislaus Rosty, processual judge of the nobles [Processualis Judlium, sic OCR] of the illustrious County of Castriferreum [Vasvár] m. p.
                                                                             L. S.
          Staphanus Horváth, juror [Jurassor] of the illustrious County of Castriferreum m. p.

     It is further added that among the aforementioned Jews there are craftsmen — furriers, tailors, Nodifices [button-/lace-makers?], glaziers, and indeed very many butchers — who are to be found in the villages belonging to the lordship (and in the market town of Rohoncz itself, according to the agreement entered into by the Jews with the Most Excellent Lady Countess, as landlady, paying one florin for each ox); and
     furthermore Jewish toll-collectors in the market towns of Rohoncz and Csákány, and also Murav Szombath, exercise the actual duty of the toll-collecting office contrary to the express publication and prohibition of the illustrious County and the laws of the Kingdom.
                                     The same judge of the nobles as above m. p.

           1725 November 1, Rakicsán. Supplement to the census of the Jews of Vasvár County.
      We the undersigned acknowledge by these presents that, by virtue of the gracious commission of the Lord Vice-Counts sent to us upon the intimation of the exalted Royal Council, we duly carried out the census of the Jews existing in the Process of Totságh and the districts belonging to it, and likewise the inquiry into the Jewish toll-collectors there, and have found as follows:
      1° There are Jews in the market town of Muraj Szombat, situated in the County of Castriferreum, [OCR: "habito, unum intelligentes" — sense unclear] three in number: the first Farkas, the second Sarnson, the third Abraham.
      2° In the market town of Sz. Gothard the Jew Salamon Morx, together with another Jew; they have their shops [Fornices] in the aforesaid places and sell their wares of various kinds, and moreover carry on trade in neighbouring and more distant places; apart from the aforementioned shops they possess nothing; they pay the agreed rent [Arenda] to the lordships, but contribute nothing into the common fund [Gremium] of the community. Those of Muraj-Szombath collect the toll by virtue of a contract entered into with them by the Lords of the lordships of Murai Szombath and Rakicsin, and live as toll-collectors in this manner: that they alone do not always collect the toll, and in their absence they do not collect it, but they have a Christi-

100

PDF viewer page 103

Source scan
an hired, who collects the said toll according to their wish and hands over what is collected to the Jews themselves. Given at Rakicsan on the 1st day of the month of November, in the year 1725.
Paulus Mlinarics, chief processual judge of the nobles [Processualis Supremus Judlium] of the illustrious County of Castriferreum m. p.
                                                                                                         L. S.
                                      Ladislaus Lippirt, juror [Jurassor] of the same illustrious County m. p.

1. 14 December 1725. Bazin. The town of Bazin reports that no Jew may spend even a single night in its territory, except in the Castle and in the curiae of the nobles, where they actually do live. [OCR: "Bazia cáros" read as Bazin város; "kicéce" read as kivéve]

"... it was ordained that, on account of certain crimes and infanticides perpetrated by them, no Jew should be permitted to stay even for a single night in the territory of this town (excepting, however, the Castle grounds and certain suburban noble curiae, where Jews actually resided)." [OCR: "eotum" read as eorum; "noctern" as noctem]

     O. L. Htt. Litt.


2. 20 July 1725. Buda. Census of the Jewry of the town of Buda.

     Published in Magyar Zsidó Oklevéltár vol. III [OCR: "Ill."], no. 273.

     O. L. Acta part. No 43.


3. 21 July 1725. Ginz [OCR: "Etiezeq"; reading uncertain, cf. Ginz in the text]. One Jew lives in the town of Kőszeg.

". .. As for Jews, apart from a single one, named Israel Schlezinger, otherwise a privileged Imperial-Royal Hebrew, who bought a burgher house here by Imperial-Royal privilege and is otherwise propertied, namely holding vineyards, and who contributes proportionately to the town from all such properties and from his trade, and who has also for many years been the toll collector, there are none; nor shall we ever strive to admit more, as being otherwise ordinarily harmful to the poor community.
     Ginz, 21 July 1725.
The judge, senate and community of the free and royal town of Keöszegh or Ginz."

     O. L. Htt. Fasc. Mensium. Litt.


4. 20 July 1725. Modor. List of the Jewry of the town of Modor.

                  CATALOGUE OF THE JEWS LIVING IN MODRA

      First. In this place there is one Jew, named Esaias Hirschel, with his wife and children; they have their own house with appurtenances, that is, a garden vineyard and a small meadow; and no others dare to settle here. [OCR: "Vinea Hortensi" — punctuation unclear; possibly "vineyard, garden"]
      Second. He has a household consisting of 3 Jewish persons and one Jewish cook. He keeps horses, but keeps Christian servants.
      Third. He carries on mercantile trade, and of other kinds as well; however, he does not keep a public shop, but only a domestic one.
      Fourth. For the house and the aforesaid means and trade, towards the public burdens, since he also does not provide quarters, he pays the town annually, by contract, an agreed sum of fl. 150. Apart from him no Jew dares (as stated above) to settle here, and none would be admitted either.
Modra, 20 July 1725.

     O. L. Htt. Litterae. Act. part.

1. 1725 [24 Sept.]. The Kővár district reports to the Lieutenancy Council [OCR: "Hu", read as Htt.] that there are no Jews in the district.

"... concerning the status of the Jews, about which we can give no information whatsoever ... we have absolutely no Jew living in our midst ..."

     O. L. Htt. Litt.


2. 1 Aug. 1725. Somlyó. Kraszna county reports that

     "Since, however, no Jew, and absolutely nowhere, resides within the bounds of this County ..."

     O. L. Htt. Litt.


3. 15 October 1726. The town of Besztercebánya reports that, under the mining law, Jews are forbidden not only to live but even to appear within 7 miles of the district of the mining towns.

      ... namely, how His Most Sacred Caesarean-Royal Majesty Leopold the Great, of glorious memory, already in the year 169H [OCR; digit unclear], on the 2H [OCR; digit unclear] day of the month of January, and in the year 1695 [OCR: "lG95"], on the 29th day of the month of July, long since past, by means of two most gracious mandates issued under the aforesaid dates, deigned graciously to commit and command to this illustrious County of Zólyom, and consequently also, in its manner, to this free and royal and at the same time mining town of Neosolium, upon the prior most humble petition of the 7 mining towns, that the same illustrious County of Zólyom should by no means suffer or permit, contrary to ancient usage and custom, Jews in the Kingdom of Hungary to stay in any way, much less to settle, within the district of the free and royal mining towns and from there for 7 miles around, in order, namely, to forestall the frauds and thefts that could be craftily practised in respect of the gold and silver mines and the smelting offices ...
      Neosolium, 15 October 1726 [OCR: "lí2G"].
N. the judge and senate of the free and royal and at the same time mining town of Neosolium.

     O. L. Htt. Fasc. Mensium Litt.

                                                                                   109

PDF viewer page 112

Source scan
4. 21 July 1725. The town of Eperjes reports that it has neither a Jewish lessee nor a permanently resident Jew.

     "Furthermore, the Jews here among us hold no leases of revenues, much less a fixed residence; indeed, within human memory they would not even be admitted into the town, as being infidels, except perhaps clandestinely and incognito, or with passes from their lords, from whom they have leased estates ..." [Latin phrasing "nullos habent exarendatores proventus" is irregular; rendering approximate]

      O. L. Htt. Litt.


5. 18 July 1725. The town of Esztergom reports that, on the basis of the town's privileges, it has not tolerated Jews on its territory; should any wish to settle among them from a foreign country hereafter, they will report it to the Lieutenancy Council.

       "... with regard, namely, to the Jews ... that although, by virtue of the privileges granted by His Most Sacred Majesty ... Joseph I ..., their admission was left to our free discretion, nevertheless, in conformity with those same letters of privilege, with the addition of a statute of this town, none of them who would fix continuous habitation here is tolerated in the territory of this town (except at public fairs, to which they have customarily appeared from time to time from other places), and none is admitted in any way; and if in future it should happen that anyone ... comes here who perhaps wishes to be received from other kingdoms into the bosom of this town, the same will in that case be duly reported to this Exalted Council ..."

      O. L. Htt. Litt.


6. 30 July 1725. The town of Késmárk reports that it has no Jewish inhabitants; only at the time of fairs do Jewish merchants come there from Moravia and Poland.

     "... that no Jews are found or accepted here, except those coming here at fair time from the neighbouring Kingdom of Poland and from Moravia, who, when the fairs have ended, customarily return again with their goods not sold here, and are by no means tolerated here beyond the fairs ..."

      O. L. Htt. Litt.


7. 19 August 1725. Kismarton. The town does not admit Jews to its territory; on the Esterházy estate, however, they were admitted in large numbers and built houses there, which the town was unable to prevent.

      "... Of the Jews, however, none has been received into the town's territory, although many have petitioned repeatedly; but that on the land of the Esterházy domain they admit them in greater number, almost to the complete ruin of the citizens here, and indeed even have new houses of several storeys built there, it is not within our power to remedy or prevent."

      O. L. Htt. Litt. 1725.


110

PDF viewer page 113

Source scan
8. 22 July 1725. (Kis)szeben. The town of Szeben reports that it has no Jewish inhabitant.

“Finally, as to the third [point], concerning the most gracious Commission issued under date of the 9th of the present [month] with regard to Jews perhaps residing in this city, we humbly inform [you] that among us no Jew whatsoever is found up to the present.”
     O. L. Htt. Litt.


9. 25 July 1725. The town of Nagybánya reports that it has had no Jewish inhabitant within living memory, and has none now either.

“Since, however, no Jew is found in this our city, nor has any of them been an inhabitant of this place within the memory of men, all the more will [this] remain so hereafter.” [OCR/source literally: “eo magis inposterum existet”; the sentence appears elliptical]
     O. L. Htt. Litt.


10. 23 July 1725. The town of Nagyszombat reports that no Jew has lived in its territory since ancient times, but for a short time they have been permitted passage through the town.

“... we would not have omitted to comply most humbly, were it not that the Jews have otherwise, from the most ancient times, and indeed by the Emperor Ferdinand of glorious memory, been banished in perpetuity on account of the crimes committed by them in this city. And although until the most recent years of this century they did not dare even to set foot on the soil of this territory [OCR: “Pcculy”], nevertheless transit (but by no means entry [OCR: “Inbroitus”, probably “introitus”]) through the territories of the city has now been allowed to them with the foreknowledge of the Royal Fiscus” ...
     O. L. Htt. Litt.


11. 27 August 1725. Report of the town of Sopron to the Lieutenancy Council concerning the conscription of the Jews.
     That Jews who, according to the gracious Resolution of His Most Serene Majesty, are to be expelled, or perhaps have already been expelled, from one or another of His remaining hereditary provinces should not take refuge here, we shall watch all the more carefully, since it is otherwise provided that no Jew may reside in this Free and Royal City.
     O. L. Hit. [OCR; probably “Htt.”] Litterae 27 August 1725.


12. 23 July 1725. The town of Szeged, on the basis of its privileges, does not tolerate Hebrews within its territory.
     Since indeed both our privileges and our particular statutes expressly forbid each and every Hebrew from subsistence and habitation in this city, consequently, just as until now we have not suffered [OCR: “nisi passe”] any Hebrew within [our] bosom, so in future too we shall admit none of them and shall not tolerate [them].
     O. L. Htt. Litt. 1725.

1. 7 October 1727. Szepsi [OCR: “Szepei”]. Conscription of the Jews of Abaújvár County.

LIST OF THE JEWS RESIDING IN THE ILLUSTRIOUS COUNTY OF ABAÚJVÁR

     In the estate of Fony [OCR: “Fon y”]: Fabianus Markovics, holding the lease of beer [OCR: “CCl'eV1SHle”, i.e. cerevisiae] and brandy of the domains of Sáros Patak and Regúcz belonging to the Most Eminent Prince of the Holy Roman Empire von Trautczen [OCR spelling], for which lease he pays annually (as far as could be ascertained) 1700 Hungarian florins. He has one son, 3 cows, 12 horses, 4 servants. To the treasury of the Illustrious County he pays 35 Rhenish florins.
     In the estate of Horváthi [OCR: “Hor v thi. á”]: the leaseholder of the same Most Eminent Prince, the Jew Josephus Herxkovics [OCR spelling], has 4 oxen, 1 horse, 1 cow. He pays as lease to His Eminence 60 [OCR: “GO”] Hungarian florins, and to the treasury of the Illustrious County 10 florins.
     In Felső Ozécz [OCR: “Fel S Ő O z é c z c”]: the Jew Moyses Herskovics, leaseholder of the brandy house of Lord Ladislaus Truranszky [OCR spelling]. He has 2 sons, 1 servant, 2 horses, 1 cow; for the lease he pays him [OCR: “Hi.”; numeral unreadable] Hungarian florins, and to the treasury of the Illustrious County 4 Rhenish florins.
     In Ozekch1~Za [OCR garbled place name]: the Jewish leaseholder of the Noble Lord Samuel Pathaj, Josephus Zelman, who has 1 servant, 3 horses, 2 cows; he pays a lease of 200 Hungarian florins, and to the treasury of the Illustrious County 10 Rhenish florins.
     In the estate of Bakta: the Jew David, temporarily dwelling in the inn of the Noble Lord Josephus Szemere, sells drink without a lease; who, being a vagrant [OCR: “quam vagus”], has not been assessed for the treasury of the Illustrious County, having nothing except one horse.
     In the estate of Fancsal [OCR: “F a n c sal.”] of Lord Adamus Vozar: the Jew Marcus, residing on the land of the same Lord, to whom he pays 10 Hungarian florins. He has two sons, the elder Joannes, aged [OCR: “R.”; numeral unclear], the younger Michael [OCR: “Miehaclcm”], aged [OCR: “a.”; numeral unclear]. Since he is unsettled and has no means apart from one horse, he has not been assessed for the treasury of the Illustrious County.
     In the estate of Zaricilly [OCR: “Z a ri ci.Il y.”]: the Jew Elias, leaseholder of brandy, for which lease he pays to Lord Michael [OCR: “Iichaeli”] Szent Mártonyi 31 Rhenish florins 40 kreuzers; having no means, he pays to the treasury of the Illustrious County 1 Rhenish florin 30 kreuzers.
     And this is the state of the Jews residing in the Illustrious County of Abaújvár, ascertained and recorded on the report of the Lords District Magistrates. Under the General Assembly of the same Illustrious County, continued on the 7th day of the month of October [OCR: “Sbris”, probably “8bris”] in the year 1727 in the town of Szepsi.
     Issued by Joannes Saygho, sworn notary of the aforesaid Illustrious County, in his own hand.

     O. L. C 29. No 1. 1727-1. and Act. part. 1727.

3. 19 September 1727. Pécs. Report of Baranya County on the county's sole Jewish inhabitant.
     ... In view of which we also voluntarily refer to our letter issued on the 9th day of the month of July of the present year from our general assembly held here in the Episcopal City of Pécs [Quinque]Ecclesiensis [OCR: “Episcopali Civitate et Ecclesiensi”], that within this our County there is [a Jew] nowhere except in the town of Siklós, named Isák, an inhabitant of Rohoncz, who has no son or kinsman in this County but only a rented shop, and stops there only from time to time. [He has] no cultivation, nor does he contribute towards the public or domestic necessities, but [only] to the Landlord. ...
     O. L. C. 28. [sic in OCR; elsewhere C 29] No 1. 1727-2.

5. 1 October 1727. Deda. Census of the Jewry of Bereg County.

Having conscribed the Jews in my own district, they were found as follows:
In Bilke [OCR “B il kén”]. One named Gléba; he has neither younger brother nor elder brother, nor does he possess a plot. Paid to the landlord fl. 100.
Given to the Jew [sic; meaning unclear] fl. 10.
One named Martin has nothing, neither elder brother nor younger brother, nor does he possess a plot.
Paid to the landlord 10.
He gives nothing to the village. Given to the Jew [sic] flo: 5.
Possessio Dobruka.
A Jew named Isak has nothing, possesses no plot, no relative [OCR “Abtafi” = Atyafi].
Pays to the landlord 10 forints.
He gives nothing to the village. Given to the Jew [sic] fl. 6.
Possessio Bene [OCR “13e n e”]. One named Josép, neither younger brother nor elder brother, possesses no plot. Paid tax to the landlord 5.
Given to the Jew [sic], pays fl: 10.
He helps the village annually with five icce [OCR “Ieee”] of pálinka, i.e. fl. 5.
Dated Borsova, on the 29th day of September [7bris] 1727.
Ordinary district magistrate (szolgabíró) of the Noble County of Bereg. Cherney Pall m. p. L. S.

We, the undersigned Ordinary District Magistrate and Sworn Assessor of the Honourable Noble County of Bereg, by commission of the Honourable Noble County came here to the locality named Ugornja to carry out the conscription written below, which follows thus:
1mo Here in Ugornja, a locality in the Honourable Noble County of Bereg, lives a Jew named Lázár.
2do He has two sons, the name of one is Joseph, of the other Oroszlan; one is five years old, the other 3.
3tio Neither a relative nor a servant lives with him.
4to He holds a plot of His Grace Mr László Vay; before the ferry [rév] [there is] an orchard, his garden, plums and apples grow in it; he sells drink [korcsma] and meat; annually he gives the lord arenda flo. 12.
5to He has so far contributed nothing to the Noble County or to the village.
Ordinary District Magistrate of the Honourable Noble County of Bereg, Guthy György m. p. L. S.
Sworn Assessor Gersini Gábor m. p. L. S.

Conscription of the Jews existing in the Illustrious County of Bereg, and specially in the Illustrious Dominions of Munkács and Sz. Miklós, made by virtue of the Most Gracious Mandate of the Exalted Royal Council.

Concerning whether:
1ma Which of the Jews has a stable residence here, and by what name is he called?
2do Whether they have sons, blood relatives, kinsmen, servants — let him declare by what names they are called.

120

PDF viewer page 123

Source scan
3tio And in what place such a one lives, and what land he holds, whether he cultivates it himself or through others?
4to And what of these he pays toward the public, either from his trade or from domestic necessities, or to the landlords themselves, and under what title he pays anything?

Izrael Lazarovics, Jewish arendator of Sz. Miklós. Ad 2dum: he has one brother with him, named Hersko Lazarevics. Two servants, one called Josi, the other Jazko. Ad 3tium: he cultivates no land, apart from the house in which he lives, which the landlord assigned to him. Ad 4um: toward the public he pays nothing other than the toleration money to the Illustrious Chamber, namely Hungarian fl. 30 annually. From trade, however, the Thirtieth [customs duty]; to the landlord, for the year, he pays arenda — understood as including also the subleaseholders in the places noted below — Hungarian fl. 585.

Jacobus Lazarevics, tapster (educillator) of Izrael Lazarovics, has his residence in the village (Possessio) Szolyva. Ad 2dum: nothing. Ad 3tium: he has only a house, which was assigned to him by the landlord. Otherwise nothing.
Ad 4tum: toward the public he pays nothing, since he was included in the arenda of Izrael Lazarevics.

Iczko, tapster, a Jew, “fraternus” of the first [relationship term unclear in OCR], has his residence in the village Szuszko. He holds no land apart from a single house assigned by the landlord. Toward the public he contributes nothing.

Parim Szmulovics, Jew, tapster of Izrael Lazarovics; his residence is in the village Verbjas. He cultivates no land except one [OCR punctuation unclear]. He has a house in which he resides, by grant of the landlord.

Abraham Herskovics, Jew and tapster of Izrael Lazarovics; his residence is in the town of Munkács.

Likewise Jutzko Nachmanovics, Jew, tapster likewise of Izrael Lazarovics; his residence is in the village Ivanyi. These cultivate no land, except that they have houses assigned for habitation by the landlord. Toward the public they contribute nothing.

Orum, Jew, tapster of Izrael Lazarevics, has his residence in the village Bilatzovicza. No land apart from a house assigned by the landlord. Toward the public he contributes nothing.

All these are included in the arenda of Izrael Lazarevics, Jew of Sz. Miklós, belonging to the Dominion of Munkács.

There follow the arendators of various villages.
Volya, Jew, has his residence below the Monastery of St. Nicholas at Munkács. He cultivates land [sic; a negative may be missing in the OCR] apart from the house assigned to him by the landlord, namely the Abbot of the aforesaid Monastery, in which he lives. Toward the public he pays nothing except the toleration money to the Illustrious Chamber, Hungarian fl. 12.
To the aforesaid landlord, by way of arenda, Hungarian fl. 180 per year.

Jacobus, Jew, arendator of the village Kulcsin, where he has nothing apart from the house in which he lives, which was assigned to him by the provisor of the Dominion of Sz. Miklós. Toward the public he contributes nothing. He pays arenda to the Illustrious Chamber, Hungarian fl. 18.

Aron, Jew, resides as an inquilinus [cottager/lodger] in Oroszvegh, in a village of the Illustrious Dominion of Munkács. Toward the public he contributes nothing.

Jacobus Juszkovics, Jew, his residence being in the village Kis Gutth, where he cultivates no land, having only a small house assigned by the landlord. Contributing nothing toward the public; to the landlord, by way of arenda, for the space of one year he pays Hungarian fl. 12.

6a [OCR “(ja”] 10 February 1727. Körösladány [OCR “1\ örösZadány”]. Report of Békés County to the Htt. [Lieutenancy Council] on its Jews.

… Within the whole bosom of this county there have been no Jews, but recently two have been introduced: one, lessee of the brewery of the Most Illustrious Lord Joannes Georgius ab Harukern at Gyula; the other, afterwards, lessee of the distillery of our Most Excellent Lord Supreme Count Joannes Jacobus a Lewenbergh [OCR “Levembergh”] in the village Váry; no others whatever exist …
From our general assembly held on the 10th day of the month of February 1727 in the village Körös Ladány.
The Universitas of the County of Békés.
Htt. Fasc. Mensium Litt.

6b 17 February 1728. Gyula. Supplementary report of Békés County on its Jews.

… in the whole county, apart from a single Jew called by name Salamon, at present at Gyula under the dominion of the Most Illustrious Councillor ab Harukern, coming from Buda, as arendator of the brewery and the distillery house, but without exercising trade. Until the arenda is completed he would stay; once his “tricesimalis” [OCR reading; sense unclear] arenda is finished, for the future year 1729, he would be freely permitted to return to Buda, whence he previously came [OCR “termit”], and further he would not be tolerated in the Harukern dominion either (as was declared by the lord officials).
Given from our general assembly held on the 17th [OCR “li.”] of February of the year 1728 in the castle of Gyula.
The Universitas of the County of Békés.
Htt. Fasc. Mensium. Litt.

7. 6 [OCR “(j.”] February 1727. Püspöki. Census of the Jews of Bihar County.

List of the Jews found in the Illustrious County of Bihar.
In the town Váradolaszi lives a Jew named Majerlével [OCR “Majerl-vel”]; he has his own house; toward the relief of the townspeople he pays fl 10 for the common burden; he has a vaulted shop (fornix) with cloth and other things, and carries on trade in the manner of the Jews.

In the same place lives the Jew Josephus Isak; he keeps lodging with Henricus Himelham, citizen, and carries on petty trade, buying smaller goods from other Jews; into the number of the townspeople he pays annually for the common burden Rhenish florins 3.
Abrahamus Poliezer at present dwells in the woods; for three years he has been arendator of the episcopal distillery house existing in Szőlős; formerly he resided in Várad and contributed into the number of the city Rh. fl. 20, i.e. 20; since he has been arendator he contributes nothing.
Another Josephus, a Jew, lives in the fiscal allodium, lessee of the fiscal distillery; he contributes nothing.
Isak, a Jew, lives in the town of the National Militia, under the protection of the commanding officer existing there, and in his house; he contributes nothing.

Simon Jvel, partner in trade of the above-specified Majorlével, also shares with him in the contribution to the common burden; he has lived here for five years.

8. 12–17–18 August 1727. Census of the Jewry of three districts of Borsod County.

12 Aug. 1727. Hangács. Ádám Szatmári Király reports that 3 Jews live in the Szendrő district [OCR garbled: “szetulrűi árásb 'tn”; the Latin text below mentions only one]:
“in this Szendrő district at present only one was found, living in the village Hangács, whom I have recorded thus: The same Jew, called Benjamin Has, born in Moravia in the town Holiso, under the Dominion of the Most Illustrious Count a Rhottal, is in his forty-second year, and first entered Hungary 10 years ago; since that time, in the Illustrious Counties first of Liptó, then of Zemplén, and finally of Borsod, in houses and breweries of various lords, together with his wife named Anna Herschel, he often practised the craft of brewing beer, but more often of distilling wine (commonly Palyinka), and had no other means of living; utterly destitute of goods, livestock, substance or merchandise, he sustains his own and his family’s life only by the aforesaid; he has 3 sons, one 16, another 11, the third 3, and a little daughter of 6 years; for the house and 2 cauldrons he currently pays us an arenda of 20 Hungarian florins. He maintains his wife’s uncle, called Joachimus Hresofszki, about 55 years of age, originating from the Duchy of Silesia, who, having left his wife and children in Moravia in the town Brusznicz, destitute of all means of living, moved here from Poland about 6 months ago solely for the sake of subsistence; he also has a servant named Jacobus, likewise a Jew, counting his 25th year of age, who lacks altogether family and merchandise, and originates from the Illustrious Kingdom of Poland, from the town Sidlov, and moved into this Illustrious Kingdom only 3 years [OCR “armis” = annis] ago for the purpose of performing service.”

O. L. Htt. Litt.

17 Aug. 1727. Miskolc. Chief district magistrate Ferenc Bük and juror György Kozári report to the Htt. that there are 3 Jews in the Miskolc district.

“In the Miskolc district 3 Jews were found, namely: In the town of Miskolcz. By name Jacobus Salomon, having come here about 18 years ago from Upper Poland and living here in the aforesaid town, has practised and currently practises the craft of distilling spirits; he is about 32 years old. For the house in which he resides and 2 cauldrons he pays annually to the lady widow of the late Mr Paulus Szepessi Hung. fl. [OCR “n.”] 20; he maintains his father, already worn out by old age, named Salamon, about 75 years old; he has a wife named Catharina, 2 sons, Salamon and Mojses Salamon, [one] 4 years old, while the other, Mojses, has not yet completed his first year; destitute of a servant and other goods, he deposits annually for the Treasury of the Illustrious County Rh. fl. 10.
The other Jew there, named Mojses Simon, from Moravia and the town Magyar Brod, came here 3 years ago, about 30 years old. He carries on trade in merchandise; he has a fellow-lodger partner named Filip, likewise born in Moravia, in the town Magyar Brod, who came here 8 years ago and likewise carries on trade in merchandise; he keeps his family in Moravia; he has a servant named Chaim. For the house and vaulted shop of the Most Illustrious and Noble Mr Franciscus Aszalai both pay annually Hung. fl. 50, and to the Treasury of the Illustrious County annually Rh. fl. 25.
In the village Felső Sócza, the Jew Mojses Isak, who came here in the year 1724 from Moravia and the town Magyar Brod, and is here under contract with the Most Illustrious and Noble Mr Andreas Dőri for distilling spirits; besides this he is unable to carry on any trade; he has a wife Catharina, [and is said] to have 2 sons, Samuel and Mojses. Samuel is about 10 years old, Mojses currently in his fourth year. Destitute of a servant and other things, he has hitherto paid nothing to the Treasury of the Illustrious County” …

O. L. Htt. Litt.

18 Aug. 1727. Mezőkövesd. Péter Sebe, chief district magistrate of Borsod County, reports that the following Jews live in his district.

“The other of them received the name Moyses at circumcision, of the lineage Getcz, originating from Moravia, the town Magyar Bratt, a subject of Count Vilhelmus Maximilianus a Kaunicz; he maintains a wife, 3 daughters and his mother-in-law; he is arendator of the distillery in the Dominion of Ónod, and pays 100 thalers; he carries on no other trade or husbandry; for the County he renders fl. 6 R. There was also another, called Moyses, who has now been absent for a long time, who at times also called himself Josephus, originating from the place Trebics; with his wife [and] son Kalme, otherwise called Salamon, he sustained his life in this way: distilling, and carrying linen and smaller wares from village to village; he has so far rendered nothing to anyone, since not long ago, in the town of Keresztur in the Illustrious County of Zemplén, he suffered the loss of all his substance in a conflagration” …

O. L. Htt. Litt.

9. 8 March 1727. Székesfehérvár. List of the Jews found in Fejér County.

Consignation of the Jews to be found in this County of Fejér.
In the village Moór [OCR “11 ó ó r”], two, one of whom is named Volffgangus Hirsll, the other Hirsll Keczll.
In Csurgó, one, named Salamon.
In Lovas Berény likewise two, called Jacobus and Salamon Hirsll.
Issued by Josephus Denkovics, sworn notary of the aforesaid County, in his own hand.

O. L. Htt. Litterae. Act. part.

The Lieutenancy Council’s remark on the submission: “… As being less adequate, and therefore belonging to the third class.” On this see M. Zs. O. III, no. 308, 19 July 1727: the Lieutenancy Council’s report on the outcome of the royal decree ordering the census of the Jews, which groups the reports into four categories: 1) where there are no Jews; 2) from where adequate reports arrived; 3) those who submitted incomplete censuses; 4) finally, those who have not yet sent in a report.

10a 6 [OCR “G.”] March 1727. Eger. Heves–Külső Szolnok County reports that one merchant Jew and one pálinka-distilling Jew live on its territory, but they have no permanent place of residence.
“Jews are found, 2, of whom one trades in various goods, the other distils spirits; but they do not have a fixed residence; rather, they have rented lodgings annually” …

O. L. Htt. Litt.

10b 25 August 1727. Eger. From the report of Heves County and the Külső Szolnok County united with it on the census of its Jewry.

… had dwellings in the town of Gyöngyös, we represent the following, to be written up for the Exalted Council.
First, that in the aforesaid town of Gyöngyös two brothers, full and uterine, carrying on trade in various goods — one of whom is called David Abraham, the other Moyses Ábraham — are dwelling; and these keep their lodging in the vaulted shop of Mr Stephanus Orczy; both married, living in one household and one trade; the first of them has a son named Ábraham, thirteen years old, and a daughter Anna, eight years old; the other, Moyses Ábraham, has a daughter Sara, fifteen years old; together they keep one servant named Marcus and one maidservant Susanna, of the Hebrew nation, paying the lord of the land twenty florins.
These moreover have one blood relative named Jacobus Ábraham, living on the land of Mr Paulus Petrovay, likewise married; he has a male child of two years named Isác, and two daughters, Elisabetha of eight years and Susanna of four. He himself, as porter of his small wares, which he is accustomed to sell from village to village, stays in the aforesaid town in the manner of an inquilinus.
There was found moreover another, named Marcus, who lives in the brewery of the town, married; he has a son Samuel, seven years old, and daughters Rosa, seventeen years old, and Eva, nine years old, Susanna, six years old, and Maria, one and a half years old. By way of arenda he pays the town fifteen florins, and he, like the preceding, is accustomed to sell his goods from village to village.

126

PDF viewer page 129

Source scan
     And two others were found still living as lodgers [inquilinario modo] with the aforementioned: one named Adam Jacob, who has a son Samuel aged four years and a daughter Sara aged two years. He pays three florins for residence [incolatus], a newcomer [neo-advena].
     The other, named Hirschl, having a son Moyses aged three years and a daughter Rachel aged five years, likewise pays three florins for the house; traders similar to the former [priori similes quaestores]. And all these, in the aforesaid town, pay twelve florins for four market stalls.
     And on these aforementioned Jews 150 florins were also imposed on account of domestic needs. Since, therefore, we have to submit the investigation of these aforementioned Jews to the Exalted Council by way of information ...
    O. L. C. 29. No 1. 1727-5.


11. 1727 July [OCR: 'júliu.d9'; probably July 9]. Nyék. Submission of Hont county in response to the Lieutenancy Council's order of the [OCR: 'ID-ih'; possibly '10th'] concerning the census of the Jews.          [margin: 1727 July]

          [...] with promptness we thought it necessary to inform [you] about this: that we have removed the Jews from the bosom of this County, and have allowed [them] safe passage across the bridges of the rivers Gran and Ipoly, situated along the Danube, towards Vaczium, otherwise exceeding a distance of six miles, pending the further disposition and ratification of the Exalted Royal Lieutenancy Council. Furthermore, regarding the apprehension of the Jew who fled from the prisons of the Margraviate of Moravia, in case he should happen to come to these parts, we have made appropriate arrangements ...

     O. L. C 29. No 1. 1727-6 [OCR: '1727-G'].

        In the estate of Konyha
Lazarus Izrael
For seven years he has lived on the territory and in the house of the nobleman Ladislaus Szaplonczai, and pays this nobleman annually Hung. flor. 20, i.e. twenty. He lives from the retail sale of brandy and beer and has no farming.
